Udaypur Evidence Map: Contextual evidence

# Chapter 1. Udaypur: Historical Memory and the Making of a Place

On the border of Bundelkhand, Udaipur was one of the busiest commercial centres of their kingdom, where, in a city encircled on all sides by a fort, a palace, several stepwells, doorways, temples, squares-and-platforms, and a courthouse were built over centuries.

## King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture (Raja Bhoj aur Parmarkaleen Nagar Sthapatya) — English translation — Bhagwatilal Rajpurohit (RAJABHOJ-EN, 4)
According to the Udaipur Prashasti (उदयपुर प्रशस्ति), King Bhoj defeated the Chedi king, Indraratha, Toggala, Bhima, and the kings of Karnata, Lata and Gurjara, as well as the Turushkas (Turks).
C01-RAJABHOJ-EN-001   p. 4   also Ch 6
According to the Udaipur Prashasti, Vairisimha alias Vajratasvami, having slain the enemy host with the edge of his sword (*asidhara*), named the town Dhara in memory of his valour.
C01-RAJABHOJ-EN-002   p. 55   also Ch 4
Nine ruins of ancient temples lie here. The art here resembles the stone-craft of Udayeshvar.
C01-RAJABHOJ-EN-004   p. 46   also Ch 5
Like Dhar, Ujjayini, Udaipur and Gyaraspur, at Mandu too excellent examples of Paramara art are found.

## भोजदेव (Bhojadeva) (BHOJDEV-HI, 4)
देश तीन प्रकार के होते हैं - जांगल, अनुप और साधारण । इन तीनों की विशेषताएं बताते हैं । 2
जल दूर, लूणी या नमकीन प्रायः, छोटी कॉटेदार झाड़ियाँ (झूते), रूखा, गर्म, तेज हवा, काली मिही आदि का क्षेत्र जांगल देश वताते हैं ।
नीचा, प्रचुर पानी, चिकना, बहुत मछलियाँ और मांस, हिम, नदियों की बहुलता, चिकने-ड़्वेच दृक्षों की विपुलता आदि का क्षेत्र अनूप कहलाता है 14
देशज विद्वान् वताते हैं कि साधारण देश वह होता है जो न अधिक शीत, न अधिक उष्ण हो और जिसमें पूर्वांक दोनों देशों के लक्षणों गे सभी सम्पत्र हों 15
*Gloss [my translation]:* Countries are of three kinds: jangala, anupa and sadharana; jangala is a region where water is far, mostly saline, with small thorny bushes, dry, hot, windy, black soil; anupa is low, with abundant water, many fish, rivers, and oily, big trees; sadharana is neither too cold nor too hot, having features of both.
C02-BHOJDEV-HI-002   p. 29   also Ch 5
इस वाँध को हुशंगशाह ने तोड़कर शील खाली करवा दी थी। कहते हैं यह मानविनिर्मित सवसे बड़ी झील थी जो सिंचाई के काम आती थी। उसके मध्य जो द्वीप था वह आज भी दीप नामक वस्ती है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Hoshangshah broke this dam and emptied the lake; it is said to have been the largest man-made lake and served irrigation; the island in its middle is still a settlement called Deep.
C02-BHOJDEV-HI-003   p. 5   also Ch 7
दूब, औषधी, मुंज, कुठन्द, कुश, वल्कलों से चारों ओर द्विरी हो एवं स्वादिष्ट, स्वच्छ,
स्थिर जल से सम्पत्र हो 141
वास्तु, यज, देवालय से युक्त तथा आराम-उद्यान से भरपूर, तालाव, वावड़ी के स्थानों
से जो चारों ओर अलंकृत हो 142
वाहनों के लिए जो सुगम हो, मिथुनी को रतिप्रद हो, श्री-लक्ष्मी उत्पत्र करने वाली हो, ऐसी भूमियाँ नगर के लिए प्रशस्त होती हैं 143
*Gloss [my translation]:* Surrounded by durva grass, herbs, munja, kusha, with sweet clear still water; endowed with vastu, sacrifice and temples, full of gardens, adorned all round with tanks and stepwells; easy for vehicles, delightful to couples, bringing prosperity - such lands are praised for a town.
C02-BHOJDEV-HI-004   p. 30   also Ch 5
घास और समूढ़ के मध्यवर्ती स्थल की भूमियाँ अच्छी होती हैं जहाँ का पानी शीतल
और स्वादिट होता है। जो भूमि दुर्जनों के धावे के योग्य न हो, अनेक को आथ्य देती
हो, हड़बड़ी-हुल्लड़, जास आदि से रहित हो और जो मन को प्रसत्र कर दें - ऐसे गुणों से
सम्पत्र ऐसी भूमियों पर यथास्थान खेट (खेड़ा), ग्राम, पुर आदि वसाने चाहिए 133-35
*Gloss [my translation]:* Land between grass and sea-like expanses, whose water is cold and sweet, is good; land not fit for raids by wicked people, giving shelter to many, free of tumult and fear, pleasing to the mind - on such lands villages, hamlets and towns should be founded.
C02-BHOJDEV-HI-005   p. 30
## The Cultural Heritage of Madhya Bharat — Patil, D. R. (MADHYABHARAT, 2)
A considerable portion of north and north-east follows the course of the river Betwa while the south is watered by the sacred Narmada. The physical face or features of this land is therefore characterised by the three river systems of the Chambal, Betwa and the Narmada and by the mountain systems of the Vindhyas and the Satpuras which have naturally made themselves felt throughout the various courses of her history and culture.
C02-MADHYABHARAT-001   p. 17
Regionally the northern Madhya Bharat is the richest, a fact which is better explained by its geography, especially by the ready availability of the sandstone from its hills.

## The Hindu Temple, Volume I — Stella Kramrisch (KRAM-1, 2)
\( ^{1} \)  They are built of the stones which are quarried in the various parts of India; sandstone prevails in central Indian temples; limestone or marble is frequent in western India; trap in the Deccan; a fine grained, black chloritic schist in the later Čalukya temples of the Kanarese districts in the Deccan; a similar stone, quarried in the Rajmahal hills is also used in Bengal for door frames, etc., of brick temples; granite in south India; laterite and sandstone, etc., in Orissa.
C02-KRAM-1-001   p. 112   also Ch 5
Correspondingly, various tests are applied to the stone before it is quarried by which its suitability for building temples and making images becomes known.
Stone, severed from the rock, taken from its site, is transformed in its nature, alike to the wood, when it becomes the substance of which the temple is built, imbued as are the bricks, with its indwelling Essence.
C02-KRAM-1-002   p. 125
## Samarāṅgaṇasūtradhāra: An Ancient Treatise on Architecture, vol. 1 — Bhojadeva (attrib.) (SAMAR-1, 1)
The earths that are bland bearing substantial substances, pure or pellucid, ones or non-adulterated having reservoirs all around or circumambiatively or circumambulatively \( ^{1} \) , water abounding, surrounded (lit. covered) by trees, dense or compact normed, and having inundations in the East and the North. Dūrvā (grass) corns, the medicinal herbs, Muñja grass (of which girdles are prepared by the ascetics), Kurunda \( ^{2} \)  (may be Kuruvinda - Mustu or motha-nāgaramothā) Kuśa grass and barks - all these stand overgrown all around wherein having waters tasty, limpid and stationary as such equipped with the residential buildings, the sacrificial altars, the temples (amarasthāna), orchards and parterres or gardens, and which ones are decorated all over by the ponds oblong tanks and spotted as such, and which ones are comfort giving for the vehicles, afforders of happiness to the couples, those lands are deemed laudable for the creation of puras or towns having glories enshrined.
C02-SAMAR-1-001   Vol. 1, p. 78 ⚠ old OCR   also Ch 4

## कथा उदयपुर (Jagta Hua Kasba) — Hindi original (JAGTA-HI, 4)
आठवीं सदी में पाल, प्रतिहार और राष्ट्रकूट राजवंशों के बीच चले त्रिपक्षीय संघर्ष के बाद के दौर में उत्तर एवं दक्षिण भारत में बड़े सियासी बदलाव हुए। कुछ नए राजवंश उभरकर सामने आए, जिन्होंने इतिहास में अपनी शानदार मौजूदगी दर्ज कराई। इनमें सबसे खास थे दक्षिण में कल्याणी के चालुक्य, जो वातापी के चालुक्य वंश के उत्तराधिकारी थे।
*Gloss [my translation]:* After the tripartite struggle among the Pala, Pratihara and Rashtrakuta dynasties in the eighth century, there were big political changes in north and south India. Some new dynasties emerged and recorded their splendid presence in history. Most notable among them in the south were the Chalukyas of Kalyani, successors of the Chalukya dynasty of Vatapi.
C03-JAGTA-HI-009   p. 138   also Ch 4
प्रतिहारों की ताकत कमजोर होने पर तीन ताकतें उभरकर आईं। इनमें कलचुरी के चेदि, जैजाकभुक्ति के चंदेल और मालवा के परमार एक साथ क्षितिज पर उगते हैं।
*Gloss [my translation]:* When the Pratiharas' strength weakened, three powers emerged. Among them the Chedis of the Kalachuris, the Chandellas of Jejakabhukti and the Paramaras of Malwa rise on the horizon together.
C03-JAGTA-HI-010   p. 139   also Ch 4
मुरादपुर से लेकर सोनारी तक विष्णु की प्रतिमाओं का पाया जाना इतिहास के सिरों को परमारों से भी पीछे गुप्त शासकों से जोड़ता है जो विष्णु के उपासक थे। इससे हम अंदाजा लगा सकते हैं कि उदयादित्य से पाँच सौ साल पहले भी यहाँ स्थापत्य के
चमत्कार रचने की प्रक्रिया निरंतर थी।
*Gloss [my translation]:* The finding of Vishnu images from Muradpur to Sonari connects the threads of history to the Gupta rulers, who were worshippers of Vishnu, even further back than the Paramaras. From this we can guess that even five hundred years before Udayaditya the process of creating wonders of architecture was continuous here.
C03-JAGTA-HI-011   p. 154–155
उत्तरापथ से दक्षिणापथ को जोड़ने वाला वह पथ मात्र परिवहन का राजमार्ग नहीं बल्कि भक्ति का महामार्ग था जिस पर चेर, चोल, पाण्ड्य, चालुक्य, राष्ट्रकूट आदि विभिन्न राजवंशों ने भारतीय सनातन संस्कृति को अक्षुण्ण रखने के निमित्त न जाने कितनी देव-नगरियाँ, पुरियाँ निर्मित कराईं। जहाँ से भक्ति की धारा जनमानस को चतुर्दिक दीप करती थी।
- यह वही महामार्ग था जिससे होकर ‘द्राविड़ ऊपजी’ भक्तिधारा ने उत्तर भारत में एक से बढ़ कर एक दीपस्तम्भ जगमगा दिए थे। ऐसा ही एक जागृत दीपस्तम्भ था उदयपुर का उदयेश्वर महादेव देवालय।
*Gloss [my translation]:* That path joining Uttarapatha to Dakshinapatha was not merely a highway of transport but a great path of devotion, on which various dynasties such as Chera, Chola, Pandya, Chalukya and Rashtrakuta built countless temple-towns and cities to keep Indian eternal culture unbroken. From where the stream of devotion lit up the people on all sides. This was the same great path through which the Dravidian-born stream of devotion lit one beacon after another in north India. One such living beacon was the Udayeshvara Mahadeva temple of Udaypur.
C03-JAGTA-HI-013   p. 165–166

## Jagta Hua Kasba / कथा उदयपुर — English translation (JAGTA-EN, 2)
The finding of Vishnu idols from Muradpur all the way to Sonari links the threads of history to the Gupta rulers — who were worshippers of Vishnu — even further back than the Paramaras. From this we can surmise that even five hundred years before Udayadity, the process of fashioning architectural marvels here was continuous.
C03-JAGTA-EN-007   ≈ p. 153 (Hindi original)
- Seeing this newly-built, broad, expansive path, made through public cooperation, one feels as though it was just such a broad splendour of the road to the Deccan that, again and again, kindled the greed of the outside invaders.
- That path, connecting the Uttarapath (northern route) to the Dakshinapath (southern route), was not merely a highway of transport but a great highway of devotion (*bhakti*), upon which various dynasties such as the Chera, Chola, Pandya, Chalukya and Rashtrakuta had built who-knows-how-many god-cities and *puris*, for the sake of keeping India's Sanatan culture unbroken — [cities] from which the stream of devotion illuminated the populace on every side.
- This was the very great highway through which the devotion-stream that "arose in the Dravida [land]" (*Dravid upji*) had, in North India, set aglow one beacon-tower brighter than the next. Just such an awakened beacon-tower was the Udayeshvar Mahadev temple of Udaipur.
C03-JAGTA-EN-009   ≈ p. 165–166 (Hindi original)
## Temples of India (TEMPLES-IND, 5)
A second example of a Maurya temple uncovered by excavations—Temple 40 at Sanchi—has likewise a consistent plan. It was a stone temple on an apsidal plan, enclosed by an ambulatory and raised on a high, rectangular (26.5 mts. × 14 mts.) socle, approached by two flights of steps from diagonally opposite sides. The superstructure, possibly of timber, has disappeared.
C03-TEMPLES-IND-002   p. 17
The earliest group of Gupta temples, dating from 5th century and showing a single-celled sanctum with a shallow portico resting on 4 pillars in front, is represented by Temple 17 at Sanchi (the site of the famous Buddhist Stupa of the 2nd Century B.C.), the Gupta Temple at Tigawa (District Jabalpur), and the Temples at Eran (District Sagar). A notable feature of these temples is the wider intercolumniation of the pillars in the middle than on the sides. As suggested by the flat roof, square or rectangular in form, the plain treatment of the walls and modestness of size, these temples must obviously have been derived from rock-cut proto-types of which early Gupta examples occur at Udaigiri near Sanchi. In fact Cave-shrine 1 at Udaigiri with a partly rock-cut sanctum and a structural porch of 4 pillars is of the early Gupta type, supporting the aforementioned inference.
C03-TEMPLES-IND-003   p. 24
As these regions were largely under the sway of the Gurjara-Pratiharas during the 8th and 9th centuries, we may call this style Pratihara. Since the Pratiharas ruled over an extensive empire from Kanauj, the style spread over vast tracts of North India, including the present States of Uttar Pradesh and the Punjab as far as the Himalayas and had its reverberations further east and west.
C03-TEMPLES-IND-005   p. 38
Both have elaborately ornamented pillars and entrance door-frame of five bands, but while the Jarai Math is an unpretentious structure comprising only a sanctum and a vestibule, the Gadarmal Temple adds to these a mandapa with transepts and a porch enclosed by a high balustrade punctuated with projecting elephant heads. The Gadarmal Temple stands on an ample, ornate platform surrounded by seven subsidiary shrines and has lavishly decorated mandapa pillars and is a little more evolved on plan and in design than the foregoing Pratihara temples.
C03-TEMPLES-IND-007   p. 41
The Chandellas of Jejakabhukti who supplanted the Imperial Pratiharas of Kanauj, the Kalachuris of Dahala and the Paramaras of Malwa were mighty powers which administered parts of this region simultaneously and competed with one another in the arts of war and peace. There were also lesser dynasties such as the Kachchhapaghatas of Narwar, Dubkund and Gwalior. These powers vied with one another in building temples with which Central India was once thickly studded, but of which only a small fraction has survived the ravages of man and nature. The rich traditions of Gupta art and architecture were kept up by the Pratiharas whose monumental remains in this region have already been noticed in a previous chapter. Between the 10th and 12th centuries the Kalachuris kept the torch burning in the eastern part of Central India, the Chandellas in the central part, the Paramaras largely in the western part and the Kachchhapaghatas mainly in the northern part.
C03-TEMPLES-IND-008   p. 74   also Ch 4
## The Hindu Temple, Volume I — Stella Kramrisch (KRAM-1, 5)
Here are some of the earliest inscriptions: On a Garuḍa pillar from Bhilsa, an ‘uttama Prāśada, of Bhagavat, 2nd century B.C., J. N.
C03-KRAM-1-003   p. 146
Brāhmanical worship cut its entry into the rock. It has the shape of a small, flat roofed Garbhagrha. To it is added, as in the contemporary Gupta temples, a structural porch or manḍapam (Udayagiri, Bhopal, C. I.).
C03-KRAM-1-004   p. 181
99 This earliest Brāhmaṇical rock-cut temple is amongst the earliest fully preserved Brāhmaṇical shrines. It dates from the reign of Candragupta II, 382-401 A.D. (Cunningham, ASR, Vol. X, p. 41).
C03-KRAM-1-005   p. 181
The places of pilgrimage are distributed through the entire country and are called Tirtha and Kṣetra. The number of these sacred sites is large; the 'Mahābhārata' speaks of hundreds of places of pilgrimage.\( ^{2} \)
Tīrtha is the name of a place of pilgrimage on the bank of a river, the sea-shore or a lake.
C03-KRAM-1-006   p. 14
Heretics, such as the Buddhists and Jains enlarged and embellished those retreats into rock cut monasteries and temples. The Hindus too carved and hollowed the living rock into cave temples though with some reticence, the earliest cave sanctuary, in Udayagiri in Central India dates from about 400 A.D. only.
C03-KRAM-1-007   p. 124   also Ch 7
## The Temple Architecture of India — Adam Hardy (HARDY, 1)
Early stupas were raised to encase relics of the Buddha or of a disciple or a saint, and thus radiated miraculous power.\(^{1}\) The great stupas of peninsular India, originally surrounded by thriving monastic complexes, belong to the period between the 2nd century BC and the 3rd century AD (7.1).
7.1 The Great Stupa (Stupa 1), Sanchi (MP), c 1st century BC, probably built over an earlier structure, which would have encased a sacred relic.
C03-HARDY-001   p. 78
## History of the Paramāra Dynasty — Ganguly, D. C. (GANGULY, 1)
About this time the Candellas of Jejâkabhukti extended their territory up to Bhilsa on the west. The Khajuraho inscription, dated 954 A. D., registers \( ^{4} \) the fact that at this time the Candella kingdom extended as far as Bhâsvat (Bhilsa), which was situated on the bank of the river Mâlava.
C03-GANGULY-001   p. 54   also Ch 4
## Temple Economics (Arthavyavasthā of Mandirs), Volume I — Singh, Sandeep (TEMPLE-ECON, 1)
Remnants of a Mauryan-era, brick-and-stone structure believed to be a mandir have been excavated in Baiarat district in Rajasthan and dated to the 3rd century BCE, and a similar oval, brick temple plan has been excavated at Besnagar, Vidisha, Madhya Pradesh dated to the 2nd Century BCE.
C03-TEMPLE-ECON-001   p. 25
# Chapter 4. The Paramāras and the Foundation of Udaypur: Kingship, Patronage and Urban Formation
## Jagta Hua Kasba / कथा उदयपुर — English translation (JAGTA-EN, 16)
When the Pratiharas' power weakened, three powers emerged. Among these, the Chedis of Kalachuri, the Chandelas of Jaijakabhukti, and the Paramaras of Malwa rise upon the horizon together. In their long span of five centuries, the ambitious Paramara kings extended their boundaries over a large territory. Their kingdom was spread — along with Malwa — up to Vidisha and Udaipur beyond Bhopal, and in the west up to areas like Hansol, Modhasa and Mahudi. In the south, the Paramaras were ruling over regions from the Narmada's coastal tracts, such as Hoshangabad, up to Kota in the north. Some branches of the Paramara dynasty of Malwa had succeeded in establishing their kingdoms in the Arbund Mandal — the Paramaras of Chandravati, and [those of] Bhinmal, Kiradu, Jalore and Bagar.
C04-JAGTA-EN-001   ≈ p. 139 (Hindi original)   also Ch 3
Ujjain was the first capital of the Paramaras, which Raja Bhoj took [and shifted] to Dhar — not Dhar, [but] Raja Bhoj's beloved city of Dhara. These splendid buildings, made in Raja Bhoj's time, were Sanskrit schools, where the images of Saraswati too were installed.
C04-JAGTA-EN-014   ≈ p. 64 (Hindi original)   also Ch 6
Bhoj was not only a king. He was also a deep knower and scholar of many subjects. There was a big, famous council (*parishad*) of scholars of all subjects — grammar, language, literature, drama, dance, music, water management, architecture, town planning, temple construction, vastu — to join which there was competition among scholars from as far as Kashmir.
C04-JAGTA-EN-015   ≈ p. 64 (Hindi original)   also Ch 6, Ch 5
Bhoj, like an ideal ruler, developed every kind of art in his kingdom. He gave respectful place, around himself, to virtuous people. He had splendid constructions made. He wrote books. He established knowledge-centres like the Saraswati-kanthabharana to shape the new generation.
He lived a small life, and in it too a very small opportunity of rule, in such a way that all the splendour of his time came to an end, but he is even now remembered with respect.
C04-JAGTA-EN-016   ≈ p. 66 (Hindi original)
There is quite a bit of proof that Bhopal was founded by Raja Bhoj. The Bada Talab (great lake) is still, today, spread over 36 square kilometres on the very man-made dam he built.
C04-JAGTA-EN-017   ≈ p. 80 (Hindi original)
Apart from inscriptions, their proofs and their testimony lie filled also in documents — such as the documents of that era: Padmagupta's *Navasahasanka Charita* and Merutunga's *Prabandha Chintamani*.
C04-JAGTA-EN-018   ≈ p. 138 (Hindi original)
The Saraswati Kanthabharan[s] built in the Paramaras' time were nothing less than the finest knowledge-centres of their day, where there was a tradition of teaching subjects of every kind. For centuries this knowledge-tradition endured. Even today we learn from documents that in the hostel connected with the Saraswati Kanthabharan of Mandap Durg (present-day Mandu), there was an in-charge, one Govind Bhatt by name.
C04-JAGTA-EN-022   ≈ p. 144 (Hindi original)   also Ch 6
He also drew the attention of history-lovers toward the remains of an 80-kilometre-long vast wall in the forests near Raisen, which was possibly the state boundary between the Paramaras and the Kalachuris.
C04-JAGTA-EN-047   ≈ p. 98 (Hindi original)
From within Maharashtra, Gujarat and Rajasthan, the northern boundary of the Paramaras' kingdom extended up to here, Udaipur. From here the territory of the Kalachuris began. Because of being on the border, Udaipur must have been very important for the Paramaras from a strategic point of view — which is evident too from its thousand-year-old development and expansion.
C04-JAGTA-EN-054   ≈ p. 11 (Hindi original)
The period from the ninth to the fourteenth century, during which the Paramaras existed in this region, was itself such a time that the royal dynasties present all over India were carrying out the finest construction work in their kingdoms. From the Cholas far in the south to the Chandelas of Bundelkhand, from the Chauhans in Rajasthan to the Pala kings in Bengal-Bihar, the priceless heritage built by the contribution of generations was shining at its peak. Just as, in a match, runs are scored rapidly in the last over, so, on a war-footing, this construction work was done. After the eleventh century, this "over" was really about to end — as though destiny were constantly inspiring India's countless dynasties in every corner to gather all their energy and all the talent of their craftsmen, and to depart only after fashioning some unmatched specimens of craft for the coming generations. Wherever stone was available, no corner was left [untouched] for India's great creation.
C04-JAGTA-EN-055   ≈ p. 16 (Hindi original)
The abundant availability of stone gave the Paramara dynasty the opportunity to erect splendid specimens of architecture. In this work the Paramara kings, as it were, competed with the Chola kings of Tamil Nadu. Everywhere in their kingdom they built matchless temples, palaces, tanks, stepwells, and Sanskrit education-centres. Raja Bhoj's work *Samarangana Sutradhara* is testimony to what high-grade creativity they were scholars who gave patronage.
C04-JAGTA-EN-059   ≈ p. 97 (Hindi original)   also Ch 5
Dam- and reservoir-construction was the Paramaras' expertise. Wherever vast temples were built, there even vaster reservoirs were built. In the eight months of the dry season, for the load-carriers, businessmen, sadhus, pilgrims and soldier-groups travelling in caravans, and for the thousands of cattle of their chariots and bullock-carts, these very reservoirs came in use at the time of the halt.
C04-JAGTA-EN-060   ≈ p. 110 (Hindi original)   also Ch 8
The last king of the Paramaras is Mahlakdeva, in whose time Delhi's invader-plunderer Shamsuddin Iltutmish had attacked Malwa. Then all the temples of this region were razed to the ground.
C04-JAGTA-EN-064   ≈ p. 65 (Hindi original)   also Ch 10
The remnants of the Paramaras he found in the form of kings of small, scattered principalities. At one place he has written — "The present ruler of the small principality of Saunti claims that he is a descendant of the ancient Panwars of Dhar. At the time of the Muslims' conquest, this family took refuge in the forests of Saunti. The only noteworthy Panwar family who consider Vikramaditya, the ancient chief of the Panwars of Central India, as their origin are the petty kings of Saunti." *[Malcolm's words, re-translated from the author's Hindi rendering.]*
C04-JAGTA-EN-068   ≈ p. 118 (Hindi original)
But about the Paramaras he mentioned everywhere with respect. He says that in the history of Central India, the Panwar Rajputs are considered more honourable than the other dynasties. But they were defeated by the Muslims, and long ago their kingdom ended. Later, a chieftain of this line got established in his ancestors' capital, Dhar; but he came as an attendant of a Maratha king.
C04-JAGTA-EN-069   ≈ p. 119 (Hindi original)
But Malcolm saw the Paramaras living and residing with much respect even after centuries. He is quite careful in writing about them. He knows that they are descendants of those great Paramara kings, whose golden memories of their splendid reign are spread far and wide in Central India.
C04-JAGTA-EN-070   ≈ p. 120 (Hindi original)
## कथा उदयपुर (Jagta Hua Kasba) — Hindi original (JAGTA-HI, 18)
भोज सिर्फ एक राजा नहीं थे। वे कई विषयों के गहन ज्ञाता और विद्वान भी थे। व्याकरण, भाषा, साहित्य, नाटक, नृत्य, संगीत, जल प्रबंधन, वास्तु, नगर नियोजन, मंदिर निर्माण, स्थापत्य जैसे तमाम विषयों के विद्वानों की एक बड़ी प्रसिद्ध परिषद थी, जिसमें शामिल होने के लिए कश्मीर तक के विद्वानों में होड़ रहती थी।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Bhoja was not just a king; he was also a deep scholar of many subjects. There was a famous council of scholars of grammar, language, literature, drama, dance, music, water management, vastu, town planning, temple building and architecture, which scholars even from Kashmir competed to join.
C04-JAGTA-HI-009   p. 65   also Ch 6
की तरह हर तरह की कलाओं को अपने राज्य में विकसित किया। गुण संपन्न लोगों
को अपने आसपास सम्मान से जगह दी। शानदार निर्माण कराए। किताबें लिखीं।
सरस्वती कंठाभरण जैसे ज्ञान के केंद्र नई पीढ़ी को गढ़ने के लिए स्थापित किए।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Like an ideal ruler Bhoja developed all kinds of arts in his kingdom. He gave honourable place around him to people of merit. He had splendid buildings made. He wrote books. He established centres of knowledge like Sarasvati Kanthabharana to shape the new generation.
C04-JAGTA-HI-010   p. 66
इस बात के काफी सबूत हैं कि भोपाल को राजा भोज ने बसाया था। बड़ा तालाब उन्हीं के बनाए मानव निर्मित बांध पर 36 वर्ग किलोमीटर में आज भी फैला हुआ है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* There is considerable evidence that Bhopal was settled by Raja Bhoj. The Bada Talab still spreads over 36 square kilometres on the man-made dam built by him.
C04-JAGTA-HI-011   p. 80
राजा भोज का ग्रंथ ‘समरांगण सूत्रधार’ इस बात की गवाही है कि वे किस ऊंचे दर्जे की रचनात्मकता को प्रश्रय देने वाले विद्वान थे। परमार राजाओं में भोज की हैसियत सितारों के बीच चमकते सूर्य जैसी है। उन्होंने उज्जैन, मांडू और धार में सरस्वती कंठाभरण बनवाए थे, जो सरस्वती के मंदिर और संस्कृत भाषा समेत अपने समय की ज्ञान की सभी विधाओं के शिक्षा केंद्र थे। मांडू तब मंडपदुर्ग के नाम से प्रसिद्ध था और वहां के सरस्वती कंठाभरण के एक छात्रावास के प्रभारी का नाम गोविंद भट्ट तक इतिहास में दर्ज है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* King Bhoja's treatise 'Samarangana Sutradhara' is evidence of what high level of creativity he patronised as a scholar. Among Paramara kings Bhoja's stature is like the sun shining among stars. He had Sarasvati Kanthabharana built in Ujjain, Mandu and Dhar, which were temples of Sarasvati and centres of learning in all branches of knowledge of the time including the Sanskrit language. Mandu was then known as Mandapadurga, and the name of the head of one hostel of the Sarasvati Kanthabharana there, Govinda Bhatta, is recorded in history.
C04-JAGTA-HI-012   p. 97   also Ch 6
उस दौर में राजपरिवार के अन्य महत्वपूर्ण सदस्यों को राजधानी से दूर के महत्वपूर्ण नगरों और सैन्य ठिकानों की कमान सौंपी जाती थी। एक तरह से राज्य शासन चलाने के पारिवारिक प्रशिक्षण का यह हिस्सा था और उत्तराधिकारियों को राज्यकार्य में दक्ष ऐसे ही बनाया जाता होगा। जैसे मध्य में सम्राट बनने के पहले अशोक के पिता बिंदुसार ने उन्हें अवंति का राज्यपाल बनाकर उज्जैन भेजा था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* In that period other important members of the royal family were entrusted with command of important towns and military bases far from the capital. In a way this was part of family training in running the state. Just as before becoming emperor Ashoka was sent to Ujjain by his father Bindusara as governor of Avanti.
C04-JAGTA-HI-015   p. 18
गुप्त काल में पहले इतिहासकारों का मानना था कि 'ततपादानुध्येत' शब्द सीधे उत्तराधिकारी की बात करता है। जैसे चंद्रगुप्त प्रथम, समुद्रगुप्त, चंद्रगुप्त द्वितीय विक्रमादित्य, कुमारगुप्त, स्कंदगुप्त और उसके आगे की वंश परंपरा में हर परवर्ती शासक स्वयं को अपने पूर्ववर्ती का तत पादानुध्येत कह कर संबोधित करता है।
परंतु काकनादबोट (साँची) और उदयगिरि के लेखों में क्रमशः आत्रकादवर्द और वीरसेन शाब को चंद्रगुप्त द्वितीय विक्रमादित्य का ततपादानुध्येत कहा गया जो सम्राट के विश्वस्त कमांडर थे। इससे यह माना गया कि इसका शब्दिक अर्थ "चरणों का ध्यान करता हुआ" अधिक उपयुक्त है। यह तारीफ का कसीदा है, जिसका उत्तराधिकार से कोई लेना-देना नहीं। इसलिए यहाँ शोरगढ़ अभिलेख से भोज से उदयादित्य को सीधे उत्तराधिकार मिलने का दावा कमजोर पड़ जाता है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Earlier historians of the Gupta period believed that 'tatpadanudhyata' speaks directly of a successor. But in the Kakanadabota (Sanchi) and Udayagiri inscriptions, Atrakadavarda and Virasena Shaba are called tatpadanudhyata of Chandragupta II, who were trusted commanders. Hence the literal meaning 'meditating on the feet' was considered more apt; it is praise with no relation to succession. So the claim of direct succession from Bhoja to Udayaditya weakens here.
C04-JAGTA-HI-025   p. 132   also Ch 6
इसमें प्रोफेसर फ्रांज कीलहॉर्न का नाम आता है जिसने ऐसा ही
माना। फिर इस मसले पर डीसी गांगुली का जिक्र है जो आईने-अकबरी के एक ब्यौरे
को इसी निष्कर्ष के पक्ष में लेकर आते हैं।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Here comes the name of Professor Franz Kielhorn, who held the same view. Then there is mention of D. C. Ganguly on this question, who brings an account from the Ain-i-Akbari in favour of the same conclusion.
C04-JAGTA-HI-033   p. 135   also Ch 11
गांगुली के मत का विरोध एच. सी. रे ने
अपनी पुस्तक "डायनेस्टिक हिस्ट्री ऑफ नार्दर्न इंडिया" में किया। वे बंधु का मतलब
भाई नहीं मानते।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Ganguly's view was opposed by H. C. Ray in his book 'Dynastic History of Northern India'. He does not take bandhu to mean brother.
C04-JAGTA-HI-034   p. 135
परमारों के राज में मंदिर सिर्फ पूजा-अनुष्ठान तक सीमित नहीं थे। वह स्थानीय स्तर पर एक नॉलेज सेंटर या पाठशालाओं के लिए भी उपयोग होते थे। बच्चों को वहीं अपने समय की समस्त विधाओं की शिक्षा दी जाती थी। धार, उज्जैन और मांडू के सरस्वती कंठाभरण इसके प्रमाण रहे हैं। चौक बाजार में पुराने भोपाल का ऐतिहासिक सभा मंडल भी, जो इसी उदयपुर के रचयिता और परमारों के राजवंश में राजा भोज के बाद हुए महाराज उदयादित्य ने ही बनवाया था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Under Paramara rule temples were not limited to worship and rituals. At the local level they were also used as knowledge centres or schools. Children were taught there all the disciplines of their time. The Saraswati Kanthabharanas of Dhar, Ujjain and Mandu have been proof of this. The historic Sabha Mandal of old Bhopal in Chowk Bazaar was also built by Maharaja Udayaditya, the maker of this very Udaipur, who came after Raja Bhoj in the Paramara dynasty.
C04-JAGTA-HI-050   p. 12   also Ch 8, Ch 6
परमार जिस नौवीं से चौदहवीं सदी के दौरान इस इलाके में हुए, वह समय ही ऐसा था जब पूरे भारत में मौजूद राजवंश अपने राज्यों में बेहतरीन निर्माण कार्य कर रहे थे। दूर दक्षिण में चोलों से लेकर बुंदेलखंड के चंदेलों तक, राजस्थान में चौहानों से लेकर बंगाल-बिहार में पाल राजाओं तक पीढ़ियों के योगदान से निर्मित बेशाकीमती विरासत अपने चरम पर चमक रही थी।
*Gloss [my translation]:* The period from the ninth to the fourteenth century in which the Paramāras flourished in this region was a time when dynasties across India were doing excellent construction in their kingdoms. From the Cholas far in the south to the Chandellas of Bundelkhand, from the Chauhans in Rajasthan to the Pala kings in Bengal-Bihar, the precious heritage built by generations was shining at its peak.
C04-JAGTA-HI-051   p. 16
परमार वंश के सभी राजा कलाप्रिय थे। मंदिरों के निर्माण पूरे राज्य में चलते थे। भोजपुर की चट्टान से लेकर धारा नगरी, उज्जयनी, मंडपदुर्ग और ओंकारेश्वर तक कितने नाम गिनाएं। अगर परमारों के राज में कोई सबसे व्यस्त था तो वे आदर्शीय शिल्पकार थे। वे पत्थरों पर आश्चर्य उकेरते थे।
*Gloss [my translation]:* All kings of the Paramara dynasty were lovers of art. Temple construction went on across the kingdom: from the rock of Bhojpur to Dhara, Ujjayini, Mandapadurga and Omkareshwar. If anyone was busiest under Paramara rule it was the ideal craftsmen. They carved wonders in stone.
C04-JAGTA-HI-053   p. 28   also Ch 5
कलाप्रिय परमार राजाओं की प्रतिष्ठा का एक कारण यह भी था कि बड़े ही विद्या अनुरागी थे। भाषा, व्याकरण, कविता, नाटक, वास्तु, स्थापत्य, ज्योतिष, संगीत जैसी तमाम विधाओं के जानकार उनके आसपास हुआ करते थे। राजा भोज तो इन प्रतिभाशाली कला विशेषज्ञों के बीच स्वयं एक विद्वान की हैसियत रखते थे। ऐसे माहौल में जिन उदयदित्य की परवरिश हुई होगी, उनके आसपास भी बिल्कुल वैसा ही माहौल होना ही चाहिए था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* One reason for the repute of the art-loving Paramara kings was that they were great lovers of learning. Experts in language, grammar, poetry, drama, vastu, architecture, astrology and music were around them. Raja Bhoj himself was a scholar among these talents. The environment around Udayaditya, brought up in such an atmosphere, must have been the same.
C04-JAGTA-HI-054   p. 69
वैसे पहाड़ी ऊंचाइयों की बात करें तो ओंकारेश्वर और मंदाता में भी परमार राजाओं ने नर्मदा के तटों पर अपनी श्रद्धा शिल्प में प्रकट की थी। चंदेरी और ग्वालियर में भी पहाड़ों पर आस्था के स्मारक बनाए गए।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Speaking of hilltop heights, at Omkareshwar and Mandhata too the Paramara kings expressed their devotion in sculpture on the Narmada's banks. At Chanderi and Gwalior also monuments of faith were built on hills.
C04-JAGTA-HI-055   p. 79
वह एक चौकोर रचना में वास्तु के नियमों के तहत बसाया गया एक व्यवस्थित नगर था, जिसके बीचों-बीच आज का चौक बाजार रहा होगा। परमारों के समय यह सभामंडल था, नगर का महत्वपूर्ण ब्रह्मस्थान। यह एक विशाल मंदिर परिसर था, जो संस्कृत का विद्यालय भी था। बिल्कुल उज्जैन, धार और मांडू के महान् सरस्वती कंठाभरणों की तर्ज पर। लेकिन सभा मंडल का निर्माण भोज के बाद हुआ और उदयादित्य ने ही कराया।
*Gloss [my translation]:* It was an orderly city settled in a square design under the rules of vastu, with today's Chowk Bazaar probably at its centre. In Paramara times this was the Sabha Mandal, the city's important Brahmasthana. It was a vast temple complex that was also a Sanskrit school, on the lines of the great Sarasvati Kanthabharanas of Ujjain, Dhar and Mandu. But the Sabha Mandal was built after Bhoja and was made by Udayaditya himself.
C04-JAGTA-HI-056   p. 80
पत्थर की प्रचुर मात्रा में उपलब्धता ने परमार राजवंश को यह अवसर दिया कि वे स्थापत्य के शानदार नमूने खड़े करें। इस काम में परमार राजाओं ने जैसे तमिलनाडू के चोल राजाओं से मुकाबला किया है। अपने राज्य में हर कहीं बेमिसाल मंदिर, महल, तालाब, बावड़ियां, संस्कृत के शिक्षा केंद्र बनवाए।
*Gloss [my translation]:* The abundant availability of stone gave the Paramara dynasty the opportunity to raise splendid specimens of architecture. In this work the Paramara kings competed as it were with the Chola kings of Tamil Nadu. Everywhere in their kingdom they had matchless temples, palaces, tanks, stepwells and centres of Sanskrit learning built.
C04-JAGTA-HI-057   p. 97
आज के विदिशा की पुरानी बस्ती में सबसे खास तो वही विजय मंदिर है, जो परमार राजवंश के राजा नरवर्मन ने बनवाया था। परमारों की वंशावली में राजा नरवर्मन का क्रम राजा उद्यादित्य के बाद आता है। वे उनके उत्तराधिकारी थे। मुमकिन है कि उदयपुर के नीलकंठेश्वर महादेव के मंदिर निर्माण के समय युवा नरवर्मन उदयपुर में ही रहे हों और राजधानी धार में राजकाज संभालने के बाद उनका झुकाव इस क्षेत्र में बना रहा हो।
*Gloss [my translation]:* The most special thing in old Vidisha is the Vijay temple built by Paramara king Naravarman. In the Paramara genealogy Naravarman comes after Udayaditya; he was his successor. It is possible that young Naravarman lived in Udaipur at the time of building the Nilakantheshwar temple, and kept an inclination toward this region after ruling from Dhar.
C04-JAGTA-HI-063   p. 57
मगर परमारों के बारे में उसने हर जगह इज्जत से जिक्र किया। वह कहता है कि सेंट्रल इंडिया के इतिहास में पवार राजपूत अन्य राजवंशों से ज्यादा सम्माननीय माने जाते हैं। लेकिन उन्हें मुसलमानों ने हराया और बहुत समय पहले उनका राज्य खत्म हो गया। बाद में इस वंश का एक सरदार अपने पूर्वजों की राजधानी धार में स्थापित हो गया।
*Gloss [my translation]:* But about the Paramaras he mentioned them everywhere with respect. He says in Central India's history the Pawar Rajputs are considered more respectable than other dynasties. But they were defeated by Muslims and their kingdom ended long ago. Later a chief of this line settled in Dhar, their ancestors' capital.
C04-JAGTA-HI-066   p. 118
जैसे-पद्मगुप्त के नवसाहसांक चरित और मेरतुंग का प्रबंध चिंतामणि जैसे उस दौर के दस्तावेज।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Such as the documents of that era like Padmagupta's Navasahasankacharita and Merutunga's Prabandhachintamani.
C04-JAGTA-HI-067   p. 138
## Corpus Inscriptionum Indicarum, Vol. VII, Part 2: Inscriptions of the Paramāras — Trivedi, Harihar Vithal (ed.) (CII, 137)
In the lower field of the left side and below the last line of the second plate is incised the representation of flying Garuda, the symbol of the Paramāra house of Mālava, holding a snake in his right arm and with the left hand raised above as if to
C04-CII-001   p. 20
As stated above, the plates constitute two grants which were issued on the bank of the Māhī by the Mahārājādhirājā and the crest-jewel among the feudatories (sāmanta-chūdā-maṇi), the illustrious Siyaka, who, as we shall see below, belonged to the royal house of the Paramāras.
C04-CII-002   p. 22
The date borne by both the grants, in the last line of each, in decimal figures only, is Māgha vada 30, Wednesday, 1005, which, as calculated by Dikshit, regularly corresponds to 31st January, 949 A.C., showing that the year was Kārttikādi and the month was amānta.
C04-CII-003   p. 22
144; and it also appears to be in consonance with the well-known fact that Krishna III, or his successor Khottīga, according to a different view, led an expedition into Mālwā against the Paramāra ruler Siyaka, probably necessitated by the latter's assumption of the title of Maharājaadhirajapati (11. 12-13) in the present grants and thus proclaiming his independence.
C04-CII-004   p. 22
Siyaka of both the present inscriptions is evidently identical with his namesake, the father of Vākpati-Muñja, as we know from the latter's Dharampuri, Ujjain and Gaōnri grants, issued respectively in V. 1031 (975 A.C.), 1036 (980 A.C.) and 1038 and 1043 (981 and 986 A.C.).¹ The first of these grants was issued 25 years later than the present records, which is just the period of one generation, as pointed out by the editors of these grants in the Ep Ind.; and that Śiyaka is also the same ruler for whom we have another date, viz. V. 1026, or 969 A.C. This shows that he held the throne at least from 949 to 969 A.C., which too is roughly a period of one generation.
C04-CII-005   p. 23
\( ^{1} \)  Then we have a metrical portion (vv. 2-4) introducing three kings, viz., Bappaiparāja, Vairisinha and Siyaka, the latter in each case being the son and successor of the former.
C04-CII-006   p. 23
Counting back from 974 A.C., the earliest known date of Vākpati-Muñja (his Dharampurī grant), and allowing 25 years to each of the three rulers mentioned in the present inscriptions, Bappaiparāja may be taken to have begun his career some time about 900 A.C., as a participant in the warfare of the Rāṣṭrakūṭa Krishna II (878-914 A.C.) in the latter's invasion of Gujarāt and the surrounding regions; \( ^{2} \)  and he may have gained for himself the region of Khēṭaka. His son and successor Vairisiṃha appears to have pressed further in the east and entered Mālava, probably during the Rāṣṭrakūṭa campaign is the north; and his son and successor Sīyaka, who may be presumed to have taken a further bold step, established himself in this region, capturing Ujjain, probably from Mādhava, a governor under the Pratīhāra king Mahēndrapāla II.
C04-CII-007   p. 24
In the lower right corner of the plate there is a representation of a flying Garudā facing right and depicted as a human being, except for the wings attached to his shoulders; he is holding a hooded snake in his left hand and his right hand is raised to strike it, as we generally find in the charters issued by the Paramāra rulers of Mālwā.
C04-CII-008   p. 29
Line 9 mentions the date, given in words only, the fifteenth of the dark half of Āśvina of the year 1026, which must evidently be referred to the Vikrama era and which for the Northern V. expired, corresponds to Thursday, 14th October, 969 A.C.¹ This date is of great historical interest, as it indicates that Siyaka, who issued the Harsōlā grants in V.S. 1005, continued to be on the Paramāra throne at least up to V.S. 1026 and was succeeded by his son Vākpati-Muñja, some time before V.S. 1031, which is so far the latter's earliest known date.
C04-CII-009   p. 32
It mentions the name of the illustrious Vākpatirājadāva, otherwise known as Amōghavarshadēva, bearing the epithets Pyithvīvallabha and Srīvallabha, who was the lord paramount meditating on the feet of the Paramabhattāraka. Mahārājādhirāja and Paramēśvara Siyakadēva, who meditated on the feet of P.M.P. Vairisinihadēva, who, in his turn, meditated on the feet of P.M.P. Krishnarājadēva. The record does not mention the name of the house to which these kings belonged, but from the names given in it and also from the Garudā symbol engraved at the end, it can obviously be known to be that of the Paramāras of Mālwā.
C04-CII-010   p. 34
The date, which is expressed in words in lines 13-14 and again in figures in lines 32-33, is the fourteenth day of the bright half of Bhādrapada in the year 1031, which, for the Chaitrādi expired year, would correspond to 3rd September, 974 A.C. and, for the kārtikādi expired, to 23rd August, 975 A.C.\( ^{1} \)
C04-CII-011   p. 34
The present grant is the first of those issued by Vākpatirāja alias Muñja and is dated (V.) S. 1031, which is equivalent to 975 A.C.; and as his father Sīyaka's last grant shows the year which corresponds to 969 A.C., it is evident that Vākpatirāja occupied the throne of Mālwā some time between the two dates. It is also interesting to note that the present one is the first royal charter issued by a member of the house from Mālwā, all the previous ones being issued from Gujarāt.
C04-CII-012   p. 35
The inscription is one of the Mahārājā, the illustrious Paramabhattāraka, Mahārājādhivāja and Paramēśvara Vākpatirājadevā, alias Amōghavarsha, who belonged to the Paramāra ruling house of Mālwā; and its preliminary portion consisting of the maṅgala-ślōkas and the mention of the genealogy is literally identical with that of the Dharampuri grant that immediately precedes and was written only five years earlier, in V.S 1031.
C04-CII-013   p. 40
other place in the region adjoining to it which is the north-western part of Málwā, may also go to suggest, of course tentatively but as it appears likely, that on this occasion Vākpati-Muñja appears to have encamped at this place in course of his struggle with some of his enemies in the west and north-west, viz., the Chaulukya Mūlarāja I, the Guhilā Sāktikumāra and the Nāḍol Chāhammāna Balirāja, all of whom are known to have been defeated by him.
C04-CII-014   p. 41
But how the plates could travel from the distant Deccan to the region of Mālwā cannot be ascertained unless we hold with Dikshit who conjectured that it is possible that in his invasion of the Rāshṭrakūta capital which took place in about 973 A.C., the Paramāra king Siyaka snatched it either from the Rāshṭrakūta treasury or from the owner and brought it with him along with the other booty and that later on it was used for engraving a fresh inscription by Vākpati.
C04-CII-015   p. 43
The record opens with two mangala-slōkas, followed by the genealogical portion mentioning three predecessors of Vākpatirājadēva, viz. Krishnarāja, his successor Vairisinīha, and his successor Siyaka. This portion is identical with that of the other records of Vākpatirāja, and nothing need be said here in this respect. We have already seen above that they all belonged to the Paramāra house of Mālwā.
C04-CII-016   p. 44
The date of the grant, as recorded only in words in ll. 11-12, is the winter solstice (Udagayana) in the month of Māgha of the (Vikrama) year 1943, which corresponds to 22nd December 986 A. C. In the end there is another date given in figures; it is thirteenth day of the dark half of the month of Māgha and corresponds to Friday, the 31st of December, 986 A.C. \( ^{4} \)  The date is important as it is furnished by the latest known epigraphical record of Vākpatirāja, though we know from some other sources that he continued for some more years.
C04-CII-017   p. 52
in length and it is wholly devoted to the sign-manual of the king; whereas on the second, the last five lines are shorter than the others by 7 cms., as their initial space is occupied by the representation of a flying Garuda, with the body of a man but the head of a bird, with a cobra in the proper left hand and the right being raised above, as if to strike it. This device is noticed on most of the plates issued by the rulers belonging to the Paramāra house of Mālwā; and the area occupied by it measures 8 cms.
C04-CII-018   p. 74
The inscription is a royal charter issued by the Parama-bhaṭṭāraka, Mahārājādhirāja and Paramēśvara, the illustrious Bhōjadēva, who meditated on the feet of the P.M.P., the illustrious Vākpatirājadēva, who Sindhurājadēva, who meditated on the feet of the P.M.P., the illustrious Vākpatirājadēva, who again, had meditated on the feet of the P.M.P., the illustrious Siyakadēva (ll. 3-6). The genealogy is already known to us and the text of the inscription is practically identical with that of the other charters issued by Bhōjadēva, e.g. the Betmā grant, differing only in respect of the grant portion and the date.
C04-CII-019   p. 85
From Mērutunga we learn that Vākpati invaded the Chālukya territories which touched the southern borders of his kingdom, as many as six times; but when in his seventh expedition in that land he crossed the Gōdāvarī, ignoring the advice of his minister Rudrāditya, he was taken prisoner by the Chālukya king Tailapa, who first treated him honourably, but an attempt to escape being detected, assassinated him. \( ^{2} \)  This story is corroborated by Chālukya inscriptions. \( ^{3} \)  This tragic event must have taken place between 993-94 and 997-998 A.C., the first of these dates being furnished by Amitagati who then composed his Subhāshutatvamsandōha, \( ^{4} \)  and the second is known to be the last year of Tailapa.
C04-CII-024   p. 133
It must be admitted here, however, that we have no details regarding Sindhurāja's victories over all these powers: but he must have taken at least seven or eight years to perform all these military exploits before Padmagupta, who too alludes to some of them, must have completed his work. In view of this, Bühler has concluded that this prince must have reigned at least up to the middle of the first decade of the eleventh century when Padmagupta wrote. How much longer Sindhurāja may have ruled is not known: but he must have certainly closed some time before 1011 A.C.
C04-CII-025   p. 133
Vākpati's younger brother was Sindhurāja, who is stated to have won a victory over a king of the Hūnas. To the description of Sindhurāja our prāsaṭi gives only half a verse (16); but the N.S. Charita, besides corroborating his success against the Hūna king, credits him with victories over a prince of the Kōsala as well as over the inhabitants of Vāgaḍa and the Muralas (X, 14-20).
C04-CII-026   p. 133
The prasatsi then proceeds to trace the origin of the house of the Paramāras, by describing the Mt. Arbuda (Ābū), which is here said to have humbled the pride of the noblest of the mountains (the Himalayas), and refers to the creation of the eponymous hero of the name of
C04-CII-027   p. 177
Verse 4, which is in praise of Śiva, indirectly also introduces the fire-born race which is said to have excelled the solar and lunar races. It is noteworthy that invocation to some of the deities occurs here for the first time in the records of the Paramāra house.
C04-CII-028   p. 177   also Ch 6
Paramāra by the sage Vasishtha who was performing penance there, from his sacrificial pit, as we find in the Udaipur prasasti, dealt with above. \( ^{1} \)  The following four stanzas (16-19) describe Paramāra's descendant Vairisimha as a warrior endowed with valour, liberality and fame, and verses 20-25 introduce Vairisimha's son Siyaka and the latter's son Muñjarāja, devoting three stanzas to describe them each. Muñjarā mentioned here is no other than Vakpatirāja II of the Udaipur prasasti, as is evident from the genealogical contents. It is noteworthy that the first four names occurring in the Udaipur prasasti, i.e., those of Upendra, Vairisimha (I), Siyaka (I) and Vākpati (I) are omitted in the present inscription which begins from the mention of Vairisimha.
C04-CII-029   p. 178   also Ch 6
13 we find the name of Vairisimha, (who is evidently the second ruler of the name, mentioned in the Udaipur prasati), as his son Siyaka (II) (called Siyāka here for metrical exigencies) is mentioned in the immediately following portion, with some of his exploits which are well known. The description as a whole is poetic, except that we learn in l. 13 that Vairisimha performed a tulādāna(? ) and that his son Siyaka II, as referred to above, defeated the Hūnas and enjoyed that portion of the earth for long.
Vairisinha's success over the Hūnas is known from the Navasāhasānkacharita,\(^{1}\) but so far as I know, the present inscription is the only record affording epigraphical confirmation of what is recorded in a literary source.
C04-CII-030   p. 299
The next ten verses, in ll. 3-8 which are all fragmentary, appear to have embodied the mythical account of the origin of the Para-māras, as to be found in the Udaipur prasasti and some other records of the house, telling us about the sage Vasishtha, his penances on the Mount Arbuda and the creation of a hero of the name of Paramāra, who was the originator of the house.
C04-CII-031   p. 299   also Ch 6
In 1. 17 appears the name of Vākpatirāja, who was also known as Muñja, as said here. He was a multitude of fame; and in 1. 19, we find his brother Sindhurāja mentioned.
C04-CII-032   p. 299
broad, is engraved the figure of Garuda, the emblem of the royal house of the Paramāras, in human form, kneeling towards the left and with four hands, the upper two of which are folded over the breast, the lower right lifted up with fingers raised and the lower left holding a snake with its hood raised. The figure has a bird's beak, and shows its hair raised up: its face is bearded, as of the Rājputs, and it has a necklace, armlets and anklets.
C04-CII-033   p. 306
In the proper right side beneath the writing, in a square measuring 19 cms., is engraved a representation of Garudā, the emblem of the royal Paramāra dynasty and common to all the grants issued by the members of this house.
C04-CII-034   p. 322
Verses 10-11 invoke the blessings, respectively, of the boar-incarnation of Vishnu and of the Pitāmaha, i.e., Brahmā; and verse 12 states that the latter of these deities, who sprang from the naval of the former, created from his own mind the Universe, the first of his creation being the seven sages, of whom Vasishtha was the foremost. \( ^{2} \)  The next five verses refer to the well-known legend about the origin of the Paramāras, as we find in the Udaipur prāsaṃti and in some other inscriptions of the house, viz., the creation by Vasishtha of a hero whom he named Paramāra (destroyer of enemies); and verses 16-24 furnish the names of eight of his descendants; they are: Kamandalu-dhara, king of Dhārā (v.
C04-CII-035   p. 323   also Ch 6
\( ^{6} \)  The Yādava-Paramāra struggle is known to have begun in the last quarter of the tenth century and with the rise of the later Chālukya house when, according to the Saingamēr grant of 1000 A.C., Bhillama II of the Yādava house, assisted his overlord Tailapa in the latter's war against the Paramāra Muñja.
C04-CII-036   p. 325
The next verse refers to the myth of the creation by Vasishtha of the hero of the name of Paramāra who became the progenitor of a family of that name, as it is found in a number of inscriptions of the house.
C04-CII-037   p. 345
The next verse refers to the eponymous king Paramāra, who sprung from the fire-altar of the sage Vasishtha at Ābū and humbled the pride of Gādheya, the son of Gādhi, i.e., Viśvāmitra—a mythical account recorded in the several Paramāra inscriptions, as we have so often seen.
C04-CII-038   p. 397   also Ch 6
To take up the first of these sections, verses 3-4 narrate the well-known myth of the creation of the originator of the Paramāra family (which is called jāti here) by the sage Vasishtha, who also blessed it with his gōtra. This mythical account, as we have so often seen, has its origin in this region and is a later creation. It is, however, worth noting that the present inscription mentions the name of the hero created by Vasishtha as Dhūmarāja, as also in No. 75, whereas in all the other records referring to the myth, he is called Paramāra: and thus both these names appear to be identical.
C04-CII-039   p. 413
It consists of two parts, the first of which is devoted to the description of the main line of the Paramāra rulers of Mālava down to Javasinhā I, and the second, to the kings of the branch line, ending with Manḍalīka, in whose reign the record was set up.
C04-CII-040   p. 422
\( ^{1} \)  Verses 7-9 describe the scene of a battle which, from the description, appears to have been very fierce; and though the name of the Paramāra king who fought it is lost, he appears to have been no other than Siyaka II, from the fact that his son and successor Muñjadēva is described in the verses that immediately follow (10-12). Muñja is said to have been a valorous prince who defeated his enemies: these enemies are not mentioned by name, but they seem to have been the Chālukyas or the Chaulukyas with whom he is known to have waged wars. Verses 13-15 mention (Muñja's younger brother) Sindhurāja; and the next five verses (16-20) are devoted to the description of (his son) Bhōjadēva eulogising his learning and munificence in a conventional manner. After Bhōja is mentioned Jayasinha (lam-anu), who is stated to have been a great warrior vanquishing all his enemies; but the statement that he subjugated the earth bounded by the four oceans is again conventional and historically worthless.
C04-CII-041   p. 423
Verse 29 mentions a historical fact, viz., that he actively participated in Siyaka struggle against Khoṭṭigadēva at Khalighaṭṭa on the Rēvā (Narmadā) and died a heroic death. This is evidently a reference to Siyaka's expedition against the Rāshṭrakūṭas of Mānaykhēṭa, in which the Paramāras succeeded in sacking and plundering the Rāshṭrakūṭa capital, which took place in 972-73 A.C., as we have seen above while editing the Udaipur prāśastī.¹ But that Kanka actually ruled for some time is known from the epithet of mahā-nṛīpa applied to him in v.
C04-CII-042   p. 423
Then it has nine verses (3-11) to describe the Mount Arbuda (Abū), the sage Vasishtha engrossed in penance there, and the creation of a warrior by him from his sacrificial pit, to fight with the forces of Visâmitra who had stolen away his (Vasishtha's) cow. The myth of the creation of the race of the Paramāras is a later invention, as we have often noted, simply to glorify the family.
C04-CII-043   p. 433
The next verse relates the myth of the origin of the Paramāras from the fire-altar of the sage Vasishtha on Mount Ābū, as we have seen so often; it also introduces Sindhurāja, a king of Maru-mandala.
C04-CII-044   p. 481   also Ch 6
The first hemistich of this verse mentions a king and compares him with Dharanidhara, i.e., Varāha: “and just as the latter supported the dharaī (Earth) immersed in Sindhurāja (the ocean), so the king also supported the dharaī (kingdom) of Sindhurāja”, the originator of the family.
C04-CII-045   p. 483
The record commences with the familiar legend relating to the creation of the Paramāra ruling house by Vasishtha, from his sacrificial fire (ll. 1-2), and then gives the names of the rulers of the family to which Visala belonged. The first of these names is that of Vākpatirāja, and he is stated to have been followed, in succession, by Chandana, Dēvarāt, Aparājita, Vijjala, Dhārāvarsha and Visala, each of these kings being mentioned as the son and successor of his predecessor.
C04-CII-046   p. 492
The inscription begins with the auspicious symbol for Siddham, which is followed by the date, specified both in words and figures, to be Sunday, the first of the bright half of the month of Jyēshṭha, in the (Vikrama) Sanivat 1067, which, taking the year to be Kārttikādi, according to the practice current in Gujarāt, regularly corresponds to the sixth of May, 1011 A.C. Then are introduced four rulers of the Paramāra ruling house of Mālwā; they are Paramabhattāraka, Mahārājādhirāja and Paramēsvara, the illustrious Siyakadēva, his successor the P.M.P. and illustrious Vākpatirājadeva, his successor the P.M.P. and illustrious Sindhurajadēva, and lastly, his successor the P.M.P. and illustrious Bhōjadēva.
C04-CII-047   p. 58
Kīrtitrāja, who issued a grant on the Tāptī in Śaka 940 or 1018 A.C.,\( ^{1} \) was a contemporary of the Paramāra Bhōja; and, in view of this, Vatsarāja of the present record appears to be the homonymous prince, i.e. a son of Kīrtitrāja; and it is not improbable that he was enjoying the said region where the inscription under study has been discovered, and may have been a feudatory of Bhōja,\( ^{2} \) who was then aggrendising himself at the cost of his inveterate enemy, the Chaulukya Mūlarāja, whose inscriptions are all found to the west of the Sābarmatī, in the region bordering to that of the Paramāra kingdom.
C04-CII-048   p. 59
The present charter is the first of those issued by a Paramāra king in the Bhopāl territory, whereas all the other grants made before this one were in the Gujarāt area or in the Ujjain region. Thus we know that this part of the region was included in Bhōja's kingdom before the date of the present grant, i.e., 1018 A.C., and even before 1020 A.C. when the Bētmā and the Bānswādā grants were issued, as to be seen below.
C04-CII-049   p. 64
It then introduces the Paramabhaṭṭāraka, Mahārājādhivāja and Paramēśvara, the illustrious Bhōjadēva, meditating on the feet (was a successor) of the P.M.P., the illustrious Sindhurājadēva, who again, had meditated on the feet (was a successor) of the P.M.P. the illustrious Vākpatirājadēva, who, in his turn, had meditated on the feet (was the successor) of the F.M.P. the illustrious Siyakadēva (ll. 2-5).
C04-CII-050   p. 64
The donation was made on the occasion of a lunar eclipse which fell, as expressed in words only, on the full-moon day of Śrāvaṇa of the year 1074, which, of course, must be referred to the Vikrama era, and the corresponding Christian date, accordingly, is 30th July, 1018 A.C.
C04-CII-051   p. 64
Of the localities mentioned in the inscription, Dhārā (l. 7) is obviously Dhār, the Paramāra capital and now the headquarters of the District of the same name in Madhya Pradesh.
C04-CII-052   p. 65
26 of the grant is mentioned the date, in words, which is the fifteenth day of the bright half of Bhādrapaṇa of the (Vikrama) Saṃvat 1076. We have no further details by which the date can be verified; however, taking the year as commencing with the month of Kārttika, as indicated by the custom prevalent at the time \( ^{1} \) , the month and the year correspond to September 1020. If the line of thought followed here is correct, we are justified in agreeing with the editor who observed that the present grant is about nine months later than the Bāṃsvaḍā grant, which was issued on the fifth of the bright half of Māgha in the same year and which corresponds to 3rd January, 1020.
C04-CII-053   p. 69
state that Bhōjadēva, after he had performed the daily duties and worshipped the venerable Bhavānī-pati, announced before all the royal officials and the other residents of the village of Nālatadāga falling in the territory of Nyāyapadra-seventeen, that the village was perpetually donated by him to Paṇḍita Dēlha, a son of Bhatta Thatṭhasika of the Kauśika gōtra with three pravaras, viz.
C04-CII-054   p. 69
This is obviously a reference to Bhōja's victory over Arikēsarin (c. 1015-1025 A.C.), the Silāhāra prince of Northern Konkana.¹ But both these expressions are so worded as to invite a discussion among scholars.
C04-CII-055   p. 70
And being an important event, this conquest probably continued to be celebrated at most of the places in the Paramāra kingdom for the whole year — on a different date at a different place.⁵
C04-CII-056   p. 70
This portion of the record is followed by the mention of the date, in line 31, which is the fifth of the bright half of the month of Māgha in the year 1076 (expressed in decimal figures only) of an unspecified era. The date must evidently be referred to the Vikrama era as in the case of the other Paramāra records. It cannot be verified, but taking the year 1976 to be kārttikādi expired, as we find in the Ujjain grant of the same king,\(^{2}\) it corresponds to 3rd January, 1020 A. C.\(^{1}\)
C04-CII-057   p. 75
Like both these inscriptions, the present one, after paying obeisance to Śiva under the name of Vyōmakēsa and Smarārāti in two stanzas, in anushṭubh, goes on to mention the genealogy of the house (of the Paramāras giving the names of Paramabhattāraka, Mahārājādhirāja and Paramēśvara, the illustrious Siyakadēva, the P.M.P. the illustrious Vākpatirājadēva, the P.M.P. the illustrious Sindhurājadēva and the P.M.P. the illustrious Bhōjadēva (ll. 3-7), in succession.
C04-CII-058   p. 75
But as the Uttarāyaṇa-saṅkrānti took place during the second tithi when Sunday also was civilly the second, the same scholar also held that the third tithi may have been put erroneously for the second.\(^{3}\) Of both these alternatives, we prefer to take the first, as it does not involve any error, and the corresponding Christian equivalent would be 24th December, 1021 A. C.
C04-CII-059   p. 80
Of the geographical names mentioned in the inscription, Dhārā (l. 10) is evidently the Paramāra capital Dhār, the headquarters of a District of the same name in Madhya Pradesh.
C04-CII-060   p. 80
As is the case with some of the Paramāra charters, the present one opens with the auspicious symbol which is followed by two stanzas in praise of Vyōmakēša and Smavārāti (both meaning Śiva). It then introduces the donor, Paramabhattāraka, Mahāvājādhīrāja and Paramēśvara, the illustrious Bhōjadēva, the successor of the P.M.P. the illustrious Sindhurāja, the successor of the P.M.P. the illustrious Vākpatirāja, who was the successor of the illustrious Siyakadēva. The object of the record is to issue the grant by Bhōjadēva, from his stay at Dhārā, of the village of Virāṇaka,\(^{1}\) situated in the Nāgahrada-pāschima-pathaka (ll.
C04-CII-061   p. 80
18-21), we have four of the customary imprecatory verses; and then the inscription records the date in figures only, as the fourteenth day of the bright half of Chaitra of the (Vikrama) year 1079. Ojha has calculated the date, according to the Chaitrādi reckoning, as equivalent to 19th March, 1022 A.C. \( ^{2} \)  But as we have seen in a number of instances, the year mentioned in the grants made by Bhōjadēva has to be taken as beginning with Kārttika, \( ^{3} \)  and following the same practice, the year of the present grant has to be taken as corresponding to 1023 A.C. We have to admit, however, that the record contains no further details for verifying the date.
C04-CII-062   p. 86   also Ch 6
The inscription refers itself to the reign of the illustrious Bhōjadēva who is called in it ‘the moon among the kings’ and who has been identified with the great literary person of that name and the king of Dhārā. The purpose of the inscription is to record the installation of the image of Vāgdevī, on the pedestal of which it is engraved. The year of the record, as mentioned in figures only and without any further details thereof, is 1091, which is equivalent to 1033-1034 A.C. The sculpture was engraved by Manathala, son of the mason Sahira, and the inscription was written by Sivadēva.
As we know from the Tilakamanjari, the palace of Bhōja or Bhōja-sālā as it is now popularly known, was then called Bhāratī-bhavana or Sarasvatīkaṇthābharaṇa, \( ^{3} \) and the image under reference may have been installed in it, in a prominent place, by Bhōja himself, who was a zealous devotee of the goddess Sarasvatī.
C04-CII-063   p. 90   also Ch 6
To review the contents of the record, the first of the two extant plates begins with the description of a king of the name of Bhōjadēva who slew numbers of the soldiers of his enemies by his valour and enjoyed a long reign. This statement is only in general terms and has no historical value. The name of the family of this ruler along with those of his predecessors appears to have been mentioned in the first plate which is lost; but it is not difficult to know that he was no other than the homonymous Paramāra king who issued the Mahauḍi and the other charters edited here and whose kingdom included in it the Sābarkāṃthā-Ahmedabad region, as we know from his grandfather Sīyaka's grants of the Harsōlā plates and from one of his feudatories at Mōḍāsā, which have been dealt with above.\( ^{2} \)
The inscription on then introduces the name of Bhōja's feudatory\( ^{3} \) Sūrāditya, who had migrated from Kānyakubja (Kanauj) and who had been born in the family of the Śravaṇabhadras. This hero is further stated to have killed his overlord's enemy warriors and thus rendered the sovereignty of the latter firm.
C04-CII-064   p. 92
However, it may not be impossible that the word Sāhavāhana, which appears to be the same as Sātavāhana\(^{2}\) may have been used by the composer of the inscription in its general significance to denote the southerners; and this at once brings to our mind the Western Chālukyas who were then ruling over the Deccan as successors of the Sātavāhanas and who are known to have been the hereditary enemies of the Paramāras. Following this line of thought it is not impossible to hold that this allusion may have a reference to Bhōja's war with the Chālukya Jayasimha, who is represented in one of his inscriptions as "the moon of the lotus which was king Bhōja"\(^{3}\), or it may refer to Bhōja's conquest of Kōnkaṇa as referred to in his Beṭmā and Bānswādā grants, as seen above. But owing to the absence of any reliable source of information, the precise significance of the term Sāhavāhana cannot be definitely known. It is, however, certain that Bhōja succeeded in extending the southern boundaries of his kingdom.
C04-CII-065   p. 93
After the introductory blessing, svasti, the inscription furnishes the genealogy of the Pravāimra (Paramāra) rulers (of Mālwā), naming Siyakadēva, his successor Vākpatirājadēva, his successor Sindhurājadēva and his successor Bhōjadēva, all in general terms and supplying the only historical information that Vākpatirājadēva was a poet of high rank (l. 3) and also that Bhōjadēva had vanquished the rulers of Karmṇāta, Lāta, Gūrjjara, Chēdi and Kōnkana (l. 6). Bhōja's success over the Karmṇātakas obviously refers to the long-drawn war which began from the time of Vākpati-Muñja and Tailapa.
C04-CII-066   p. 98
The inscription also refers to Bhōja's conquest of Kōnkaṇa, which is evidently the same as referred to in the Bētmā and Bānswādā copper-plate grants of V.S. 1076 or 1020 A.C., which is almost the time of Jayasimha Chālukya's inscription referred to above. It would thus appear that Bhōja's struggle with all his enemies mentioned in the present record has reference to his warfare effected during the time between the Bētmā and Bānswādā grants and that of Jayasimha at whose hands he had to suffer a reverse. At any rate, it may be taken to have been much before Bhōja's last days when he had to suffer a reverse at the hands of most of his enemies.
C04-CII-067   p. 99
The inscription is important as it indicates that Bhōja's kingdom included in it the region around Nāsik and also for the reason that it is sectarian, belonging to the Śvētāmbara sect, as a very few Jaina grants are so far known.
C04-CII-068   p. 100
The next king was Sindhurāja, who was fond of warlike activities and a poet as well (vv. 29-31). His relationship with his predecessor is not mentioned in the inscription, but we know him as a brother of Vākpatiārāja-Munja. Sindhurāja was succeeded by his son Bhōjadeva, whose description is all poetic (vv. 32-35).
C04-CII-073   p. 324
Satyarāja is stated to have fought with the Gūrjaras and obtained dignity (vibhava) from Bhōjadeva, the renowned Paramāra king of Malwā.
C04-CII-074   p. 423
The inscription belongs to the reign of the Panambhattāraka, Mahārājādhurāja, Paramēśvara, the illustrious Jayasimhadēva, who was evidently a member of the Paramāra house of Dhāra. The object of it is to record the grant, by Jayasimhadēva, of the village Bhima, situated in the territorial division of Maktulā-forty-two in the Pūrnapathaka-mangala, for the maintenance of the Brāhmaṇas residing in the patta-sālā (tent-house) at the holy Amarēśvara, for the religious merit of his parents and himself. The date is given in 1. 29 in decimal figures only: it is the thirteenth of the dark half of Āshāḍha of the (Vikrama) Sanivat 1112. For the expired Chatrīādī year 1112, this date would correspond to 27th May, 1055 A.C., and for the expired Kārtthāḍī year to 13th June or 13th July, 1056 A.C.\(^{1}\) It does not admit of verification.
After two maṇḍula-sākās which are generally to be found in the several grants of the Paramāra king and which pay homage to Īyōma-kāśa and Śmar-ārātī (both meaning Siva), the inscription gives the genealogy of the king Jayasimha, beginning with the P.M.R., the illustrious Vākpatirājadeva, his successor the P.M.P., the illustrious Sindhurājadeva, his successor the P.M.P., the illustrious Bhājadeva, and his successor the P.M.P., the illustrious Jayasimhadeva (ll. 3-6).
C04-CII-075   p. 107
We have no means to verify the date but since while discussing the date of the preceding inscription, I have given reasons to conclude that the year of the present record should be taken as current, and thus the true equivalent of the date would be 24th August, 1094 A. C. Following this line of thought, we are also justified in holding that Udayāditya died in the latter part of 1093 A.C.
C04-CII-089   p. 170; also at p. 170
Following this, it goes on to state the genealogy of the donor in the usual way, vuz., the Paramabhattāraka, Mahārājādhurāja and Paramēśvara, the illustrious Sindhurāja, his successor P.M.P. Bhōjadēva, his successor P.M.P. Udayādityadēva, and his successor P.M.P. Naravarmadēva (ll. 3-6).
C04-CII-090   p. 170   also Ch 6
The importance of the present inscription, however, lies in the fact that the gift was made on the occasion of the annual funeral ceremony of Udayādiya in V.S. i152 or 1095 A.C., by his son Naravarman, whose earliest known date is supplied by the preceding inscription, \( ^{4} \) giving the year to be V.S. 1151 or 1094 A.C., which is ten years earlier than the earliest known date of the king, and here we have also to bear in mind that it is a private record. This date, however, is incidentally corroborated by the present charter which was issued by a member of the royal house himself. It also makes it evident that Udayādiya died a year before V.S. 1152, which is stated to have been the year of his annual funeral ceremony.
C04-CII-091   p. 171
This portion contains nothing new, but what is noteworthy here is the omission of the name of Jayasinha, the son and successor of Bhōja, as we also find in the undated Udaipur prasati, the Nagpur prasati of Naravarman, the Ujjain grant of Yasōvarman and the Kadambapadraka grant of Naravarman just referred to above. \( ^{2} \)  The omission is probably because Jayasinha's reign was not long, as rightly assumed by Kielhorn while editing the Mándhāta plate-inscription of Jayavarman himself.
C04-CII-092   p. 171   also Ch 6
The Prabandhachintāmaṇi tells us that in the last days of Bhōja the Kalachuri Karṇa and the Chaulukya Bhāma made an alliance against him and invaded Mālwā, respectively from the east and the west and took possession of his kingdom. \( ^{1} \)  As we have seen above, Bhōja's unfortunate end is also hinted at in the Udaipur prasasti; and we know that in that predicament his successor Jayasinha regained his hereditary kingdom through the help of the Chālukya king Sōmeśvara-Ahavamalla, who sent his son Vikramāditya VI to help him against the enemies. \( ^{2} \)  The expression nājye cha kuly-ākule in the verse under reference may also hint at some internal disturbance in the Paramāra kingdom after Bhōja had deceased, and Kielhorn's suggestion that it was 'possibly crowded with nobles' appears to be correct in view of the use of the particle cha in the first foot of the verse.
C04-CII-093   p. 178
The next three verses (32-34) introduce Bhōjadeva's relative (bandhu) Udayāditya, who, according to the Dōngargāon inscription, \( ^{1} \)  was his brother.
Verse 34, which is historically important, states that 'when, after the death of Bhōja, the kingdom was troubled by the enemies, Udayāditya played the part of the primeval Boar in delivering the earth (i.e., his kingdom) which had submerged in the mighty ocean in the form of Karṇāṭas, Karṇa and other kings, as we know from the word prabhytti used in it.
C04-CII-094   p. 178
Considering this, I am inclined to hold that the Karṇāta king mentioned in the present inscription appears to be Ereyānga and Karṇa was no other than the Kalachuri king who had once before invaded Mālwā but was repulsed by the Chālukya Vikramāditya coming for Jayasiniha's help, as just seen. The word pūbhijti of the verse under reference thus appears to denote the other king to be the Gaṅga Udayāditya. And this confederacy of three enemies attacking Mālwā from different sides, with the agreement of exterminating it appears to be also referred to in v. 5 of the Dōngargāon inscription, which tells us that the attacks of three enemies were repulsed by Udayāditya who acted as the great Boar at the time of the pralaya, which corroborates the statement of the present record.
C04-CII-095   p. 178
Sōmēśvara entered into a quarrel with his younger brother Vikramāditya; and in the fatricidal war that ensued between the two brothers, the latter appears to have been helped by Jayasiniha, who was indebted to him for help in regaining his throne. To subdue Jayasiniha, Somesvara sent a contingent against Mālwā under his general Gaṅga Udayāditya and his feudatory, the Hoysalā Ereyānga, and he also appears to have made an alliance with the Kalachuri Karṇa, who too advanced towards Mālwā almost simultaneously with him. Jayasiniha's army was utterly routed in the battle, resulting in a severe catastrophe befalling his kingdom. He was possibly also killed in the battle, as the expression magna-svāmini of the verse indicates.
C04-CII-096   p. 178
This grandiloquent account, with its high literary value, is of little use to the historian, as we have often stated; but the importance of the inscription lies in the fact that this is the only record where we find the name of Lakshmadēva, who was the eldest son of Udayāditya and who is otherwise unknown.
C04-CII-097   p. 179
Verses 33-34 speak of Udavāditya's valour and glorify him. But the description is all general, not mentioning any specific exploit undertaken by him.
C04-CII-098   p. 179
It then proceeds to trace the genealogy of the ruling house of the Paramāras, beginning it from the Paramabhattāraka. Mahārājādhirāja, and Paramēśvara Sindhurāja, his successor P.M.P. Bhōjadēva, his successor Udayāditya and his successor Naravarman (ll. 2-5). This portion is identical with the corresponding portion of the previous grant.
C04-CII-099   p. 186   also Ch 6
As in the case of many Paramāra grants, the inscription opens with Om wasti and Srīr = jjayō = bhyudayaś = cha, followed by two verses in Anushṭubh, in praise of Śiva. It then gives the genealogy of the house of the Paramāras, mentioning the names of Udayāditya, Naravarman, Yaśōvarman and Jayavarman, in succession, and each of these with the epithets of Paramabhattāraka, Mahārājādhivāja and Paramēśvara (ll. 4-7). The relationship of the first three of these princes, from father to son, is known from the records edited above, and the present inscription which only adds to our knowledge by mentioning Jayavarman as the successor of Yaśōvarman, is however silent about their mutual relationship.
C04-CII-100   p. 209
\( ^{6} \)  But Jayavarman may be assumed to have had more intimate relations with his contemporary Kalachuri king Gayākarṇa, who had married Alhanadēvī, the grand-daughter of Udayāditya; \( ^{7} \)  and in view of this, we may hypothetically hold that soon after the capture of Mālwā and of Yasōvarman, Jayavarman may have moved to the eastern fringe of his kingdom where he ruled for some time as an independent king, as is indicated by the expressions Parana-bhattāvaka-Mahāvājā-dhirāja-Paramēśvara of the present inscription.
C04-CII-101   p. 210
It then introduces the house to which Lakshmīvarman belonged, mentioning the names of the Paramabhattāraka, Mahārājā-dhivāja and Paramēśvara, the illustrious Udayādītyadāva, his successor the P.M.P. Naravarma-dēva, his successor the P.M.P. Yaśōvarmadēva and thereafter we read in it the name of Lakshmī-varman (ll.

C04-CII-104   p. 270
Thereafter, the inscription is silent on the whole of the unpleasant episode following Bhōja's death, and merely states that then arose Udayāditya, who was the boar in relieving the earth (Paramāra kingdom) which had been drowned in the ocean of (in the form of) the Gurjara king (v. 36). This is evidently a reference to Udayāditya's success in repulsing the attack of the Chaulukyas, in alliance with the Kalachuris, on Mālava, as we know from history. How Udayāditya was related to Bhōja is not stated in the inscription, but from the Dōngargāon inscription of the time of Jagaddēva, of Šaka 1034 or 1112 A.C., we know him to have been the latter's younger brother.
C04-CII-105   p. 324
The adjective  \( nity-otṣaḥaika-kautukī \)  appearing in the second foot of the verse and applied to Udayāditya has been translated by Wilkinson as “whose constant delight was the pursuit of pleasure”. This is evidently wrong and goes against history. I take the expression to mean “whose sole delight was to perform deeds of valour”. This ruler is known to have subdued the enemies who had overpowered the kingdom in the last days of Bhōjadēva, as also referred to by the expression  \( mahā-kalaha-kalpa \)  in the next verse of the present record.
