*Gloss [my translation]:* Even in fighting the Turks he made no distinction in creative work; verses jointly composed by Bhoja and a Turk poet are found.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-005   p. 2
राजा भोज ने परम्परानुसार 55 वर्ष 7 मास 3 दिन तक गोड़ देश और दक्षिणापथ सहित विस्तृत क्षेत्र पर राज्य किया था -
पंचाशत्वंचवर्पाणि सप्तमासदिनत्रयम् ।
भोजराजेन भोक्यः सगौड़ो दक्षिणापथः ।।
इससे प्रतीत होता है कि उसने अपने विस्तृत राज्यक्षेत्र पर दीर्घकाल तक शासन किया था ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* By tradition Bhoja ruled 55 years 7 months 3 days over a wide region including Gauda country and Dakshinapatha, which suggests a long rule.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-006   p. 3
राजा भोज (999-1055 ई.) इसी सिन्धुराज का पुत्र था। उसके 1010 से 1055 ई. तक के कई ताप्रपत्र, शिलालेख या भूतिलेख प्राप्त होते हैं। एक ताप्रपत्र के अन्त में लिखा गया है - स्वहस्तोयं श्रीभुजदेवस्य । अर्थात् यह (ताप्रपत्र) भोजदेव ने अपने हाथ से लिखा (और दिया) है ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Raja Bhoja (999-1055 CE) was this Sindhuraja's son; several copper plates, stone inscriptions and image inscriptions of 1010 to 1055 CE are found; one copper plate ends 'this is the own hand of Shri Bhojadeva', i.e. Bhojadeva wrote and gave it himself.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-007   p. 3
ताम्रपत्रों में भोज के प्रथमों की पुष्पिकाओं में उन्हें 'महाराजाधिराज परमेश्वर श्रीभोजदेव' कहा गया है। स्पष्ट ही राजा भोजीराजाओं के भी राजा थे।
*Gloss [my translation]:* In the colophons of the copper plates Bhoja is called 'Maharajadhiraja Parameshvara Shri Bhojadeva' - clearly a king of kings.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-009   p. 4
ऐसे वारह मण्डलों के स्वामी राजा भोज के अधीन थे तव समरांगणसूत्रधार रचा गया। ऐसे मंडलों में अवन्ति मण्डल, पूर्णपथक मण्डल, मोहड़वासक, अर्थाएट मण्डल, स्थाली मण्डल, संगमखेट मण्डल, हूण मण्डल इत्यादि उल्लेखनीय हैं ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Lords of twelve such mandalas were subordinate to Bhoja when the Samarangana-sutradhara was composed; notable mandalas include Avanti, Purnapathaka, Mohadavasaka, Arthuna, Sthali, Sangamakheta and Huna.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-010   p. 4
भोज परम दानी था । यह कहा जाता था कि श्रुओं को शुंखलावढ़ करने से लोहा और दानपत्र लिखने से भोज के राज्य में ताँव का अभाव होने लग गया था । यह अतिशयोक्ति हो सकती है परन्तु अमूलोक्ति नहीं हो सकती । स्वोक्ति इतिहास से भी यह सिद्ध है कि उसने अपने अनेक श्रुओं को पराजित किया था और अनेक तामप्रन जारी किये थे जिनमें से कुछ तो प्राप्त भी हो गये हैं ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Bhoja was supremely generous; it was said that fettering enemies exhausted iron and writing grant-plates exhausted copper in his kingdom; this may be exaggeration but not baseless, since history shows he defeated many enemies and issued many copper plates, some of which have been found.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-012   p. 4   also Ch 6
राजा भोज के सुदीर्घ शासनकाल में युद्ध के शंखनाद होते रहे, रणभेरी बजती रही। परन्तु वह शासन के सुनियोजन में दक्ष था। वह योजनावढ़ तरीकेसे शासन करता था। अधिकारीगण अपने-अपने विभाग दक्षता से सम्हालते थे। अतः राजा भोज अपने उन विश्वस्त सहयोगियों के भरोसे निश्चिन्त हो कलासाधना तथा साहित्यसाधना करता रहता था। कवियों, कलाकारों तथा विद्धानों का वह अद्धितीय आश्यदाता था। वह स्वयं गुणी था तथा गुणों का पारखी भी।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Though war-drums sounded through his long reign, he was skilled in administration; officials ran their departments competently, so he could pursue art and literature; he was an unequalled patron of poets, artists and scholars, himself accomplished and a connoisseur.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-013   p. 8
भोज न केवल मित्रों अपितु शत्रुओं के लिए भी अपने गुणों में आदर्श रहा । और वे भी भोज के समान स्वयं को घोंपित करवाने में गौरव का अनुभव करते थे । गुजरात के राजा कुमारपाल स्वयं को लघु भोजराज कहलाना पसन्द करते थे ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Bhoja was an ideal in virtues for friends and foes alike; they too felt pride in being likened to Bhoja; King Kumarapala of Gujarat liked to be called 'little Bhojaraja'.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-014   p. 11
राजा भोज महाराजाधिराज परमेश्वर तो था ही क्योंकि उसके प्रस्थों से भी झात होता है कि उसके अधीनस्थ वारह राजा थे । यह उपाधि उसके ताप्रपत्रों, शिलालेखों तथा प्रस्थों की पुष्पिकाओं में प्राप्त होती है । मालवमण्डन, मालवाधीश, मालवचकवर्ती, सर्वभौम, अविन्तनायक, धारेश्वर, त्रिभुवननारायण, रणरंगमल्ल, लोकनारायण, विदर्भराज, अहिराज या अहीन्द्र, अभिनवाजून, कृष्ण आदि कितने ही विफ्तवों से भोज मण्डित था ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Bhoja was Maharajadhiraja Parameshvara, for his works show twelve kings under him; the title appears in his copper plates, inscriptions and colophons; he bore epithets such as Malavamandana, Malavadhisha, Malavachakravartin, Sarvabhauma, Avantinayaka, Dhareshvara, Tribhuvananarayana, Ranarangamalla, Loknarayana, Vidarbharaja, Ahiraja, Abhinavarjuna, Krishna.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-015   p. 15
भोज के ज्योतिर्यन्थ राजमूगांक का प्रकाशन ब्रह्मविद्या (4/3) में प्रक्राशित हुआ जो 964 शक (1042 ई.) में रचा गया।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Bhoja's astrological work Rajamriganka, published in Brahmavidya (4/3), was composed in Shaka 964 (1042 CE).
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-016   p. 20
यह समस्त वारह राजसमूह जिस राजद्धडामणि की भुजास्तम्भ से वेंधी वृत्ति का है और जिसकी इच्छा से चारों ओर घूमता है। उस श्रीमान् पृथ्वी के एक मात्र राम (तुल्य मनोहर) रा(भो)जदेव ने शीघ्र ही अपनी वृष्टि से यन्त्र प्रपंचों सहित इस यन्त्राध्याय की रचना की 167
*Gloss [my translation]:* This whole circle of twelve kings, whose conduct is bound to the pillar of the arm of that crest-jewel of kings and moves around at his will - that glorious Bhojadeva, the one Rama of the earth, quickly composed this chapter on machines together with these elaborations of devices from his own intellect.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-017   p. 92
राजा भोज एक महान् निर्मति था। भोज ने सुरक्षा की दृष्टि से अपनी राजधानी उज्जेन से हटाकर धारा नगरी कर दी। भोज ने उसे नयं ति सरे से वसाया। वह नवनिर्मित नगरी लोकाकर्षक बन गयी थी।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Raja Bhoja was a great builder; for security he moved his capital from Ujjain to Dhara and settled it anew; the newly built city became attractive.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-018   p. 5
राजा भोज के समय से मालवा में निर्माण-कांति आ गयी थी। प्रासाद, मन्दिर, तालाव, प्रतिमार्ज़ बनती रहीं और पूरा मालवा इन अनोखी कलाकृतियों से भर गया। मन्दसीर जिला इस दृष्टि से अधिक समुद्ध है। वहाँ का हिंगलाजगढ़ तो तत्कालीन अप्रतिम प्रतिमाओं का अकृत खजाना है। आज मालवा में जो कुछ निर्मितियों के प्राचीन रूप दिखाई देते हैं, उनमें से बहुधा परमारकालीन हैं।
*Gloss [my translation]:* From Bhoja's time Malwa saw a revolution in construction: palaces, temples, tanks and images; Mandsaur district is rich, Hinglajgarh a treasury of images; most ancient structures seen in Malwa today are of the Paramara period.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-019   p. 6   also Ch 7
यह वताया जाता है कि धारा नगरी में चौरासी चौराहे थे और प्रत्येक पर एक-एक मंदिर था। भोज ने चित्तीड़ में मन्दिर वनवाया था और कश्मीर के कपटेश्वर में कृष्ण सहित मन्दिर वनवाया था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* It is said Dhara had eighty-four crossroads each with a temple; Bhoja built a temple at Chittor and one with Krishna at Kapateshvara in Kashmir.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-020   p. 6
प्रवन्धचित्नामणि के अनुसार भोज के 104 भवनों, ग्रन्थों और उपाधियों के एक ही नाम थे । इसकी पुष्टि जयवर्मदेव (1258 हैं.) के खण्डित लेख से भी होती है । इसके अनुसार राजा भोज ने 104 मेठ, त्रिकूट आदि प्रासाद वनवाये थे । इससे आगे का लेख खण्डित है । इससे पूर्वांतक प्रवन्धचिन्तामणि की वात की पुष्टि होती है । इन प्रासादों की चर्चा समरंगणसूत्रधार (अध्याय 55-56) में प्राप्त होती हैं ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* According to Prabandhachintamani Bhoja's 104 buildings, works and titles had the same name; this is confirmed by a fragmentary inscription of Jayavarmadeva (1258 CE) saying Raja Bhoja built 104 prasadas such as Meru and Trikuta; the rest is broken; it confirms Prabandhachintamani to that extent; these prasadas are discussed in Samarangana-sutradhara chapters 55-56.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-022   p. 12   also Ch 6
प्रभाच्चाचार्य के अनुसार धारा में 84 प्रासाद और इतने ही चीराहे थे । पूर्वोक्त जयवर्मदेव के 1258 ई. के लेख के अनुसार भोज के मेठ, जिक्रट आदि 104 प्रासाद थे -
नाम्ना यच्यतुरन्तारं शतमियन्मेरुत्रिवादयः ।
प्रसादा: जल……
इससे स्पष्ट है कि 104 की संख्या की पुष्टि साहित्य और शिलालेख दोनों से होती है ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* According to Prabhachandra Dhara had 84 palaces and as many crossroads; per Jayavarmadeva's inscription of 1258 CE Bhoja had 104 prasadas such as Meru and Trikuta; so the number 104 is confirmed by both literature and inscription.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-023   p. 15   also Ch 6
राजा भोज स्वयं महान् निर्माता था । उल्लेखों से स्पष्ट है कि उसने शताधिक प्रासाद तो अपनी राजधानी धारा नगरी में ही वनवाये थे । धारा को उसने व्यवस्थित स्वासा ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Raja Bhoja was himself a great builder; references make clear he built over a hundred palaces in his capital Dhara alone; he planned Dhara in an orderly way.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-024   p. 21
मालवा, मेवाङ़ में कितने ही मन्दिर बनवाये, पाठशालाएँ वनवार्थी। भारत की धार्मिक दृष्टि से एकसूत्र में वाँधने के लिए धारी सीमाओं पर केदार, रामेश्वर, सोमनाथ तथा मुण्डीर मन्दिर बनवाये और मध्य के उज्मैन में महाकाल का मन्दिर भी बनवाया। इस प्रक्रिया भोज वास्तुशारत्र और उसके निर्माण की वारीकियों का मर्मजा था। यह न केवल युक्तिकल्पता अविपतु समरांगणसूत्रधार से भी सिद्ध होता है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* He built many temples and schools in Malwa and Mewar; to bind India together religiously he built Kedara, Rameshvara, Somanatha and Mundira temples on the boundaries and the Mahakala temple at central Ujjain; in this he was a master of vastushastra and its construction subtleties, as both Yuktikalpataru and Samarangana-sutradhara show.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-025   p. 22
चौसठ पद नामक पूरा नगर कल्पित करें । उसमें सोलह कोष्ठ, छः पथ और नौ चत्वर
(चौराहे) हो 14
चौकोर क्षेत्र में पूर्वे से पश्चिम आते चार भागों में तीन-तीन बॉस (वंश) का विभाजन
करें 15
छः बाँस के इस विभाजन में सोलह पद युक्त होने पर मध्यम वंश पर आश्रित शुभ
राजमार्ग बनाना चाहिए 16
*Gloss [my translation]:* Conceive the whole town as a sixty-four-pada grid, with sixteen kosthas, six paths and nine chatvaras (crossings); divide the square plot into four portions of three vamshas each; in this six-vamsha division of sixteen padas the auspicious royal road should be made on the middle vamsha.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-026   p. 45
ज्येष्ठपुर चार हजार चाप (चार कोस या प्रायः बारह किलोमीटर क्षेत्रफल) का होता है। मध्य दो हजार का और एक हजार चाप (एक कोस) व्यास का अधम होता है 12
*Gloss [my translation]:* The best town is of four thousand capa (four kos, about twelve km); the middle of two thousand; the lowest is one thousand capa (one kos) in diameter.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-027   p. 45
जो अशास्त्रज द्वारा और विना लक्षणों के ही किया गया तथा अनुपठान से पवित्र हुए विना ही किया गया वह अप्रशस्त और निष्णल होता है 173
ज्योतिधी और पुरोहित द्वारा शान्ति आदि अनुपठानों के बाद शास्त्रज शिल्पविद्ध स्थपति कर्म करे 174
पुरोहित अग्नि में हवन करे, ज्योतिपी स्थरता दे, स्थपति भेट पूजा करे - इस प्रकार शान्ति की योजना करे 175
जब नगर के मर्म (केन्द्र) में, चौराहे पर स्थायी रूप से स्थापित देवता निरन्तर पूर्ण जाते हैं तब उस नगर में शान्ति रहती है 176
*Gloss [my translation]:* Work done without shastra knowledge, without characteristics and without the rites is inauspicious and fruitless; after the astrologer and priest perform the propitiatory rites, the shastra-knowing sthapati should do the work; the priest offers oblation in fire, the astrologer gives stability, the sthapati makes offerings; when deities installed at the city's centre and crossroads are constantly worshipped, peace prevails in that city.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-028   p. 50   also Ch 8
सुवर्णकार सहित सभी अग्निजीवी और अन्य मजदूरों को आग्नेय (पूर्व-दक्षिण) दिशा में वसाये 189
वैश्य-व्यापारी, जुआड़ी-धूतं, चक्रिक (तेली, कुक्शर, मुखविर) तथा नट-नर्तकों के घर दक्षिण में बनवाये 190
*Gloss [my translation]:* Settle all those who live by fire, including goldsmiths, and other labourers in the south-east; build in the south the houses of vaishya merchants, gamblers and rogues, oil-pressers and actors and dancers.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-029   p. 51
विप्र पूर्व दिशाभाग में, क्षत्रिय पश्चिम में, वैश्य और शूद्र क्रमशः दक्षिण तथा उत्तर में किये जायें 1100
वणिक, वैद्य और मुख्य लोगों को घारों दिशाओं में वसायें । विशेषतः सेना को घारों दिशाओं में स्थापित करें ।101
नगर से वाहर पूर्व दिशा में लिंगस्थों (शैव या विभिन्न सम्प्रदायों) को वसायें । भशान और उसके निवासियों को ज्ञानी स्थपति दक्षिण में वसायें ।102
*Gloss [my translation]:* Brahmins in the east, kshatriyas in the west, vaishyas and shudras in the south and north respectively; merchants, physicians and chiefs in all four directions; the army especially on the four sides; outside the town in the east settle the lingasthas (Shaiva or sectarians); the wise sthapati should place the cremation ground and its inhabitants in the south.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-030   p. 52   also Ch 9
यूअरवाले, भेड़ पालक, शिकारी, मछुआरे, दमनाधिकृत (दण्ड वाले अधिकारी) आहे नैन्ध्त्य दिशा में वसाये जाये 191
जो रथ और आयुध में कुशल हों वे सव नगर की परिश्रम दिशा में वसाये जाये 192
विभिन्न कर्मों के अधिकारी, सेवक, सुराजीवी कलाल आदि वायव्य दिशा में वसाये 193
यतियों के आश्रय, ब्रह्मवत्स (ब्राह्मण/ब्रह्मचारी) की सभा, प्रपा (प्याऊ) और पुण्यशाला (सदावर्त, मनिर्दर, धर्मशालादि) उत्तर में दिशा में वनवायें 194
घी तथा फल के विक्रता ईशान (पूर्वांत्तर) दिशा में वंसाये जाये तो उचित है 195
*Gloss [my translation]:* Swineherds, shepherds, hunters, fishermen and officers of punishment in the south-west; those skilled in chariots and weapons in the west; officials of various works, servants and liquor-sellers in the north-west; shelters of ascetics, the Brahmin assembly, water-sheds and charitable halls (punyashala) in the north; ghee and fruit sellers in the north-east.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-031   p. 52   also Ch 9
इन प्रस्थों में सर्वाधिक लोकप्रिय वल्लाल का भोजप्रवंध है । इसमें भोज, कालिदास और अन्य अनेक कवियों से सर्वन्धित छोटी-छोटी घटनाएँ हैं । वे सव मिलकर काव्यात्मक वातावरण वना देती हैं ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Among these works the most popular is Ballala's Bhojaprabandha, with short anecdotes about Bhoja, Kalidasa and many other poets, which together create a poetic atmosphere.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-032   p. 9
शुभशील के भोजप्रबंध में कतिपय कथाएँ वल्लाल की पुस्तक जैसी भी हैं। रल्ननंदन गणि (1340 ई.) भोजप्रबंध में कतिपय कथाएँ वल्लाल की पुस्तक जैसी भी हैं । पद्मपुस्त ने भी किसी या रल्नमंदिर गणि (1460 ई.) के भोजप्रबंध में सात खंड हैं । पद्मपुस्त ने भी किसी भोजप्रबंध की रचना की थी । राजशेखर (सूरि?) का भी भोजप्रबंध रचा बताया जाता है । वत्सराजकृत भोजप्रबंध (वारहवीं सदी) का भी उल्लेख मिलता है ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Shubhashila's Bhojaprabandha shares stories with Ballala's; Ratnanandana Gani (1340 CE) and Ratnamandira Gani (1460 CE) wrote Bhojaprabandhas (the latter in seven sections); Padmagupta, Rajashekhara (Suri?) and Vatsaraja (twelfth century) are also mentioned.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-033   p. 10
इनके अतिरिक्त राजस्थानी, अवधी सहित विभिन्न भापाओं के काव्यग्रन्थों में भी भोज उपस्थित है। नरपति नाह् के बीसलदेव रासो, जायसी के पद्रायत, सिंढायय, दयालदास के पंवारवंशदर्पण, वाँकीदास री ख्यात, मालदेव के भोजचरित्र राहा और भोजचरित्र चौपाई, सारंग के भोजप्रवंध चतुप्पदी और भोजचरितरास, उदयभानु के भोजरासों, देवभद्र सूरि के श्रेयांसचरित, मुल्हण कवि के भोज राजा परकाया प्रदेशिनी पंद्रहवीं विशा कथा, हेमाणंद के भोज चरित्ररास, माघ का संवाद, कुशलधीर के भोजराजा चौपाई इत्यादि में इस राजा भोज के विषय में यथार्थ एवं कल्पित विभिन्न कथाएँ प्राप्त होती हैं।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Bhoja is present in verse works in various languages including Rajasthani and Avadhi; Jayasi's Padmavat, Dayaldas's Panwar-vansh-darpan, Bankidas's khyat, Maldev's Bhojacharitra and others contain both real and imagined tales of Raja Bhoja.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-034   p. 10
इसीलिए कविजन अपने आय्यदाता की "नवभोज राज" अपथि से प्रशंसा करते थे। इस भोज के वंशज तेरहवीं सदी के अन्त में हुए अन्य राज भोज की प्रशंसा इस पूर्वज भोज की उपमा से की गयी - भोजो भोज इवापर:।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Hence poets praised their patron with the epithet 'New Bhojaraja'; a descendant of this Bhoja, another king Bhoja at the end of the thirteenth century, was praised by comparison with this ancestor: 'Bhoja is Bhoja's equal'.
C04-BHOJDEV-HI-035   p. 12
## The Udayeśvara Temple: Art, Architecture and Philosophy of the Śaiva Siddhānta — Anupa Pande (PANDE, 4)
शैवागमेषु मुख्यं पतिपशुपाशा इति क्रमात् त्रितयम्।
तत्र पतिः शिव उक्तः पशवो ह्यणवोऽर्थपञ्चकं पाशः॥५॥
*Gloss [my translation]:* Among the Śaivāgamas the chief triad is, in order, pati, paśu and pāśa; of these pati is said to be Śiva, the paśus are the aṇus, and the fivefold set of objects is pāśa.
C04-PANDE-001   p. 112   also Ch 6
यस्याखिलं करतलामलकक्रमेण
देवस्य विस्फुरुति चेतसि तत्त्वजातम् ।
श्रीभोजदेवनृपतिः स शिवागमार्थ
तत्त्वप्रकाशमसमानमिदं व्यधत्त ॥७६॥
*Gloss [my translation]:* King Śrī Bhojadeva, in whose mind the whole body of principles shines like an āmalaka fruit in the palm of the hand, composed this unequalled Tattvaprakāśa on the meaning of the Śivāgamas.
C04-PANDE-002   p. 117   also Ch 6
Tattvaprakāśa, one of the most significant works on Śaiva Siddhānta, was authored by King Bhoja Paramāra of Malwa (1000-1055 CE). It expounds the three principles of the Lord (pati), the individual soul (paśu) and the fetters (pāśa) in accordance with the Śaivāgamas.
C04-PANDE-003   p. 118   also Ch 6, Ch 8
Verse 75: The tattva of the tattvas has also been stated with ease by King Bhoja who has composed the Tattvaprakāśa.
Verse 76: That king, in whose mind the full reality (Śaivāgama) was disclosed in the manner of an āmalaka* placed on the palm,* he was king Śrī Bhojadeva, who for the sake of Śaivāgama composed the incomparable text called the Tattvaprakāśa.
C04-PANDE-004   p. 128   also Ch 6
## King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture (Raja Bhoj aur Parmarkaleen Nagar Sthapatya) — English translation — Bhagwatilal Rajpurohit (RAJABHOJ-EN, 37)
From the literary and epigraphic sources contemporary with the Paramaras, it is learnt that the founding ancestor of this line was born from the sacrificial fire-pit (अग्निकुण्ड) of the sage Vasishtha (वसिष्ठ). No sooner was he born than he drove away the foe (*par*, i.e. enemies) by slaying them; hence the true name of that hero became "Paramar" (परमार).
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-001   p. 2
The names of various kings of the Paramara dynasty, such as Upendra (उपेन्द्र), are found. Among them, Siyak (सीयक) or Shriharsha (श्रीहर्ष) is the first Paramara king whose tenth-century copper-plate grants are found. He had two sons: Vakpatiraja Munj (वाक्पतिराज मुंज) and Sindhuraja (सिन्धुराज) or Sindhul (सिन्धुल).
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-002   p. 2
The event of King Bhoj's ancestors obtaining the kingdom of Malwa is described in the *Prabandha-chintamani*. According to it, the ancestors of Mularaja ruled at Kalyanakataka in Kanyakubja. Then Bhumada or Bhumadeva became ruler. That famous king donated the whole of Malwa to Mahakaleshvara, the lord of Ujjayini, and appointed the Paramara family as its foster-protector.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-003   p. 164
According to this story it is clearly evident that the Paramaras were once the protection-officers of Mahakal's Malava region, who later, getting the opportunity, became its masters. Full sovereignty over the kingdom was attained by King Munj, and it was conferred with the supreme glory by Maharaja Bhojadeva.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-004   p. 165
For reasons of security, King Bhoj shifted his capital from Ujjain to Dhar. Before this, he had duly settled and developed the city of Dhara to befit a capital, making it attractive with a rampart (प्राकार / outer fortification wall), a moat (परिखा / outer ditch), and — in the manner of the age — up-to-date gardens, lakes and the like. Then, following that same plan, he also re-founded Ujjain, Vidisha (विदिशा) and others afresh. Near Bhopal and near Patna he founded new cities named Bhojpur (भोजपुर) after himself.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-005   p. 1
Many copper-plate grants and stone inscriptions of King Bhoj are found. References to King Bhoj occur also in the inscriptions of other contemporary kings. Abundant references to Bhoj are found also in the poetry, treatises and so forth of the Bhoj period. In later inscriptions, texts, traditions, folk-tales, proverbs and poems, countless references to and accounts of King Bhoj are found. Our knowledge of King Bhoj from history proper is limited, but from traditional literature, oral traditions and the like we obtain much more. Thus, in gaining knowledge of King Bhoj's personality and achievements, history and tradition become mutually complementary.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-006   p. 3   also Ch 6
A text of 1055 CE was written during King Bhoj's reign; from this it is clear that King Bhoj was alive up to 1055. According to the *Ain-i-Akbari* (आइने-अकबरी), King Bhoj's age was 90 years. From various texts it is learnt that King Bhoj ruled over an India extending from Gauda/Bengal (गौड़) to the Deccan (दक्षिणापथ) for 55 years, 7 months and 3 days. Subtracting 90 from 1055 gives about 965 as the apparent time of King Bhoj's birth. If King Bhoj ruled up to 1055, then subtracting 55 years, 7 months and 3 days from that makes his accession possible around 999 CE.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-007   p. 3
Thus, by defeating various known and unknown kings — Gangeyadeva the Kalachuri king of Chedi, Tailapa II the Chalukya king of Karnataka, Indraratha the king of Adinagara, Bhimadeva of Lata, Rajyapala the Gurjara-Pratihara king of Kanyakubja, the Turushka (Turk) king, Viryarama the Chauhan king of Shakambhari, Abhimanyu the king of Dubkund, Bhillama (III) the king of Vidarbha and Marathwada, and others — Bhoj made his power and the extent of his realm all-India in reach. Hundreds of kings and great kings honoured Bhoj as their "Maharajadhiraja Parameshvara" (supreme sovereign of sovereigns) and constantly strove, through offerings of daughters in marriage (kanyopayana) and the like, to keep King Bhoj ever pleased.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-008   p. 4
Of all of them, Munj — because Bhojdev was the most capable — made Bhoj alone king. At this the other brothers and kinsmen of the family became displeased. But in the end the kingdom remained in King Bhoj's hands, and he securely enjoyed the pleasures of sovereignty all his life.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-009   p. 5
But in the *Navasahasanka-charita* and in Bhoj's copper-plates, Sindhul too is called king — probably because, after Munj's sudden death, it was Sindhuraja who for some years conducted the governance like a regent on Bhoj's behalf, since Bhoj must have remained absorbed in his pursuit of learning. This is why neither any copper-plate nor any inscription of Sindhuraja is found. Yet even then he enjoyed kingship; and so Bhoj and the later kings too, out of respect, called Sindhuraja a king. But when Sindhuraja died in a battle, King Bhoj himself had to take up the reins of governance. This was around 999 CE.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-010   p. 6
> आकीर्णाङ्गितलः सरोजकलशच्छत्रादिभिर्लच्छने-
> स्तस्याजायत मांसलायुतभुजः श्रीभोज इत्यात्मजः ।
> प्रीत्या योग्व इति प्रतापवसतिः ख्यातेन मुञ्जाख्यया
> यः स्वे वाक्पतिराजभूमिपतिना राज्येभिषिक्तः स्वयम् ॥
*Gloss [my translation]:* To him was born a son, Shri Bhoj, whose palms bore auspicious marks such as lotus, pitcher and parasol; Muñja, the abode of valour, out of affection and judging him worthy, himself consecrated him to the kingdom.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-011   p. 6
King Bhoj not only composed a voluminous treatise on craft such as the *Samarangana-sutradhara*, but also founded the cities of Dharanagari, Ujjayini, Vidisha (Bhaillasvamipur / भाइल्लस्वामीपुर), Bhojpur (Raisen / रायसेन) and the Bhojpur near Patna. He shifted his capital from Ujjayini to Dharanagari, which was his hereditary capital, because it was a secure place. He shifted Ujjain from the Gadhkalika (गढ़कालिका) area and re-founded it toward Mahakal. He re-founded Vidisha under the name Bhaillasvamipur, which was later called Bhelsa (भेलसा).
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-012   p. 16
In Sindhuraja's time, Dhara was the hereditary capital of the Paramaras and Ujjayini the chief capital. Naturally, at the time of Bhoj's accession too, Ujjain was the capital. But for the security of his own rule from enemies and from his own kinsmen, he conferred upon his hereditary capital itself the glory of the chief capital. Bhoj bestowed upon Dhara the glory of Ujjain.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-013   p. 54
From the Pratapgarh inscription of 946 CE it is learnt that Mandu and Ujjain were under the Pratiharas.¹ In 1046 CE the Karnata king Someshvara defeated Bhoj, and his commander Gundamaya seized Mandu.² Bhoj had constructed the forts of Dhar and Mandu.³ In the inscription of Bilhana, the peace-and-war minister of Vindhyavarman, found at Mandu, a charming hymn to Vishnu is found.⁴ In the latter half of the 11th century there was a temple of Kalikadevi at Mandu.⁵ An inscription filled with a hymn to Sarasvati has been found at Mandu.⁶ This
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-014   p. 61
King Bhoj of Malwa, in the first half of the eleventh century, composed many works on various subjects. Some of those books relate to royal living and administration. He was himself a capable king; hence the authenticity, in this regard, of the books written by him or in his time cannot be doubted. In his vast work named the *Samarangana-sutradhara*, the various forms and shapes of royal palaces are discussed in detail.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-015   p. 143   also Ch 6
King Bhoj's *Yukti-kalpataru* is a work on various subjects for the knowledge of princes. In it are briefly described those subjects that were expected, in that age, of kings and princes. This book has nearly two thousand verses.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-016   p. 146   also Ch 6
According to the works found relating to King Bhoj, he was a peerless patron of poets and artists. Five hundred scholars, poets and artists enjoyed his patronage. Among them, many authors are famous — including Dhanapala, Halayudha, Chhittapa, Amitagati, Ubbata and Purantaka. The image of Vagdevi, made by the capable sculptor Manathala patronized by him, was made in Samvat 1091 (1034 CE), which is today in the museum in London.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-017   p. 153   also Ch 6
This Gangeyadeva had humiliated and killed King Munj. According to the *Prabandha-chintamani*, when King Bhoj became king, a play of this killing was shown to him. Angered, King Bhoj attacked him and won a glorious victory over him. In the *Parijata-manjari* play — composed in the eulogy of King Bhoj's descendant Arjunavarman (1210 to 1218) — at its beginning (1/3) it is stated that, on his wish being fulfilled, King Bhojadeva held a "Gangeyadeva-bhangotsava" (festival of Gangeyadeva's rout).
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-018   p. 156
But King Bhoj conquered even him, and on the occasion of the festival of the conquest of that Gangeyadeva Telang, Bhoj erected this iron pillar, which was later, in the Muslim period, broken in an attempt to remove it from its place and take it to Ahmedabad; and the removers, it being broken, left it there. Those weight-stones were for firming the pillar in the ground; and on that platform, amid those stones, the whole 44-foot-high iron pillar had been erected. It was in fact erected on the occasion of the victory over Gangeyadeva.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-019   p. 157
According to the *Rasamala*, the Kalachuri Gangeya extended his kingdom from Nepal to Karnataka. He also had a war with Bhoj, in which he was defeated. Humiliated, he went off to Prayag, where he died in 1041 CE. In connection with this victory, Bhoj erected a victory-pillar and held a festival.
It is said that several towns' names kept being formed after Bhoj's name — Bhupal or Bhojpal (Bhopal), Bhojpur (Raisen district), Bhojpur (near Patna, after whose name the rich folk-language of that region too is named Bhojpuri), Bhopawar (near Sardarpur in Dhar district).
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-020   p. 159
King Bhoj was a supreme connoisseur of poetry. In the Vijayanagara eulogy, in praise of Krishnaraya, it is said that he was a second Bhoj, a knower of the essence of poetry, drama and rhetoric. The 13th-century Paramara Bhoj II is described in the *Hammira-mahakavya* as being like the first Bhoj — *Bhojo Bhoja ivaparah* ("Bhoj, another like Bhoj").
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-021   p. 163
King Bhoj, by his talent, power and well-ordered planning, had established a vast empire. According to the presently known references, King Bhoj's dominance extended from Kannauj, Patna and Bhojpur in the north to the Tungabhadra river in the south, and from Dwarka in the west to Magadha and Gauda (Bengal) in the east. King Bhoj had also defeated the Turushkas and shown them the way out of the country, and had given aid to other kings in this aim too.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-022   p. 165
The son named Vatsaraja (Vachchharaja) is confirmed even by an inscription. Besides these, Bhoj had two more sons. In Rajavallabha's *Bhoja-charita*, Vatsaraja and Devaraja are mentioned. In the genealogy of Kunwar Mahmud Ali, former Governor of Madhya Pradesh, a son of Bhoj named Phulkumar is stated, whose son was Jayasimha, who became Bhoj's successor. Thus Vatsaraja, Devaraja, Phulkumar, Viranarayana — these were the four sons of King Bhoj. Knowledge of all of them comes from folk-tales and folk-traditions; but of them the name Vatsaraja is confirmed by the copper-plate of 1010.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-023   p. 167
In laying out a town, a delightful region should be chosen. Of Purna, Supadma, Bhadra and Dhuma, the last is considered inauspicious. The soil should be tested; in it, shape, colour, sound, taste and so forth are examined. Only after this are the *grama*, *kheta*, *kharvata*, *puri*, *nagara*, *pattana* and so on laid out.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-026   p. 24
In a Sarvatobhadra town, Brahmanas and Kshatriyas should be settled in the east and the water-quarter. In the southeast (Agneya) direction, goldsmiths and other artisans who use fire should be settled. Vaishyas, gamblers, rogues and potters should be settled in the south; and there too actors and dancers should be settled. Swine-keepers, shepherds, hunters (those who do *mrigaya*), boatmen (*kevata*), and suppression-officers (police, *damanadhikari*) and the like should be settled in the southwest (Nairritya). People skilled in chariots or weapons should be settled in the west of the town. The abode of ascetics (*yati-sadana*), the assembly of young Brahmin students (*batuka*), the water-booth (*prapa*), the merit-hall (*punya-shala*) and the like should be in the north.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-027   p. 27
Thus, only in an auspicious town are the royal building, the assembly-hall (*sabha*), the court of justice, the horse-stable, the elephant-stable, the picture-gallery (*chitrashala*) and so on laid out, each in its proper place.
In the *Yukti-kalpataru* too there is a brief discussion of town-layout, the gist of which it will not be out of place to present here.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-028   p. 31
Thus, in Bhoj's *Samarangana-sutradhara* and *Yukti-kalpataru*, scriptural light has been cast on town-planning. In it, chief attention was given — and is still given today — to air, water, health, trade, security and the like. Between today's and ancient town-planning there has been some difference only in accordance with the age and needs; in the rest there is similarity.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-029   p. 32
Thus Dhara was Bhoj's model of town-layout, which he had settled in accordance with his own conception. According to the *Prabhavaka-charita*, Bhoj made and settled 24 separate markets or bazaars for various goods. Because of these very features, because of its well-ordered town-planning, Dhara had become a centre of attraction for visitors; it had become a tourist spot.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-030   p. 41
In the ancient settlement situated at this site of Vidisha, the aforementioned Paramara-era architectural features are found. The remains of moat and rampart, the remains of temples and various ancient structures, are still today eloquent of Vidisha's Paramara-era splendour. That era's town was, in later times, razed to the ground by invaders, but the remains still cast light on its magnificent and well-planned layout.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-031   p. 42
In the Paramara era this very settlement had become fully organized.
The name of one Paramara-era part of Vidisha is Bhailasvamidevapura. It was this that, corrupted, became the name Bhelsa. Bhailasvami was the name of a king and also of the Sun. Here there is a Paramara-era Bhailasvami temple too. A stone-inscribed hymn to the Sun of this same period is found here, whose composer was Chhittapa, the famous poet patronized by King Bhoj.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-032   p. 43; also at p. 43
From the ancient remains of Modi Pattan it is learnt that the settlement here, like Dhara, was endowed with parallel paths and crossroads. These paths ran straight north–south and east–west. This was a distinctive feature of the Paramara-era town-system.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-033   p. 49
King Bhoj himself, in his *Shringara-manjari-katha*, presented a detailed, embellished account of the peerless town of Dhara.² This account is basically traditional, yet some facts can be found there. According to Bhoj, no other town is more marvellous than Dhara, and so, before beginning the tale, its description is essential. Dhara is the finest and most wondrous town in all the world.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-034   p. 57
Thus, in ancient literature the fullest description is found of Ujjayini, Vidisha and Dhara. But in Paramara-era literature, a distinctive description is found only of Dhara — and that too in the *Shringara-manjari-katha* composed by King Bhoj himself. He conceived the ideal town in the *Samarangana-sutradhara*, himself gave that town concrete form as Dhara, and himself also described that ideal town.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-035   p. 63
These towns were temple-dominated. In Dhara there were 84 crossroads, and at each crossroad was a temple (*surasadana*). It is called *"chaturashiti-chatushpatha-surasadana-pradhana"* ("dominated by the temples at eighty-four crossroads") for this reason. This same condition prevailed elsewhere too.
Not only this — in every town there was one chief shrine. At Ujjayini, Mahakal; at Omkareshvara, Omkara-Mamaleshvara; at Dhara, the Sharadasadana or Sarasvati-kanthabharana, which today is called Bhojshala; at Mandav too there was a Sarasvati-sadana; and at Vidisha the chief was the Sun temple, which was called
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-036   p. 68
If there is a temple of Vishnu at the entrance or in the middle, it is a capital. It is called a capital only when there are gates with *gopuras* in all four directions, guard-houses, army-quarters, merchants and markets, and many temples inside and out.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-037   p. 102
**Yukti-kalpataru** — King Bhoj, in his *Samarangana-sutradhara*, has given a detailed account relating to architecture. But in his famous work on various subjects, the *Yukti-kalpataru* too, he has cast light on this subject in brief. According to it, a town should be founded at an auspicious time.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-038   p. 103
In Merutunga's *Prabandha-chintamani* too, various stories relating to Bhoj are found; many of them are considered historical. This book was written
King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 161
in the fourteenth century at Vardhamanapura (present Badnawar) in the Dhar district of Malwa. These stories, though brief, are interesting, factual, and various folk-facts relating to Bhoj.
C04-RAJABHOJ-EN-039   p. 161–162
## History of the Parmar Rajput Dynasty — Venziya, Pratapsinh Ranaji (PARMAR-RAJPUT, 10)
Based on ancient relics, inscriptions, and corroborated historical evidence, the Parmars are traditionally regarded as belonging to the Agnikula (fire-born) lineage. The narrative of their emergence from the sacrificial fire pit (yajna-kunda) performed by sage Vashishta appears consistently in inscriptions and literary traditions. According to Dr. Dasharatha Sharma, “No dynasty other than the Paramaras should be regarded as an Agnikula lineage; only the Parmars can be identified as the true Agnikula clan.” Archaeological findings from Vasantgarh, Udaipur, Nagpur, Harthal, Delwara, Patnarayan, Achaleshwar, and other sites further support this view.
C04-PARMAR-RAJPUT-001   p. 1
the Parmara dynasty is described in the “Navasahasankacharitra” composed by the contemporary poet Padmagupta during the time of Sindhuraja, the father of the Paramara king Bhoja. Kavishri Padmagupta describing in “Navasahasa Charitra” says, “Mount Arbudachala (present day Mount Abu) is similar to the pillars of the mandalas of the universe. On this holy land is the tapo land of sage Vashishtha, a royal priest of the Ishvaku dynasty.
C04-PARMAR-RAJPUT-002   p. 2
To destroy all these heathens, Rishi Vashishta by his fervor made offerings to the Agni Kund and produced the Chauhans, Pariharas, Parmaras, and Solankis (Cholukyas). But specific information is not available about these four dynasties, all four are fire dynasties. The inscriptions do not reveal the four Agni dynasty.
C04-PARMAR-RAJPUT-003   p. 2
The original residence of the Parmaras is Malwa. And the branch there is believed to be the main branch of the Parmaras. The Parmar rulers of Malwa played an important part in the historical events of ancient India. Sometimes the Paramars were called 'Malvapati' and sometimes they were honored with the title of 'Avanti Naresh'. Their capitals mainly included Ujjain and Dharnagari. As far as the boundaries of their kingdoms are concerned, the empire extends to the present-day states of Kota and Bundi in the north, parts of Bhilsa, Hoshangabad and Sagar Janapada in the east, up to the Godavari river and the territory of Khandesh in the south, and up to the Mahi river in the west.
C04-PARMAR-RAJPUT-005   p. 3
Vakpati Munj A.D. From 973 to 995, Parmar reigned supreme. Vakpati Munje was the adopted son and successor of Raja Siyak. Vakpati Munj defeated the Kalachuri ruler Yuvraj II and the Cholukya king Tailap in battle. Tailap was defeated by Vakpati Manj approximately 16 (sixteen) times. And for the seventeenth time he was captured and killed in battle.
C04-PARMAR-RAJPUT-006   p. 4
Vakpati Munj is succeeded by his younger brother Sindhuraj on the throne of the kingdom. And Sidhuraj Kumar assumes the titles of “Narayan” and “Sahasank”. Vakpati builds a lake named Munj Sagar after himself in Munje Dhar. Sindhuraj was an Indian king of the Parmara dynasty in the 10th century and father of the great Parmara ruler Bhoja. A historical account of the subject of Sindhuraja is found in the “Navasahasankachachitra” by Padmagupta. Also the information is found from the stone inscriptions of Raja Bhoja.
C04-PARMAR-RAJPUT-007   p. 5
During the Parmar reign, the Siyak started a campaign in the north-west against the Hun princes. Balavarman, the father of Yogaraja of Saurashtra, defeated the Hun prince Jajapa in battle and killed him during the battle. According to the description of “Navasahsankacharitra” Siyaka killed the prince and made a place for widows to stay. The wars with the Huns did not end until the end of the tenth century.
C04-PARMAR-RAJPUT-008   p. 5
Raja Bhoja was the ninth king of Parmar dynasty. (Mathur, 1969, p. 319) There is historical evidence that the Parmar dynasty ruled from the eighth century to the first half of the fourteenth century. Raja Bhoj fought many wars and achieved a prominent position in the glory of the Parmara dynasty. He is known to have built many temples in Malwa.
Raja Bhoj himself was a scholar but it is said that he wrote books on religion, geography, art, cell design, building construction, poetry, medicine, etc. which are still seen today. During the reign of King Bhoja (1000 to 1055 AD) he was patron of scholars and scholars. And a proverb has also become popular for him that, "Kahan Raja Bhoj Kahan Gangu Taili" Raja Bhoje wrote books on each subject which includes about 84 books.
C04-PARMAR-RAJPUT-009   p. 6
- Battle with Indraratha: According to the Udaipur Prashasti, when the Bhoja king was fighting with Solanki Jaisingh, at the same time a battle took place with a prince named Indraratha and King Bhoja won a decisive victory over Indraratha.
- War with Lat: Apart from the wars with Indraratha and Jaisinha, Lat fought over the territory and gained control over this vast region. King Bhoja's mighty army won many kingdoms.
- War with Muslims: When Raja Bhoja came to power, at the same time, Muhammad Ghazni started a campaign against India. At this time, there is evidence that Muhammad Ghazni made preparations for war with Anandpal and the Paramars fought to help Anandpal out of trouble. Thus, the war with Muslims is also seen somewhere in the pages of history.
C04-PARMAR-RAJPUT-010   p. 7
At this time the Yadavas invade Malwa from the south and the Yadavas are victorious. So now, the fall of Paramara was coming. The internal wars did not end. Then the Muslim invasions gained momentum.
At this time Devpal was busy destroying the Gujarat Empire. At that time, Muslims invaded the lower eastern border of Malwa and gained control over this part of Malwa.
C04-PARMAR-RAJPUT-011   p. 8   also Ch 10

## The Hindu Temple, Volume I — Stella Kramrisch (KRAM-1, 2)
In the ‘Samarāṅganasūtradhāra’, a treatise on architecture, the author, King Bhoja of Dhārā, who ruled over Mālava in the first part of the eleventh century, says: “He, who begins to work as an architect (sthapati) without knowing the science of architecture (vāstuśāstra) and proud with false knowledge must be put to death by the king as one who ruins the kingdom (rājahiṃsaka); dead before his time, his ghost will wander on this wide earth. He, who though well versed in the traditional science is not skilled in the work will faint at the time of action like a timid man on the battle-field. He, who is expert only in his workmanship, but unable to understand the meaning of the traditional science, will like a blind man be misled by anyone’ (S.S. XLIV. 6-10).
C04-KRAM-1-001   p. 19   also Ch 5, Ch 6
Man here is the patron or Yajamāna (lit. the sacrificer) on whose behalf the temple is built by the architect who is guided by the priest in the principles of his work. In the diagram of the Vāstupuruṣa a communication is established between man (puruṣa) as the patron of the work and the Puruṣa, the Essence of all things.
C04-KRAM-1-002   p. 18
# Chapter 5. The Udayeśvara Temple: Architecture, Sculpture and Religious Iconography
## The Udayeśvara Temple: Art, Architecture and Philosophy of the Śaiva Siddhānta — Anupa Pande (PANDE, 49)
Thus is the image of Cāmuṇḍā.'⁸⁷ The entire description, thus, paints her to be of terrible, cruel and fearsome form, one who awakens great fear and is verily like death incarnate to the enemies of the worshipper! Small wonder, then, that kings and powerful lords were great devotees of this terrible goddess.
C05-PANDE-078   p. 53
One may draw attention to the Dwarka temple of Gujarat where there is long tradition, which still continues, of hoisting flags atop the śikhara after the fulfillment of a wish. According to the temple authorities, the flags are changed five times a day and there is very long advance booking. Perhaps the flag-bearer at Udayeśvara hoisted the flag after the fulfillment of the wish of being victorious.
C05-PANDE-106   p. 93
Karaṇa is a sequence of hand and foot movements of dance culminating in a pose or stance. The Nāṭyaśāstra [chapter 4] describes 108 such karaṇas, which are depicted in the Chidambaram Temple in Tamil Nadu. A sequence of karaṇas is termed aṅgahāra [Nāṭyaśāstra, 4.29]. The 25th karaṇa is the iirdhvajānu karaṇa [Nāṭyaśāstra, 4.85]. In this karaṇa, a kuñcita foot (i.e. toe pointing downwards) in the same position is raised high up.
C05-PANDE-107   p. 93
Listen to the attributes of Cāmunḍā, the goddess who was the slayer of Caṇḍa. She is emaciated, of cruel form, with a formidable nose and sunken belly. She is tawny, her hair standing erect, dark-limbed, with red eyes, with flashing eyes and teeth and terrible nails and face.
2. She has a trident, sword and shield, bow and arrow, noose, goad, bell, mirror and thunderbolt. She has the rod, axe, conch-shell, discus, mace and club. She is to be with lifted corners of the mouth (i.e. smiling a ghostly sort of a smile) and bearing a skull garland. Cāmunḍā is seated on a corpse and has bells hanging down the shanks.
3. She wears a garment of tiger skin, ornaments of serpents, anklets with deep sound and is another (form of) death to the demons. Thus is the image of Cāmunḍā.
C05-PANDE-108   p. 191
The human body is a microcosmic parallel to the macrocosm. This was traditionally known as the parallelism of piṇḍa (body) and brahmāṇḍa (cosmos) – ‘yathā piṇḍe tathā brahmāṇḍe’. So, the whole process of tantric worship consists in exploring the body as the miniature cosmos. Various helps in this process were devised, such as the yantras or diagrams. The temple was also a three-dimensional model which exhibited the cosmic body of god (vāstupuruṣa). To understand the temple, its form and mystery, it is therefore necessary to understand the philosophy on which it rests, that is, not simply the architectonics of the Śilpaśāstras but also the philosophy of the Āgamaśāstras.
C05-PANDE-118   p. 99
The system believes in thirty-six tattvas of which twenty-five are the same as in Sāṃkhya. Beyond them, eleven more tattvas are added. These are Śiva, Śakti, Sadāśiva,
THE METAPHYSICS OF ŚAIVISM AND ŚAIVA SIDDHĀNTA
Īśvara, Sadvidyā* and the six *kañcukas*.
C05-PANDE-119   p. 100–101
The first five *tattvas* constitute the pure order, i.e. the *śuddha* *addhvā*. The rest constitute the impure order.
C05-PANDE-120   p. 101
The Śaiva Siddhānta system postulates thirty-six *tattvas* or basic constituents of reality. Out of these twenty-five are broadly similar to the division found in Sāṃkhya, though with some differences, especially in their ontological and cosmological status. To these are added eleven more; six being the subtle causes of the created world and five being transcendent and eternal. These five are called the *śuddha addhvā* (pure path). All the *tattvas* here are timeless aspects of the same eternal reality which is Śiva. It is called pure path because the soul in its process of emancipation moves up from the impure path of creation to the pure path of eternity.
C05-PANDE-123   p. 104
The three basic constituents of Śaiva Siddhānta are *pati*, *paśu* and *pāśa*. The Śaiva Siddhāntis make an ontological difference between *pati* (god), *paśu* (individual soul) and *pāśa* (insentient elements that act as fetters which bind the soul). *Pati* is pure consciousness endowed with power and bliss. He is one without a second, the creator and lord of all.
C05-PANDE-124   p. 104
The first five pure principles are Śiva, Śakti, Sadāśiva, Īśvara and Vidyā. They are different moments of infinite consciousness having infinite knowledge and will, *jñānaśakti* and *kriyāśakti*. This is eternal, though we can intellectually distinguish different moments in it. Śiva is the perennial source of all the *tattvas*. He is the nature of infinite knowledge, power and bliss. He is one without a second, the sole cause of the universe, the lord of the destiny of the souls and the supreme savior. Śiva and Śakti are distinct but not entitatively or qualitatively different. Śiva and Śakti are essentially one but conceptually distinguished as 'Being' and 'Energy'. Śakti is the power or the energy of Śiva, conceived as functioning by his nature. Sadāśiva is the lord with a dominant inward consciousness in which the universe to be created is reflected potentially, as in a mirror. Sadāśiva is also the source of the five *āmanāyas*, and thus the eternal source of grace as the eternal teacher. Īśvara is the lord as creator of the universe and the controller of the destiny of the *jīva*. Sadvidyā is the nature of objectified consciousness which contains the seed of all things.
C05-PANDE-125   p. 104
Verse 2: That which is not born, that which is not destroyed, that which does not obtain salvation (itself) but bestows salvation, (such) is Śiva, who is the nature of both consciousness and will.
Note: The nature and power of Śiva is constituted by both knowledge and action (jñānakriyāsvabhāva). He creates a real world. We have here a dualistic and realistic picture.
Verse 3: I bow (to that power), Śakti, which is unequalled, foremost, conscious and which is the instrument by which Śiva is able to bestow pleasure and salvation to all beings.
Note: Śiva and Śakti are distinguishable but not separable.
C05-PANDE-126   p. 118
The earliest textual reference to such figures is seen in the Viṣṇudharmottara Mahāpurāṇa in the context of the rendition of decorative motifs such as palm leaf, makara etc.⁵ It states that the form of makara should be made akin to that of a crocodile. Further, there is a cryptic reference to figures atop makaras as well. These are described as on top of makara and as “acyutaḥ”, i.e. stable and not falling.
C05-PANDE-127   p. 16
From the 10th to the 12th century, the syncretic images of the trinity, i.e. Brahmā, Viṣṇu and Maheśa along with Sūrya had become very popular in western and central India; they are described in the Śilpasāstra texts of Rajasthan and Gujarat of this period as Hariharahiranyagarbha or Hariharārkapitāmaha. The Aparājitapṛcchā of Gujarat describes the Hariharahiranyagarbha image saying that the four gods should be amalgamated into one and have four faces, eight hands and should stand straight and erect.¹⁷ The front face should be that of Sūrya holding lotuses, the hands of Rudra should be made to his right holding a khaṭvāṅga (skull-topped mace) and trident, Brahmā at the back should hold a rosary (akṣasūtra) and water-pot (kamaṇḍalu), Viṣṇu, on the left, should hold a conch and a discus.

## The Hindu Temple, Volume II — Stella Kramrisch (KRAM-2, 91)
The Hindu temple is a monument, whose outer surface consists of sculptures. The Mandapas, the various halls, preceding the Prāsāda itself in which is contained the innermost sanctuary, the Garbhagrha, are architecture in the accepted meaning. The Prāsāda itself however is almost completely a solid mass but for the Garbhagrha, which is a small, dark, cubical chamber even in the largest temples. But for the main image or imageless symbol enshrined within, the walls of the Garbhagrha, as a rule, are plain. The carvings cease at its door; there they confront the devotee for the last time as he approaches the innermost sanctuary; he may not himself, as in the temples of South India proceed further, the officiating priest performing the rites for him.
C05-KRAM-2-001   p. 12
The temple has no façade; it faces the four directions. Its walls however are not four surfaces meeting at right angles. Each wall projects with a number of offsets, the main pier being in the centre. The buttresses vary in thickness; in the majority of temples built throughout India (except in the South) the offsets are moreover frequently superimposed resulting in a stepped, cross-shaped plan. Whereas the plain walls of the dark Garbhagra form a square in plan, the carved walls of the exterior of the temple, exposed to the light of the sun, the moon and the stars, form an indented plan. The plan of the centre of the temple, the square, is translated by its walls into a perimeter of variedly stepped and indented design which even may be 'star shaped'.
C05-KRAM-2-002   p. 13
Facing the entrance, outside the Prāsāda, in the open (Pls. LVIII, XXXVI), or in the Mandapa (Pl. LII), is the image of the Vāhana, the vehicle of the divinity which is enshrined in the temple. Carved in the round, self-contained as form and symbol, the Vāhana ‘conveys’ the deity. The theriomorphic image, the Vāhana, is, it appears, as ancient as any temple; \( ^{48} \) while its position is outside the entrance, it confronts the temple or the image in the Garbhagrha with which it is coaxial. No other sculpture or part of the building is so placed. The Vāhana is a counterpart of the image enshrined in the Prāsāda and framed by the door-way. It leads to the entrance.
C05-KRAM-2-003   p. 30
The temple stands on a high platform; four small shrines in the corners (cf. plan of the Laksmāna temple, built in 954 A.D.; p. 255) which made it a Pācāyatana temple, no longer exist.
C05-KRAM-2-004   p. 78
It is a Sāndhāra Prāsāda whose Pradakṣiṇa or ambulatory is extended in the Mahāmaṇḍapa; two pillars demarcate the Antarāla (cf. the Citragupta Temple, Pl. V, which also has two corresponding pillars flanking the entrance to the Garbhagṛha); the central square (vedi) of the Mahāmaṇḍapa is co-extensive with the square of the Kandabhitti (SS. LVII. 491), the walls of the Prāsāda,—the distance between the central square of the Maṇḍapa moreover being equal in the Lakṣmaṇa temple, to the side of the Prāsāda. Prāsāda and Mahāmaṇḍapa are contracted as one walled-in building to which are attached the Maṇḍapa and Ardha (half) Maṇḍapa as half-open, pillared hall and porch.
C05-KRAM-2-005   p. 79
I); in dark openings below projecting rooflets (chādya) it stretches along its length, within its body, a band of measured compartments which contradicts the volume of piers and Sringas and bisects the building; below is the pile of masonry raised on its terrace (Jagatipitha; S.S. LVI. 124, gives its height as half of the width of the Prāsāda).
C05-KRAM-2-006   p. 80
256); in the ground plan it is the incision which separates, and at the same time connects, Mandapa and Prāsāda. Here it has a superstructure of its own, a keel vaulted body which juts out from the Śikhara. The sides are encased in a five storeyed mansion (Pl. XLIX) in which the image of Śiva is displayed in many of its forms and flanked by attendant divinities.
C05-KRAM-2-010   p. 107
The Āmalaka is the crown of a Nāgara temple. Above the truncated body of its Šikhara it clasps the shaft (venu; Pl. I) \( ^{161} \) and is surmounted by the finial (stūpikā) with the vase (kalasā) as its most conspicuous part. The Āmalaka has a cogged rim. Its solid shape is that of a ring stone; it may be compared to an indented wheel, the spokes being shown as ribs of its ring shape (Pl. I).
C05-KRAM-2-013   p. 61
\( ^{161} \)  The earliest yet known crowning Āmalaka of a structural temple has been unearthed in fragments at Nāgari, Chitor, Rajputana. Mem., ASI, No. 4, p. 126. It belongs to the fifth century A.D.
C05-KRAM-2-014   p. 61
The Kandariya temple measures 102' 3" in length, 66' 10" in width and is 101' 9" high (B. L. Dhama, l.c., p. 9).
C05-KRAM-2-015   p. 78
The Sukanāsā is the last halting point (Pls. I, XLVIII, XLIX); its articulation is similar to that of the superstructures of the Mandapa and different from the Sīkhara of the Prāsāda of which architecturally it forms part. So close do the Mandapas come to the Prāsāda but here they stop; and none may exceed the height of the Sukanāsā. From there the Mūlamānjari, in an elastic curve carries its Uromānjaris and Sṛngas towards its high finial.
C05-KRAM-2-016   p. 80
The rhythmical compactness of the Šikhara and the dwindling of sculptured form into the texture of the monumental shape are brought about by the order of Uromanjāris, four in each of the main directions, the lower clinging to the 'chest' (uras) of the higher. They are flanked by Karṇaṅnajāris in parallel horizontal rows, three Karṇaśrīngas on the two lower and one in the third row, fill the corners from the Uromanjāris in the East to those in the South. Intervening between Uromanjāris and Karṇaśrīngas are Naṣṭaśrīngas (Khaṇdarekha) or 'hidden' small Sikharas, whose edge (koṇa) and Āmalaka are to be seen whereas the curvature of their sides is hidden and oversected by the adjacent Karṇaśrīngas. Four such Naṣṭaśrīngas belong to each of the two lower rows in each sub-quarter of the Sīkhara.
C05-KRAM-2-017   p. 84
The Mūlamānjari (the 'root' or central, main Sīkhara) is a Latārekha of the Saptaratha variety; its curve is steeper than that drawn by 'sadguna sūtra'; each of its four sides has seven facets ('pāga' in Orissan terminology) including the corner portion (koṇaka-pāga) which itself has one more minor central offset; the subordinate Śrīgas, alike in their 'structure', however have but five buttresses on each side (pañcaratha).
C05-KRAM-2-018   p. 84
With the wings of the wooden door opened, during Pūjā, the image is seen by the devotee in the middle of the door; the frame of the door is also that of the image; the distance between door and image is translated into colour and atmosphere. Having the image for its centre and subject, this picture is framed by the door-way with its carvings on sill, jambs, and lintel. The several parts of the door-way as given in the 'Bṛhatsamhītā' form a geometrical progression; the width of the threshold being equal to that of the door-jambs, the width of the door-way or entrance is double of each; and its height is twice the width. Similarly also are the parts of the door-way proportioned, assigned as they are to various images and carvings. At the bottom are the large figures of the Guardian divinities of the door, etc.; they occupy ¼ of the height of the door.
C05-KRAM-2-025   p. 26
The name for the antepagments of the door-jamb is ‘śākhā’, meaning ‘branch’. A number of branches were fixed in the ground, tied and bundled together originally; 3, 5, 7, or 9 contribute their stems to the varied vertical mouldings of the door-jambs (Br.
C05-KRAM-2-026   p. 26
Carved on the centre of the threshold ('udumbara'; 'bhuvangama') is a long stemmed full blown lotus, symbol of this universe in which divinity is established, symbol also of the state of dispassion of the Bhakta in which divinity is revealed to him. None may tread on it. \( ^{36} \)  It is the mark of the threshold, above it, the image in the Garbhagrha appears raised to the Bhakta who approaches it; and, while he or the priest is about to enter the innermost sanctuary he too is raised to the status of divinity.
C05-KRAM-2-027   p. 27
Most conspicuous and significant are the large images of the River-goddesses, often accompanied by their retinues, their figures being set against the several Sākhās. Above their groups are carved and repeated in many panels such shapes and configurations, in which life is young and quick; procreative couples on the posts, and baby-shapes of Gānas (“quantities” of celestials) amidst creepers rambling around the door-way. Serpents are interlaced on the door-way of Viṣṇu temples, especially.
C05-KRAM-2-028   p. 27
The door-way is an iconostasis of the descent of the Rivers, of Śakti; and of the ascent of life competing for its heavenly origin in the creepers rambling upwards on the ‘branches’ (śākhā) of the frame, in the multiform concatenations within their stalks and, on each single Śākhā, in the sequences of lovers ('mithuna'), prancing chimaerae (śārdūla) and jubilant spirits (gaṇa) (cf.
C05-KRAM-2-029   p. 28
The closed body of the temple is full of openings not factually but symbolically. As a rule there is only one opening, that of the entrance; in effect and in symbol it is repeated at the cardinal points, as a 'massive door' (ghana-dvāra) or a rectangular niche; in addition there are many niches of varying sizes in the wall, socle and the superstructure of the temple. The major niches hold images which are directly connected with the main divinity of the temple, the smaller ones, which occupy less important positions, on the socle, in any direction, housing lesser divinities. Being thus enshrined in a rectangular frame, the image is singled out, its importance being increased in that it appears manifested straight from the centre. Across the massive door its presence shines forth. The Ghanadvāras possess this primary symbolic function by virtue of their original position as doors in the cardinal points.
C05-KRAM-2-030   p. 31
In its largest form as exemplified on the Hindu temple, the Gavakşa is an antefix known as Šukanāsā; it shields the structural opening of the Šikhara or superstructure (Pls. XLVII-VIII); there it has, on the temple, its original and leading place. It may however be repeated on a lesser scale in the three other main directions, in front of the central buttress. In South India it is placed at each cardinal point of the Sikhara or dome shape of the 'High Temple'. In its particular Drāvidian version beset with flames it blazes forth from the many chapels on each Bhūmi; varied in size and the elaboration of its details.
Such Nāsās (Nāsikā; Kudu) are also repeated horizontally on the Kapotālis or cornices; there they had their place, originally, as attic windows, in which form they have figured since the days of Barhut. Thus the Gavāksa is originally a dormer or gable window and it retains as its outline the shape of the arch of vegetation, the shape of Prakrti. \( ^{54} \)
On Nāgara temples, the offsets of the Sīkhara are each enmeshed and covered by an unending web of Gavāksas; \( ^{55} \)  where the Sīkhara consists of offsets only it appears shrouded in Gavāksa-lace; so are the Śrīngas and Tilakas; on some temples
C05-KRAM-2-031   p. 32
Pre-eminent amongst them is the Face of Glory, the Kirttimukha. Its essential place is at the apex of the Gavakşa. It figures most prominently on the largest Gavakşa of Nāgara temples, called Šukanāśā, the antefix of the open arch of the Šikhara (Pls. XLVII, XLVIII, L.). \( ^{67} \)  On Hastiprṣtha temples it looks over the apex of the arch of the façade (Kapotēśvara Temple in Chezarla, Guntur).

On the main surfaces of the offsets (ratha) of the Śrīgas, the tiers are overspun by the carved Gavākṣas, a tight fitting sheath, as if of lace, with the round eyelets of the windows and the scrolls of tracery surrounding them in several patterns (jāla; cf.
C05-KRAM-2-037   p. 85
The main parts of the surround of the door are: the high threshold; the lower part of the jambs with the large images; the upper part of the antepagments with the compartments of small figures, the lintel and the overdoor. The opening of the door is equal in width to each of the jambs and this being equal to the height of the large figure groups at the bottom of the door, the height of the door is twice its width.
C05-KRAM-2-038   p. 85
The Vedi is here the vertical portion of the wall, below the lotus petal edge; it corresponds in its position to the 'jānghā' of the wall (see Pl. I), i.e., to its lowermost zone.
The Vedi consists here of carved posts; they rise from a slightly recessed necking (rājasenā), itself divided by uprights into panels filled with deep shadows, figures and rosettes.
C05-KRAM-2-039   p. 88
devatās, the main aspects of the divinity whose image or symbol is enshrined in the Garbhagrha. Placed within the body of the wall, within a niche or “massive door” (ghanadvāra) as it is called, on the co-ordinates of the Prāsāda these main images are nearer to their centre which is also that of the entire monument, the temple. The surrounding images (āvarana-devatās) are however more exposed; carved, as they sometimes are, almost completely in the round, they are yet of one piece of stone with the surfaces in ressault and remain connected with them by the strut-like extensions of their modelled limbs at angles not meant for view.
The carved figures on the surface of the temple are thus at different distances from the centre. They are part of it, not only ritually and iconographically but also in their position in space. They appear projected from the centre through the thickness of the wall or they are embedded in it as it steps forth.
C05-KRAM-2-046   p. 14
In belts around the body of the temple, and in vertical sequences where one image is superimposed on the other, the figures are part of the space-body of which the outside of the monument, the Prāsāda, consists. They do not form groups except such as are unavoidable by their juxtaposition on symmetrical and repeated offsets and recesses. "They do not enter into any 'composition' which would extend farther than the single buttress or beyond the console on which the image is placed (Pls. III, XLIV, LXXI). If more than one figure are part of the image they are carved in front of a single surface, they are collateral, auxiliary to its meaning and complete its form.
C05-KRAM-2-047   p. 15
The main aspects, for example, of the God in the temple such as certain Avatars, in a Visnu temple; or the divinities most closely related to Siva, if the temple is consecrated to him; or the images of the three great gods, Brahma, Visnu and Siva, are placed in 'massive doors' in three of the cardinal points, whereas the entrance itself, generally facing East, frequently has a small image of the main divinity carved at the centre of the lintel. \( ^{7} \)  It abounds also in other carvings \( ^{8} \)  all linked up as one great composition. Over and above, it frames the main image in the Garbhagrha. \( ^{9} \)
Facing the cardinal points are the ‘doors’ where the image of God shows forth. On the actual entrance especially, the small image on the lintel is similar in position to the image of Christ carved in the tympanum above the entrance and is akin to His words: “I am the Door”.
Apart from the main images, in their niches, and indispensable to all temples are the images of the Aṣṭadikpālas, the Guardians of the Eight points of space, each in its correct location. The multitude of divine figures stationed between these two kinds of essential images, each on a facet and having a console of its own, are Nāgas, Sārdūlas, Apsarās, Surasundaris, Mithunas, etc. (Pls. XLI, XL, XI, XXXIII), and certain specific images of the lesser gods.
Each such type of the ‘surrounding divinities’ is repeated in many variations of posture and movement on the walls of the temple; like the chorus in an Indian Yātrā performance announcing the particular passage of the play not once only, but by repetition in the four directions of space.
C05-KRAM-2-048   p. 17
Ends of garments flutter to enhance their movements and be a foil to their rhythms; whereas folded scarves clasp their fullness and are carved as if they were a special type of jewellery, accentuating through the contrast of their own shape and texture that of the modelled body.
Garments, jewellery and coiffure of the images are a selection and enhancement of those worn in the respective country where the temple was built. The preference of the sculptor however is for the bare body and he makes sparing use only of garments; worn in the shape of a 'dhoti', the cloth clings to the body and is recognisable, as a rule, only by such patterns as are engraved on the modelled shapes of the limbs (Pls. XVIII, XIX, XXIII, XXXIII); and by carving the hem of the cloth, petal-like (Pl. XXX) or as if it were one more ornament of the smooth limbs (Pls. XXI, LXII). Thus all forms of apparel accentuate and accompany the smooth roundness of the figures and their movement; only the headgear and coiffure are additional volumes of sculptural consequence; the high crowns (mukuta) of the greater gods, add height to the image (Pls.
C05-KRAM-2-049   p. 19
It carries them, sustains them in the most exacting contortions, bears them aloft when they are represented as flying and adds power, breadth and dignity to their stance when they stand firmly planted on both feet (samapādasthānaka). This erect stance with the weight of the body evenly distributed, right and left, is classified as 'samabhaṅga' or the even bend. It is the first variety in the classification of stances, they are classified in the surface, as the even bend, the slight bend (abhanga), the triple bend (tribhaṅga) and the excessive (triple) bend (atibhaṅga).
C05-KRAM-2-050   p. 20
\( ^{40} \)  This then is the celestial region whence the rivers have descended, Gāngā, the most sacred of all, celestial Mandākini, and also Yamunā. From heaven they have come to earth; in the later temples their images are stationed on either side, at the bottom of the door, the current and ripples of flowing waters are in their swaying stances. To look at them is equal in effect to the ritual bath in their waters, especially in the most sacred water of the Ganges. The energy of the waters is so great that the bath itself confers Dīksā, initiation ('Maitr. S.' III. 6.2): Ablution, transmutation, and initiation are effected at the entrance.
C05-KRAM-2-051   p. 28
The guardians of the threshold thus are the most enduring images of the door \( ^{44} \) where perils must be warded off and contamination with the impurities of the world prevented. They bear the weapon and traits of the particular god whom they serve in each temple and they frequently exhibit his ‘fearful’ aspect. \( ^{45} \) Thus they are the agents of the image in the Garbhagrha, and of its smaller version in the centre of the lintel.
C05-KRAM-2-052   p. 29
It usually has the mien of a lion; and is therefore also known as Simha-mukha, the Lion's face. It is horned; the frown on its forehead (simha-lalāta) is gathered in a third and middle horn between the two stag-or ram horn like extensions of the bulge of the eye globes.
C05-KRAM-2-053   p. 36
The Face of Glory on the Hindu Temple has its essential position on the apex of the Sukanāśā, at a definite proportionate height of the superstructure; above it practically no images are carved on the trunk of the Sīkhara of a Nāgara temple; from it downwards extends the realm of figured shapes (Pl. XLIII-IV, XLVIII).
The Kirttinukha is a mask made up of the face of the Lion (Simhamukha), of Death's head (Kāla) and the Dragon's head (Rāhu, Tamas).
C05-KRAM-2-054   p. 39
One legend, similarly recounted in the 'Padmapurāna' (Uttarakhanda, X. 10 f. XI. 36-44) and the 'Skandapurāna' (Visnukhanda, Kārtikamāsa Mahātmya, XVII) narrates how Rāhu as messenger of Jālandhara, the Asura, demanded Pārvati from Śiva whom he was about to wed. Śiva thereupon produced a terrible being from his third eye. Lion-faced, with lolling tongue, the eyes like lightning, hair on end, looking like another Narasimha it rushes at Rāhu; but Śiva stops it and bids it to devour itself. This is does leaving only its head, the Kīrtimukha.
Śiva thus, from his wisdom-eye sees and produces a reflex of Rāhu which commits the act of self-sacrifice.
C05-KRAM-2-055   p. 43
Vāstu is laid to rest at the base of the Temple and is its 'plan'; Rāhu's head is fixed above, on the pinnacle of the Śukanāsā of the Sīkhara; below it is the realm of images and appearance. Rāhu's head is also on the apex of gates; it is repeated moreover on Gavāksa emblems throughout the superstructure, on all the Bhūmis of South Indian temples, and on the cornices of the walls. There it is carved in the fullness of its meaning, as the Head; and Prakrti, the arch below.

C05-KRAM-2-056   p. 43
Šārdūla is also the name of an animal shape ‘made by art’. (cf. kṛtrima-grāsa; ‘Samarānɡanāsūtradhāra’, LVII. 777). It is also known as Virāla in Orissa \( ^{10s} \) or Vyāla (S.S. LVII. 643), particularly in South India (‘Mayamata’, XVIII. 77). Šārdūla means a tiger, leopard, panther; a demon, a kind of bird, or the animal Šarabha which is stronger than a lion, has 8 legs and of which there is no likeness on earth.
In Indian sculpture, tiger and lion are interchangeable shapes and denote the foremost amongst all animals. \( ^{104} \)  The Sārdūla is also called Simha-virāla, if its head is that of a lion; Nara-virāla if the head is that of man \( ^{105} \)  and Gaja-virāla if it has the head of an elephant. It is represented rampant, but also in other postures. Endowed with the shape of the Lion, is the Simha, the group of the animal and the man which is carved in the round, at the base of the temple \( ^{106} \)  (Pl. II) and also on the ridge of the Šukanāsā; \( ^{107} \)  tiger, lion, boar, ram, bird and elephant combine where it rears, fearsome and playful, in relief on the walls of the temple, \( ^{108} \)  in the recesses between piers (Pl. III).
C05-KRAM-2-057   p. 46
\( ^{108} \)  The Lion and the Sārdūla appear at different periods, in the various schools, in different parts of the sacred architecture: the lion figures as lowermost section of the pillar of Pallava temples, as bracket in the Čalikyan buildings, lion or Sārdūla are brackets of capitals in the Maṇḍapas of Candela temples in Central India, etc; on the wall, in procession in friczes on the base of Hoysala temples, etc. etc.
Sārdūla and Makara are hypostases of parts of the Krittimukha, i.e., the Lion and the Dragon, but they each have a body being fully 'animal'.
C05-KRAM-2-058   p. 46
This protean animal made by art, has always the body of a lion, and in its more important versions, also its face. It is a symbolic shape and corresponds, within manifestation, to the Face of Glory, the Mask of the Asura. \( ^{109} \) .
The original position of the Lion shape is in the corners of the Uttara Vedi, the High Altar. Similarly too, the Lions have their places in the corners of the Throne of the Supreme Spirit (Paramātman) or of Sūrya, \( ^{110} \) the Sun-god, and of the Buddha. The Sīnghāsana, the lion throne, is the figured equivalent of the Uttara Vedi. Thus, on the temple, the lions are not only at the bottom, on the Vedi, but also on top, on the Vedi of the Sīkhara, where they support the stainless Āmalaka; they are also carved below the section of the Sīkhara called Vedi, where the Lion (called Udā-simha, the 'flying' lion, in Orissa) springs on a corbel from its body.
C05-KRAM-2-059   p. 47
- The group of the Lion and man is carved in the round and placed in front of the body of the temple. It shows man as he approaches the Centre, under the threat of the Lion; man draws the sword of Knowledge and protects himself from Māyā with the shield of dispassion. On the walls of the temple and the pillars of its halls, man rides the monster, Sārdūla, whose prancing gallop has the rhythm of Nature and inspiration.\(^{120}\) Hers is the fiery splendour of creative thought;\(^{121}\) her shape is rhythm itself. This noble beast embodies at the same time the lordly power of the wild animals; and represents the Lordly power (rājanya) in general; man, who rides it, copes with it, is particularly the Kṣatriya, the warrior, the royal knight;\(^{122}\) it is his body of Fame (yaśāssārīram), his royal Splendour.\(^{123}\)
C05-KRAM-2-060   p. 49
The most exalted temple-type, the Meru, must be built by a Ksatriya only as its patron, the architect being a Brāhmaṇa or a Vaiśya. \( ^{124} \)  The royal patron is most closely identified with his temple by the symbol of the Sārdūla. On the walls of the temples of the Candella dynasty, in Central India, images of the Sārdūla alternate with images of the Apsarās and other goddesses (Pl. III).
The Sārdūla, an animal of perfervid ingenuity, composed of rhythm, is an embodiment per artem of the Lion, the Lordly Power of the wild beasts, who is Prakrti, Sakti, Māyā.
C05-KRAM-2-061   p. 50
Surasundari, which means Celestial Beauty, \( ^{125} \) is but one of the names and types in which the image of Sakti is carved on the walls of the temple. Sakti is Energy, the primordial Power and substance of the world. Māyā,—the visible world, measurable in its forms,—belongs to Brahman as Sakti ('Vedānta Sūtra' I. IV, 1-7). She is herself the "ability to act"; \( ^{126} \) her image is placed next to that of any of the gods. By her activity she attracts and helps the devotee; she is his guide and appeals to all. While every man is not equipped for riding the Sārdūla, he may be led by Sakti and grasp the meaning of her hands (hasta), \( ^{127} \) postures and actions. While the Sārdūla is the 'angel' of active man, the various images of Sakti assist his contemplative and passive nature. The images of Sārdūla (Vyāla), and Sakti are collateral; they alternate on the walls of the temples of Khajuraho and on the capitals of the pillars (Pls. III, XVIII).
C05-KRAM-2-062   p. 51
The Celestial Beauties belong to the Āvaraṇa-devatās, “surrounding divinities”. Their number is large. Their figures are repeated on the temples, all round the walls, to either side of each god, and as on the Kandariya and Devi Jagadambā Temples, in all the three belts of sculpture.
C05-KRAM-2-063   p. 52
On the walls of the temples they are figured in all directions as Celestial Beauties. They perform this work by the side of the gods whose activity their presence releases.
C05-KRAM-2-064   p. 52
Other names and forms, other image of Sakti are: Apsarā, \( ^{133} \)  Nātakā, \( ^{134} \)  Yakṣini \( ^{135} \)  and Dig Devi. \( ^{136} \)  Being active power and causal stress, her image is an embodiment of movement; as Apsarā she is the movement in the atmosphere, as Yakṣini she is the movement in vegetation, as Nātakā she is the movement in the body of man and is shown as a dancer.
C05-KRAM-2-065   p. 53

The Śalabhañjikā is frequently carved on the brackets of capitals or forms the bracket of the Ucchālaka (Pls. XVIII, LX).
C05-KRAM-2-066   p. 53

C05-KRAM-2-067   p. 53
Breath, the vital manifestation of the Spirit, is the support and prime mover of all action; it is shown having formed a body of its own which is particularly that of the Ganas (Pls. LXV, XXIV, etc.). They are mere quantities (gana = quantity), look like windbags (Pl. LXV), hosts of them. Their bodies inflated by air race in gusts across the atmosphere, support the Vimānas, the chariots of the gods (Pl. XXIV), and produce the music out of the sound which is immanent in the ether and in the air. Full of it, they play it on different instruments and make it as articulate as their air-borne, air-filled bodies are made by the sculptor. They—and not caryatids—carry the superstructure and walls of the temple (Pls. LIII, LXVI, XXXIX).

the course of thousands of years, \( ^{150} \) in procession, or in couples confronting each other. Where, as in Pl. As form (rūpam) and figure (ākṛti) of a verbal symbol, the Hamsa bird carved on the temples means that union towards which fly the hosts of celestials, the Gānas, Vidyātharas and Gandharvas and whose dancers are the Apsarās. They are given form, are bodied forth by art as images of the power and the movement of the Breath and the Spirit.

Gods and ascetics therefore should be represented in their love sport (kridā, līlā) on the walls of temples \( ^{158} \) but ascetics practising the game of love should not figure on the habitations of men \( ^{159} \) for their game is none of the three purposes of life. \( ^{160} \) It is a symbol of final release, its fourth and ultimate purpose.
