## कथा उदयपुर (Jagta Hua Kasba) — Hindi original (JAGTA-HI, 3)
भोज के समय इनके नाम थे-सरस्वती कंठाभरण। इस नाम के दो ग्रंथ भी उन्होंने लिखे थे और यह उनकी उपाधि भी थी।
*Gloss [my translation]:* In Bhoja's time their names were Sarasvati Kanthabharana. He wrote two books of this name, and it was also his title.
C06-JAGTA-HI-040   p. 65
परमारों के समय बने सरस्वती कंठाभरण तो अपने समय के बेहतरीन नॉलेज सेंटर ही थे, जहां सभी तरह के विषयों को पढ़ाने की परंपरा थी। सदियों तक यह ज्ञान परंपरा कायम रही। हमें आज भी दस्तावेजों से यह पता चलता है कि मंडप दुर्ग (वर्तमान मांडू) के सरस्वती कंठाभरण से संबंधित छात्रावास में कोई गोविंद भट्ट नाम का एक प्रभारी था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* The Sarasvati Kanthabharana built in the Paramara period were excellent knowledge centres of their time, where there was a tradition of teaching all subjects. This tradition of knowledge continued for centuries. Even today documents tell us that a person named Govinda Bhatta was in charge of the hostel attached to the Sarasvati Kanthabharana of Mandapa Durga (present Mandu).
C06-JAGTA-HI-041   p. 145
उपस्थिति को दर्शाती थी। इन पांच वाद्यों में श्रृंग(सींग), तमट(खँड़ड़ी), शंख, भेरी
और जयघंटा शामिल थे। राजा, राजपरिवार अथवा राजा की विशेष कृपा से जिन
अधिकारियों को भी यह सम्मान दिया जाए उनके सम्मुख ही इन्हें बजाने की परंपरा
थी।
*Gloss [my translation]:* The tradition of playing five instruments by the royal band showed the king's presence in the royal court. These five instruments included the shringa (horn), tamata (a drum), conch, bheri and jayaghanta. The tradition was to play them in front of the king, the royal family or any officials given this honour by the king's special favour.
C06-JAGTA-HI-044   p. 148
## History of the Paramāra Dynasty — Ganguly, D. C. (GANGULY, 33)
There is an iron pillar at Dhar, the date of erection of which cannot be definitely ascertained. \( ^{2} \)  It may be the work of Vairisimha II. The Udayapur prasasti \( ^{3} \)  attributes to him the erection of a number of pillars of victory.
C06-GANGULY-024   p. 283
Another inscription, of Udayâditya's reign (i. e. Udayavarman), dated Saka 1103—1186 A. D., has been found inserted in the wall of this temple.\( ^{2} \)
C06-GANGULY-033   p. 281
25 Udayapur inscription of Kumā- I. A., Vol XVIII, rapāla, 1163 A. D. p. 343.
C06-GANGULY-034   p. 367
On the northern wall of the passage are engraved the inscription of Udayâditya and a "Sarpa-bandha" (intertwined snakes), similar to that on Bhoja's school at Dhârâ.
C06-GANGULY-039   p. 273
Two slabs of stone, incised in the walls of this building, contain inscriptions of the reigns of Bhoja and Arjunaverman. Besides these, inscriptions of the reigns of Udayâditya and Naravarman, and a chart containing the Sanskrit alphabet and rules of grammar, have also been discovered here. The modern structure is quite Muhammadan in style, \( ^{2} \) and seems to have been constructed with the materials of a Hidu temple which stood on the same site.
C06-GANGULY-040   p. 281
The second chart is made up by the intertwining of two serpents. Its object is to record some chief rules of Sanskrit “Kâ-tantra” grammar. Personal terminations of ten tenses, moods and three numbers are described. There are also two sets of terminations, viz, Parasmaipada and Atmanepada, for each tense and mood.
Both these records were certainly set up there for the guidance of the pupils. A similar mode of teaching adopted all over the country, and charts of exactly the same type are also found in the temple of Mahākāla at Ujjain,\(^{3}\) and in a fane at Un.\(^{4}\) Through the strenuous
C06-GANGULY-041   p. 291
The modern Kamalmauka mosque at Dhârâ, as we have already noticed, is believed to have been a school building erected by Bhoja. The tradition is strongly corroborated by the discovery of two charts on the walls of that building, which contain alphabets and rules of grammar. \( ^{2} \)  They are very important, inasmuch as they give us an idea of the method of teaching that was pursued in those ancient days.
C06-GANGULY-042   p. 291
The first is sketched out by the windings of a single serpent. On its body is engraved the Sanskrit alphabet in the Nâgari characters of the eleventh and twelfth centuries A. D. The tail contains the inflectional terminations of nouns and verbs. There are altogether fifty-three letters and symbols, and twenty-one nominal and eighteen verbal inflectional terminations in it.
C06-GANGULY-043   p. 291
The great poet Padmagupta was a contemporary of both the kings, Vākpati-Muñja (A. D. 972-995) and his successor Sindhurāja.
C06-GANGULY-044   p. 15
He was himself a great poet, and granted without stint enormous bounties to those who devoted their lives to literary work. By his liberal patronage, unvarying devotion to the goddess of learning, and rich contributions, he brought about a renaissance of Sanskrit literature in Malwa. Padmagupta was his chief court-poet \( ^{1} \) ; Dhanānjaya, Bhattā Halāyudha, Dhanika, Dhanapāla, Šobhana, and other poets \( ^{2} \) flourished under his beneficent patronage.
He excavated many beautiful tanks in Malwa, one of which, situated at Dhârâ, is still called after his name Muñjasâgara. He is also said to have built many temples and embankments at Ujjain, Maheshwar, Onker-Mândhâtâ and Dharmapuri.
C06-GANGULY-045   p. 77   also Ch 4
As regards Navasâhasânka-carita, we have also sufficient reason to believe that it represents a solid historical fact in the garb of a romantic story. The poet expressly tells us that the object of his narrative is to record the life-story of Sindhurâja, which he has undertaken, not from motives of poetic pride, but at the command of his master. That the book has something of an historical character, and is not purely fantastic panegyric, is further proved by the fact that the death of the minister Ramângada at the hands of the demon-prince is mentioned in the narrative, though it could safely have been omitted without any material injury to the plot. Professor Bühler remarks that “the story which Padmagupta relates in his Navasâhasânka-carita with the peculiar breadth of the Mahâkâvyas, has, without doubt, a historical background. Not only did the hero of the poem, king Sindhurâja, really exist, the other people too, who appear in the poem as Nâgas, Vidyâ-dharas, Asuras, etc., have played a part as comrades or enemies of the king.
C06-GANGULY-046   p. 83   also Ch 4
Side by side with his political activities, Sindhurâja energetically fostered the literary movement, which had been vigorously carried on in Malwa under the patronage of his predecessors. Padmagupta tells us that, \( ^{2} \)  “The seal which Vâkpatirâja put upon my song when he was about to enter heaven (by his death), Sindhurâja, brother of that friend of poets, now breaks.” By this he means that the loss of Vâkpati silenced him, and now the genial patronage of Sindhurâja revives his poetic genius.
This indirectly shows that the literary activities, which had fallen into abeyance owing to the death of Vâkpati, received fresh impetus under the encouraging attention of Sindhurâja. The old poets Dhanika and Dhanapâla seem also to have adorned his court.
C06-GANGULY-047   p. 94   also Ch 4
This record is engraved on the pedestal of an image of Sarasvatī, now in the British Museum. It states that in Samvat 1091=1033 A. D., Bhoja caused this image of Vågdevī to be erected by a sculptor, Manathala, the son of the craftsman Sahira.
C06-GANGULY-048   p. 99   also Ch 4
Schools were established for the proper education of his subjects. \( ^{1} \)  Sufficient emoluments and bounties were granted to those who devoted their lives to the cultivation of literary science. He himself was a great poet, and spent his days in the company of erudite scholars. \( ^{2} \)  He was beloved by all the literary men of his age, and his unbounded liberality gave them fresh encouragement to pursue their work.
C06-GANGULY-049   p. 135   also Ch 4
information about this battle. It was found engraved on a slab of stone in the Bhojasâlâ, at Dhar. It was composed by the royal preceptor Madana, and contains the two first acts of a drama of four acts, called Pârijâta-mañjari or Vijayasri. The remaining portions are lost. It was first staged in the temple of Sarasvati, at Dhârâ, in the spring festival, when Arjunavarman was ruling in Mâlava. The king is described as the son of Subhaṭa and a descendant of the great Bhoja.
C06-GANGULY-050   p. 215; also at p. 215
On the whole, the reign of Arjunaivarman was one of peace and order. The Pārijātamañjārī sets before us the high state of civilisation that gloried Malwa during this period. The people lived their lives in joy and happiness, and their social activities were marked by refined taste. Arjunaivarman, like his predecessor Bhoja, was not only a patron of learning, encouraging in others the cultivation of literature, but himself a great poet. \( ^{2} \)  Madana was his court poet and Āśādhara, the great Jaina author, lived in his kingdom, in the city of Nalakacchapura (modern Nalcha).
C06-GANGULY-051   p. 219
The Paramāra kings were great votaries of the Goddess of learning, and were in reality the very heart of the great literary movement of the period. Many of them were themselves great poets. Their generous sympathy encouraged others to devote their lives to the enrichment of the stores of literature.
C06-GANGULY-052   p. 285
He is said to have exclaimed, when he was led to execution in the Câlukya capital—
"Fortune will go to Govinda; glory and heroism to the house of the Hero;
"But when Muñja has passed away, that storehouse of fame, Sarasvatī, will be without support."
C06-GANGULY-053   p. 287
Bhâskarabhatta, son of the poet Trivikrama, received from him the title of 'Vidyâpati.' The Âin-i-Akbarî relates that—"Bhoja held wisdom in honour, the learned men were treated with distinction, and seekers after knowledge were encouraged by his support. Five hundred sages, the most erudite of the age, shone as the gathered wisdom of his court, and were entertained in a manner becoming their dignity and merit."
C06-GANGULY-054   p. 288
It is doubtful whether Bhoja himself wrote so many books on different subjects. Some of them might have been his own productions, but the rest seem to have been written by a group of literary men under his guidance.
C06-GANGULY-055   p. 289
Sarvadeva, the father of Dhanapâla, migrated from Madhyadeśa, and settled in Ujjain. In fulfilment of his promise to the Jaina Vardhamâna, he asked his son to embrace the Jaina faith; but Dhanapâla was averse to forsaking the Brahmanical religion, and this refusal to comply with his father's wishes completely dissociated him from his family. He migrated to Dhârâ, and there studied all branches of Brahmanic learning, but subsequently, through the influence of his younger brother, he became a follower of the Jaina faith.² He gained the favourable notice of king Bhoja, and rose to be one of his principal court poets.
C06-GANGULY-057   p. 292
The king was curious to read the book, and, when he had done so, he was much impressed by its merit, and asked the poet to make him the hero of the tale, changing the name of the country from Vinatā to Avanti. But Dhanapāla refused to comply with the king's request, whereupon Bhoja became highly incensed, and burnt the entire manuscript. This gave a rude shock to the poet's feeling, and he became dejected and broken at the loss of his precious book. But, fortunately for him, his daughter Bālapanditā, was a woman of keen intelligence, and she remembered half the text of the book, and reproduced it in the original form. This, to a certain extent, consoled the heart of the poet, and, later on, she is said to have composed the remaining portion of the work. We have no evidence to corroborate this story.
C06-GANGULY-058   p. 294
\( ^{4} \)  He was connected with the administrative department of the Paramāra government. In one of his compositions he speaks of himself as a “mahāsādhvapāla” of king Utpalarāja. \( ^{5} \)  He cultivated the art of literature, in which he attained a high degree of perfection. He wrote a commentary in prose on Dhanājjaya’s ‘Daśarūpa’ entitled Daśarūpāvaloka or Daśarūpāloka, which gives clear proof of his high intellectual and literary attainments.
C06-GANGULY-059   p. 295
Padmagupta, whose other name was Parimala, was a court poet of both Vākpati—Muñja and Sindhurāja, \( ^{2} \)  He tells us that he followed the path laid down by the poets through the grace of Vākpatirāja. He composed the book Navasāhasānka-carita, \( ^{3} \)  it is said, at the order of the king Sindhurāja.
C06-GANGULY-060   p. 298

Mālava, and lived in Dhārā, at the court of Muñja. He prepared his “Mṛtasamjīvani” a commentary on the “Piṅgala-chandaḥsūtra”, about this time, and speaks highly of the king’s liberality.

Uvata, or Uata, was the son of Vajrata, a resident of Ånandapura, the modern Vadnagar, in Gujarat. He settled in Avanti in the first half of the eleventh century A. D., and devoted his time to literary studies. He composed his book “Mantrabhāṣya”, a commentary on the Vājasaneya Samhitā, during the reign of Bhoja.
C06-GANGULY-063   p. 301
When Sapādalakṣa was conquered by Shihāb-ud-dīn Muḥammad Ghorī in 1192 A. D., Āśādhara, for fear of being molested by the conquering armies, left his native country, and migrated with his family to Mâlava. He was a contemporary of the kings Vindhyavarman, Arjunavarman, Devapāla, and Jaitugideva. The town of Nalakachapura was the chief centre of his activities as a writer and as a teacher, and it was through his learned instruction that several great scholars of high merit were produced at that period.
C06-GANGULY-064   p. 302

C06-GANGULY-065   p. 304
\( ^{1} \)  In his early years he learnt the art of poetry from Āśādhara, \( ^{2} \)  and obtained the title of “Bālasarasvatī”, in recognition of his poetic genius, \( ^{3} \)  rising to the position of royal preceptor \( ^{4} \)  during the reign of Arjunavarman. His facile pen also produced the drama of “Pārījāta-mañjarī” or, as it is sometimes called, “Vijayasri”, commemorating the victory of Arjunavarman over the Gujarat king, Jayasimha. \( ^{5} \)  In his commentary on Amaruśataka, Arjunavarman quotes one of Madana’s verses in Sardûlavikṛdita, and ascribes to him the authorship of an Āryā and two Anuṣṭubha verses. \( ^{6} \)  Three inscriptions of the reign of Arjunavarman, which have been noticed above, were also composed by him.
C06-GANGULY-066   p. 305
But above all else, Malwa can claim the highest credit for her rich contributions to the stock of Sanskrit literature. In those ancient days, few provinces of India witnessed the birth of so many literary magnates, within so short a period, as did Malwa during the reign of the Paramāra dynasty. The literature of a country is admittedly the crucial test of its culture, and Malwa, through her possession of so valuable, varied and rich a literature, has proved her high achievement in that direction.
C06-GANGULY-067   p. 307
The Mount Abu inscription, of the reign of Dhārāvarsa, dated 1209 A. D., describes him as a prince expert in all the fine arts and useful sciences. The Mount Abu inscription, in the temple of Neminātha, dated 1230 A. D., speaks of his high culture and learning. The poet Somesvara, who composed that record, says—“I am not certain whether it is the goddess sprung from the lotus-seats (Brahmā) or the celestial cow, granting (every) wish that has come to earth in the form of Prahlādana. The same author writes in his Kirtikaumudī that Prahlādana, by composing some pleasant stories, again delighted the goddess of learning, who was afflicted with sorrow on the passing away of Bhoja and Muñja.
C06-GANGULY-068   p. 328
In this record Bhoja announces to the government officials, Brahmans and other local residents, Patels and townsmen, assembled at Nālataḍāga, situated in the Nyāyapadra Seventeen, that he has granted the above mentioned village of Nālataḍāga to the learned Delha, son of Bhatta Thatthasika, who was an emigrant from Sthānvīśvara and whose ancestors had migrated from Visālagrāma. The gift was made on the occasion of the annexation of Konkaṇa, with the object of increasing the religious merit of the king and his parents.

## Jagta Hua Kasba / कथा उदयपुर — English translation (JAGTA-EN, 1)
One reason for the prestige of the art-loving Paramara kings was this too — that they were great lovers of learning. Knowers of all disciplines — language, grammar, poetry, drama, vastu, architecture, astrology, music — used to be around them. Raja Bhoj himself held the status of a scholar among these talented art-experts. In such an atmosphere as that in which Udayaditya must have been raised, around him too there must have been exactly such an atmosphere.

## Some Paramāra Temples From Madhya Pradesh: A Case Study of Village Un — Swati Mondal Adhikari (PARTEMPLES, 4)
As noticed before, in the wall of antarala, one sarabandha inscription and two small undated inscriptions are engraved. The first one is an alphabetical chart with some of the conjugational terminations. The inscription is engraved on the body of a single serpent, combined with the dagger-like object, and measuring 80 cm.
C06-PARTEMPLES-005   p. 18
Bhojaśāla, an institution for learning Sanskrit, was built by him at his capital, Dhāra. To help the students in the process of learning, many inscriptions containing alphabetical charts, grammatical rules etc. were engraved on the wall and pillars of Bhojaśāla. These alphabetical charts were displayed within twin snake coils. Scholars refer to this peculiar mode of presenting grammatical charts by inscription as Śarpabandha inscriptions.
C06-PARTEMPLES-006   p. 45   also Ch 4
He granted villages to many brāhmanas.\(^{19}\) The three grants issued by him speak highly of his personal achievements as a scholar, as a poet and also as a patron of literature and art. Great scholars like Upādhyāya Madana, Paṇḍita Āśādhara and Mahāpandita Bilhana adorned his court.

## King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture (Raja Bhoj aur Parmarkaleen Nagar Sthapatya) — English translation — Bhagwatilal Rajpurohit (RAJABHOJ-EN, 16)
For such learned programmes, King Bhoj had built at Dhara and Ujjayini splendid buildings named Sharada-sadam (शारदासदम्), Bharati-bhavan (भारतीभवन) or Sarasvati-kanthabharana, in which poetry, treatises or grammar were engraved upon various slabs and pillars. This made the very atmosphere of that building one of learning. Dramatic performances too were held in that building.
C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-004   p. 7
King Bhoj had several Sarasvati-kanthabharana halls built in his kingdom, of which the hall at Dhar is now famous by the name Bhojshala (भोजशाला), in which abundant stone-inscribed literature can still be seen today. It is, in truth, a stone-inscribed library.
C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-005   p. 16
It was a Sanskrit school. In it, several Prakrit-Sanskrit poems and plays, and grammar-diagrams such as the *nagabandha*, are engraved.
C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-006   p. 40
many are available. In truth this building was a wondrous library of stone-inscribed works, whose enrichment kept going on until the time of Arjunavarmadeva. The *Parijata-manjari* play of the poet Madana, the royal preceptor of this king, is also engraved in this very building.
C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-007   p. 57
Munj was himself a king and a poet, and a patron of poets. Among those he patronized were several scholars: Padmagupta "Parimal" (पद्मगुप्त परिमल), Dhananjaya (धनंजय), Dhanapala (धनपाल), Amitagati (अमितगति), Halayudha (हलायुध) and others. Of these, Padmagupta composed an epic poem concerning Munj's younger brother Sindhuraja, the *Navasahasanka-charita* (नवसाहसांक चरित). The last three — Dhanapala, Amitagati, Halayudha — were still engaged in composition in the time of King Bhoj as well.
C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-008   p. 2   also Ch 4
It is said that in King Bhoj's court there were five hundred poets and scholars. Among them Uchchata (उच्चट), Dhanapala, Halayudha, Amitagati, Chittapa (चित्तप) and others were renowned. The books of all of them are published. The president of the assembly of these scholars was Maharaja Bhojdev. In that assembly there were recitations of poetry, the completion of poetic problems (*samasya-purti*), scholarly debate (*shastrartha*), displays of art, and so on.
C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-009   p. 7   also Ch 4
According to the *Prabandha-chintamani*, King Bhoj had 104 song-compositions (*gita-prabandha*), as many buildings, and as many epithets. According to a commentary on the *Sarasvati-kanthabharana*, King Bhoj had 84 works and as many epithets. According to the *Parijata-manjari*, the city of Dhara had 84 crossroads and as many temples. For example, *Sarasvati-kanthabharana* is the name of two of King Bhoj's works, was also the name of his Bharati-bhavans at Dhar and Ujjain, and was also one of his epithets.
C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-010   p. 8
King Bhoj continually inspired his scholars to write, according to their competence, treatises on various subjects. The self-acknowledgements of several such scholars and poets are known from their books. Among them, Ubbata (उब्बट), Dhanapala, Chhittapa (छित्तप), Amitagati, Keshava (केशव), Nichula (निचुल) and others are noteworthy. To the poet Purantaka (पुरान्तक), composer of the *Shyamala-dandaka* hymn, Bhoj gave one hundred villages in 1001 CE.
C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-011   p. 8
King Bhoj was an appreciator of merit. Various poets, scholars, artists and scientists enjoyed his patronage. King Bhoj himself was not only a poet and a theorist of poetics, but also a knower of nearly all the subjects known up to his time, and a capable and learned composer of various treatises on those very subjects. From ancient texts it is learnt that he had 104 song-compositions and 84 works.
C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-012   p. 9
From the *Prabhavaka-charita* (प्रभावकचरित) it is learnt that in the Sarasvati-kanthabharana palace of Ujjain, the works of King Bhoj — crest-jewel of the circle of scholars and lord of Malwa — on many subjects were read and taught. Among the foremost of them were: phonetics/lexicon (*shabda*), rhetoric (*alankara*), astrology (*daivajna*), logic (*tarka-shastra*), medicine, statecraft (*raja-siddhanta*), arboriculture (*taru*), architecture (*vastu*), horoscopy (*udaya*), numerology (*anka*), augury (*shakuna*), spirituality (*adhyatma*), dream-lore (*svapna*), physiognomy (*samudrika*), omens (*nimitta*), exposition (*vyakhyana*), *Prashna-chudamani*, cessation (*nivritti*), economics (*arthashastra*), *Megha-mala* (cloud-lore), *Aya-sadbhava*, and so on.
C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-013   p. 10
The *Shringara-manjari-katha* is a linked cycle of thirteen stories, in the manner of the *Dashakumara-charita*, in graceful and mature prose. It is fragmentary in places. In it are found narrative illustrations of the twelve preliminary phases of love (*dvadasha purva-raga*) described in the *Shringara-prakasha*. In this tale a traditional description of King Bhoj himself and of the city of Dhara is also found.
C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-014   p. 11
He had a beautiful image of Vagdevi made in Samvat 1091 (1034 CE), which is now in the British Museum, London. King Bhoj composed a hymn of praise to Vagdevi, which is now available.
C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-015   p. 11   also Ch 5
The *Sarasvati-kanthabharana* on poetics is famous among scholars. Although its five chapters give a traditional exposition of figures of speech (*alankara*), sentiment (*rasa*), poetic merit (*guna*), poetic fault (*dosha*) and so on, it has novelty. In it, 24 varieties each are made of word-based, sense-based and both-based figures of speech. Of merits and faults too there are as many varieties. Several new figures of speech are discussed with illustrations.
C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-016   p. 12
In the town there was a Sharadasadam or Bharati-bhavan or Sarasvati-kanthabharana, where students pursued higher study of Sanskrit. Here the assemblies (*goshthi*) of scholars were held.⁴ In it, debates and learned recreations (*vidya-vinoda*) took place. This scholarly assembly was presided over by King Bhoj himself. In this assembly, various scholars from India and abroad received ample financial benefit and honour from the state.
C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-017   p. 56
Several forms of King Bhoj's book *Charu-charya* are found. Of them, the largest in size is preserved in Mysore. One section of it is named *Jala-mangala*. In this *Jala-mangala* section, a brief account is given of the Ayurvedic properties of the waters of various sources — river, tank, sea, pond, well, stepwell, spring, direct rainwater, hail, snow.
C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-018   p. 112
(3) King Bhoj was a knower of all fourteen branches of learning.
"As Vararuchi [knew] the Vedas and their divisions, so [keen] was the thought in his mind;
King Bhoj was [master of] the fourteen [sciences], with love for the wise."
C06-RAJABHOJ-EN-019   p. 160

Vidisha has been a centre of Sanskrit learning since time immemorial. In the last decade of the 19th century, efforts were made by the ruler of Gwalior State to introduce Sanskrit in the educational institutions of Vidisha. But the efforts were not successful due to lack of Sanskrit teachers.
C06-GAZ-005   p. 277   also Ch 3
## The Cultural Heritage of Madhya Bharat — Patil, D. R. (MADHYABHARAT, 4)
Almost in the same generation in A.D. 861 a temple, dedicated to the god Shauri or Krishna was built at Pathari in front of which was raised a large pillar which still exists with an inscription recording the facts above. The temple, however, does not exist now.
C06-MADHYABHARAT-002   p. 74
Few provinces in India can claim such a rich galaxy of literary magnates in so short a period as did Malwa in the days of the Paramaras. It is because of her vast contribution to the field of Sanskrit learning that Malwa's fame is eternal, a large share of which goes to Bhoja, the Great, who was indeed one of the most celebrated monarchs in Indian History.
C06-MADHYABHARAT-004   p. 35
Monuments.—Kamal Maula Mosque or Bhojashala.—Raja Bhoja was noted in the then Indian world for his great love for learning and literature and is said to have established a college known as Bhojashala remnants of which are still seen … in the famous Mosque of Kamal Maulana near the outskirts of the modern town. The mosque consists of a large open court with a porch in front, colonnades at sides and a large prayer hall at the back in the west. The carved pillars used over the building and the delicately carved ceilings of the prayer hall seem to have belonged to the original Bhojshala. In the pavement of the prayer hall are seen numerous slabs of black slate stone the writings on which were also scraped off. From a few slabs recovered from the other part of the building and now exhibited there, containing the texts of the poetic works of viz., the Parijata Manjari and Kurmastotra, it appears the old [college was adorned with numerous Sanskrit and Maharashtra Prakrit texts beautifully engraved on such slabs which have unfortunately been lost to us for ever.
C06-MADHYABHARAT-005   p. 128–129
They usually consist of long flat stone slabs, not more than 6 feet in height, divided into two, three or even more sculptured panels with, in most cases, a short inscription in the bottom referring to their erection, the name of the ruling sovereign, the name of the village, the name of the sati, her relations, etc. The inscriptions are often found to contain dates.
C06-MADHYABHARAT-006   p. 78
## History of the Parmar Rajput Dynasty — Venziya, Pratapsinh Ranaji (PARMAR-RAJPUT, 3)
Vakapati was a successful king as well as a patron of poets and scholars. During the time of Vakpati, the author of "Yashorupavlik", the great writer of "Navasahasancharita", the poet Padmagupta, the author of "Dasarupaka", Dhananjaya etc. lived in the state. And we find all these scholars in their literature also portraying the glorious journey of the Parmar dynasty.
C06-PARMAR-RAJPUT-003   p. 4   also Ch 4
अद्य धारा सदाधारा सदालम्बा सरस्वती।
पण्डता मण्डता: सर्व भोजराजे भुवि स्थिते।
Meaning : today when Raja Bhoj is on this earth. So the basis of Dharanagari can be seen. And Pandits and scholars are respected in the court of Raja Bhoja.
*Gloss [my translation]:* Today Dhārā has a firm support, Sarasvatī has a prop; all the learned are adorned, while Bhoja rules on earth.
C06-PARMAR-RAJPUT-004   p. 6   also Ch 4
- Abhilekh from Banswara : An annual festival is celebrated in Abhilekh after the conquest of Konkan. A gift of land to a Brahmin is then described. Which is published in Samvat 1076 and 7 January 1020.
- Abhilekh of Betama: Samvat 1076 and E.S. In this inscription of 1020, the information of Bhoja Raja's employees, Brahmins and other villagers is recorded. There is mention of King Bhoja donating on the death anniversary of his parents and in the joy of Konkan victory.
C06-PARMAR-RAJPUT-005   p. 7   also Ch 4
## Art of the Paramāras of Malwa — Sharma, R. K. (ARTPAR, 1)
The Jaina temples at Un \( ^{13} \) ascribed to the eleventh and twelfth centuries appear to have been built during the reign of the later Paramāra kings of Mālwā. This is confirmed by the two inscriptions of Udayāditya and a Sarpabandha inscription of Naravarman.
C06-ARTPAR-002   p. 25
## भोजदेव (Bhojadeva) (BHOJDEV-HI, 24)
14. प्राकृतकाव्य - (1) कोदण्डसंघात (2) अवनिकूर्मशतम् (3) खड्गशतम् (तीन)
(4) अज्ञातनाम काव्य (सभी खंडित-शिलांकित और धार मे सुलभ)।
*Gloss [my translation]:* 14. Prakrit poetry: (1) Kodandasanghata, (2) Avanikurmashataka, (3) Khadgashataka (three), (4) an unnamed poem (all fragmentary, inscribed on stone and found at Dhar).
C06-BHOJDEV-HI-001   p. 17
धाराधीश भोज के रचे कई प्राकृत काव्य शिलांकित हैं । इन काव्यों की भाषा सरल और ललित है ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Several Prakrit poems composed by Bhoja, lord of Dhara, are inscribed on stone; their language is simple and graceful.
C06-BHOJDEV-HI-002   p. 19
राजा भोज ने शारदा सदन या सरस्वती कणठाभरण बनवाये थे। वात्स्यायन ने कामसूत्र में संकेत किया था कि प्रत्येक नगर में सरस्वती भवन होना चाहिए। वहाँ गोष्टिओं, नाटक आदि हर पन्द्रह दिन में होते रहने चाहिए। राजा भोज ने सम्भवतः उसी परम्परा का निर्वाह कर धार, माण्ड्व तथा उज्जेन में सरस्वतीकण्ठाभरण नामक भवन बनवाये थे।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Bhoja built Sharada-sadana / Sarasvati-kanthabharana; Vatsyayana had said each town should have a Sarasvati hall for gatherings and plays; Bhoja probably followed this and built such halls at Dhar, Mandu and Ujjain.
C06-BHOJDEV-HI-003   p. 6
यहाँ तक वताया गया कि एक कवि को रहने का स्थान चाहिए था । राजा ने आदेश दिया कि धारा नगरी में जो कविता नहीं करता हो उसका घर … इस कवि को दे दिया जाये । कर्मचारी एक जुलाहे को पकड़ लाये । जुलाहे ने तव राजसभा में एक अनेखा श्लोक रचकर सुना दिया -
*Gloss [my translation]:* A poet needed lodging; the king ordered that the house of anyone in Dhara who did not compose poetry be given to this poet; officials brought a weaver, who composed a striking verse in the royal court.
C06-BHOJDEV-HI-004   p. 9–10   also Ch 9
चित्रप, हलायुध, भटुलक्ष्मीधर, धनपाल, अमितगति आदि कितने ही विद्वान् साहित्यकार इस युग को अपनी झानसाधना से समूढ़ कर रहे थे । इसी समय उज्जैन में उव्यट ने वेद की व्याख्या की और महाकाल के पुजारी पुरंतक ने श्यमलादण्डक नामक विख्यात स्तोत्र की रचना की । इस समय कितने ही जैन विद्वान् और संत हुए । राजा भोज ने उनका भी हृदय से स्वागत किया । इस प्रकार तत्कालीन समूचा शिक्षित समाज राजा भोज का आदरपात्र हो गया और उसका प्रशंसक भी हो गया ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Scholars like Chitrapa, Halayudha, Bhatta Lakshmidhara, Dhanapala and Amitagati enriched the age; at Ujjain Uvvata commented on the Veda and Purantaka, priest of Mahakala, composed the Shyamaladandaka; many Jain scholars and saints appeared and Bhoja welcomed them.
C06-BHOJDEV-HI-005   p. 9
कहते हैं कि भोज हर छः माह में चालीस दिन का उस्प व करता था और उसमें कवि, क्लाकार तथा विडान सम्पिलित होते थे। उन्हें उचित सम्पान तथा भंट उपहार दिया जाता था । जो धारा नगरी में वसना चाहते थे उनकी उचित व्यवस्था की जाती थी । सम्प-सम्प पर विडानों में चर्चा-परिचर्चा होती रहती थी । यहाँ शास्त्र-परीक्षा तथा काव्य-परीक्षा की सभाएँ होतीं, कथागोष्ठियाँ होतीं, समस्यापूर्ति होतीं ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* It is said Bhoja held a forty-day festival every six months attended by poets, artists and scholars, who were honoured and given gifts; those wishing to settle in Dhara were accommodated; there were debates, tests of sastra and poetry, story gatherings and samasya-purti.
C06-BHOJDEV-HI-006   p. 9
राजा भोज भी ऐसी सभाएँ करता रहता था । और विभिन्न विषयक प्रस्थों की स्वयं रचना करता था और रचने की प्रेरणा भी देता था । इस प्रकार राजा भोज की नगरी विद्यानगरी हो गयी थी और राज्य विद्याराज्य हो गया था ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Raja Bhoja also held such assemblies and himself wrote works on various subjects and inspired others to write; thus his city became a city of learning and his realm a realm of learning.
C06-BHOJDEV-HI-007   p. 9
वल्लाल के अनुसार राजा भोज की सभा में पाँच सौ कवि थे। वल्लाल के अतिरिक्त भी विभिन्न कवियों ने समय-समय पर अनेक भोजप्रबंध रचकर उस अप्रतिम राजा के प्रति अपनी आस्था प्रकट की।
*Gloss [my translation]:* According to Ballala Raja Bhoja's court had five hundred poets; apart from Ballala various poets composed Bhojaprabandhas.
C06-BHOJDEV-HI-008   p. 10
द्वितीय कूर्भशतं, कोदण्डकाव्य, तीन खड्गाशतं, अन्य अनेक खण्डित प्राकृत काव्यों में भी भोज को वीर, दानी और उदार वताया गया है । ये सव ललिल और प्रसाद गुण सम्पत्र हैं । ये काव्य खण्ड शिलाओं पर धार और कुछ उज्जेन में भी प्राप्त होते हैं ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Other Prakrit poems (Kurmashataka II, Kodandakavya, three Khadgashatakas, many fragmentary ones) also call Bhoja brave, generous and liberal; these are graceful and clear; these poem fragments are found on stone slabs at Dhar and some at Ujjain.
C06-BHOJDEV-HI-009   p. 11   also Ch 4
भावप्रकाश और रुचिवनिश्चय (माधवकृत) में उसे आयुवंदलेखक वताया गया है । केशवार्क ने भोज को ज्योतिर्यन्थ का रचयिता बताया । क्षीरस्वामी, सायण और महीप ने उसे वैयाकरण तथा … कोपकार वताया है। कवि चित्रप, विनायक शंकर सरस्वती, कुटुम्बदुहितू आदि ने भोज की काव्यक्षमता की प्रशंसा की है। शब्दसाप्राज्यम् मृत्य मे भोज्याकरण के अनुसार शब्दसिद्धि की गयी है। कार्ययेम ने शाकुन्तल-टीका में भोज को भरत के समान नाट्यवेता वताया है -
*Gloss [my translation]:* Bhavaprakasha and Ruchiviniscaya call him an Ayurveda author; Keshavarka an astronomy author; Kshirasvamin, Sayana and Mahipa a grammarian and lexicographer; poets praise his poetic ability; Shabdasamrajya is composed on Bhoja's grammar; Karyayem in the Shakuntala commentary calls Bhoja a knower of drama like Bharata.
C06-BHOJDEV-HI-010   p. 12–13
सुप्रसिद्ध टीकाकार मल्लनाथ ने भोज का कई सन्दर्भों में उल्लेख किया है । यथा अथशास्त्र, व्याकरण, कोप, काव्यशास्त्र, राजमृगांक (ज्योतिपु), चारुचर्यादि आचारप्रन्थ इत्यादि भोजकृतियों के उद्दरण दिये हैं । रामचन्द्र युधेन्द्र ने तो भोज को धनपाल के समान “निश्णेपवाङ्मयविद्” ही कहा है । इससे स्पष्ट है कि राजा भोज की विविध विपयक अनेक प्रामाणिक प्रन्थों के रचयिता के रूप में ख्याति थी । प्रभावकचरित में भोजकृत प्रन्थों के विभिन्न विषयों की एकत्र सूची ही दी थी जो तव शाला में पढ़ाये भी जाते थे । यथा - भोजव्याकरण, शब्द-अलंकारशास्त्र, तर्कशास्त्र, चिकित्साशास्त्र, राजसिद्धांत, रसशास्त्र, वासुशास्त्र, उदयशास्त्र, अंकशास्त्र, शकुनशास्त्र, अध्यात्मशास्त्र, स्वनशास्त्र, सामुद्रिकशास्त्र, निमित्ता-व्याख्यानशास्त्र, प्रशनचूढ़ामणि, अयःसद्भाविवृति, अर्थकाण्ड, मेघमाला इत्यादि ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Commentator Mallinatha cites Bhoja's works on political science, grammar, lexicon, poetics, astrology and conduct; Ramachandra Budhendra calls him like Dhanapala a master of all literature; Prabhavakacharita gives a list of Bhoja's works on many subjects, which were then taught in schools.
C06-BHOJDEV-HI-011   p. 13
भोज विविध विषयक इन प्रस्थों की रचना वड़ी शीघ्रता से कर लेता था। प्रवंध चिन्तामणि में भोज की इस विशेषता को रेखांकित किया गया है - जवाइन्थ नाना प्रवन्धः।
इसकी पुष्टि करते हुए सत्रहर्वीं शती का राजदूझामणि दीक्षित कहता है कि भोज ने एक रात में रामचरित रचा जिसे एक दिन में युद्धकांड रचकर चूझामणि पूरा कर रहा है
भोजेन रामचरितं प्रथितं निशयैकया ।
एकेन पूरयत्थना श्रीचूझामणि दीक्षितः ।।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Bhoja could compose works on diverse subjects very quickly; Prabandhachintamani underlines this; a 17th-century writer, Chudamani Dikshita, says Bhoja composed the Ramacharita in a single night, which Chudamani completes with the Yuddhakanda in one day.
C06-BHOJDEV-HI-012   p. 14
जयसिंह सिद्धराज का सभाकवि भी कहता है कि उसने एक दिन में महाकाव्य रच लिया था - एकाह निष्पत्र महाप्रवन्धः ।
इससे स्पष्ट है कि दक्ष रचयिता का भापा और शैली पर इतना अधिकार होता था कि वे एक दिन में भी पूरा काव्य रच सकते थे । राजा भोज ऐसा ही दक्ष रचनाकार था । अतः विभिन्न विषयक प्रस्थ एक साथ रच लेना भोज जैसे प्रतिभाशाली के लिए असंभव नहीं था । भोज के वंशज अजुनवर्मा सहित कितने ही अन्य प्रस्थकारों ने भी भोजदेव की असामान्य प्रतिभा की पुष्टि की है ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Jayasimha Siddharaja's court poet also says he composed a mahakavya in one day; skilled composers could write a whole poem in a day; Raja Bhoja was such a composer, so composing works on various subjects together was not impossible for him; others including his descendant Arjunavarman confirm Bhojadeva's talent.
C06-BHOJDEV-HI-013   p. 14
भोज के नाम से झात विभिन्न प्रस्थी की सूची इस प्रकार है -
1. साहित्य -
(1) घम्पूरागयण, (2) शुंगारमंजरीकथा, (3) अविनिकुमशत, (4) सुभाषितप्रवन्ध, (5) वार्देवीस्टुलि, (6) विद्याचिनोद, (7) शालिकथा, (8) महाकाली विजय।
*Gloss [my translation]:* List of works known under Bhoja's name: 1. Literature: (1) Champu-Ramayana, (2) Shringaramanjarikatha, (3) Avanikurmashataka, (4) Subhashitaprabandha, (5) Vagdevistuti, (6) Vidyavinoda, (7) Shalikatha, (8) Mahakalivijaya.
C06-BHOJDEV-HI-014   p. 16
राजा भोज की विविध विषयक रचनाओं को परवर्ती ग्रन्थकार प्रमाण मानकर उब्ज्त करते रहे। फिर चाहे वह साहित्यशास्त्र का सरस्वतीकण्ठाभरण हो अथवा शूंगारप्रकाश हो, धर्मशास्त्र हो अथवा ज्योतिपूर हो, वैद्यक हो अथवा जीवनचर्यां की चारचर्यां। शैव दर्शन हो अथवा व्याकरण, कोष हो अथवा नीति या अर्थशास्त्र, संगीत हो अथवा नाटक । स्पष्ट ही भोज को भारतीय विद्धत्समाज विशेष मान निरन्तर देता आ रहा है ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Later authors kept quoting Bhoja's works as authority, whether poetics, dharmashastra, astrology, medicine, Shaiva philosophy, grammar, lexicon, polity, music or drama; clearly Indian scholars have continually given Bhoja special regard.
C06-BHOJDEV-HI-015   p. 18
भोज ने वागदेवी की सुवित में पूरा काव्य ही नहीं रचा, अपितु सरस्वतीकणठाभरण, शूगारमंजरी सहित कितनी ही कृतिया में भी उसका नमन किया गया। यही नहीं 1034 ई. में भोज ने वागदेवी की मनोहर प्रतिमा भी बनवायी।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Bhoja not only wrote a whole poem in praise of Vagdevi; she is also saluted in Sarasvatikanthabharana, Shringaramanjari and other works; in 1034 CE Bhoja had a charming image of Vagdevi made.
C06-BHOJDEV-HI-016   p. 18

## Temple Economics (Arthavyavasthā of Mandirs), Volume I — Singh, Sandeep (TEMPLE-ECON, 3)
The Chaturbhuj mandir of Bhagwan Vishnu in Gwalior is carved out of the rock. Its construction has been dated to 876 CE. The mandir has got zero engraved at two places. One is stand alone and the other is part of the number '270' which apparently refers to the size of a land grant. The mandir was built by Alla, the son of Vaillabhatta, and the grandson of Nagarabhatta of the Gurjara-Pratihar dynasty.
C06-TEMPLE-ECON-001   p. 48
The detailed inscriptions on mandir walls are important historical records that bring alive an ancient world of kings and kingdoms, traders and farmers, priests and peasant folk whose lives revolved around the mandir.
C06-TEMPLE-ECON-002   p. 50
While rebuilding temples, she ensured that existing inscriptions were copied out and inscribed later in the vimana of the new structure. This is found in Thirukodikaval and Aduthurai temples.
C06-TEMPLE-ECON-003   p. 98
## Samarāṅgaṇasūtradhāra: An Ancient Treatise on Architecture, vol. 1 — Bhojadeva (attrib.) (SAMAR-1, 2)
Pratipāla Bhatia \( ^{1} \) Muñja's nephew Mahārājādhirāja Kavirāja Śiṣṭa Śiromaṇi Dhāreśvara, Śrī Bhojadeva was first and foremost a man of great learning, a versatile scholar, a polymath indeed, for he has been credited with works in almost every branch of knowledge. According to Ājada \( ^{2} \) who wrote a commentary named Padakaprakāśa on Sarasvatī Kaṇthābharaṇa \( ^{3} \), Bhoja wrote 84 works giving them names with his own titles and binds. Prabhācandra Suri Prabhāvaka Carita \( ^{4} \) refers to Bhoja's works in several branches of learning. Of the works ascribed to him, the authorship of the following can be traced with Rājamārtanḍa a commentary on Patañjali's Yogasūtra, Sarasvatī Kaṇthābharaṇa (grammar) and Sarasvatī Kaṇthābharaṇa (Poetics), Śṛngāra Prakāśa (Poetics and Dramaturgy), Tattva Prakāśa (a manual on Agamic Śaivas), Brhadrājamārtanḍa (dharmasāstra grantha), Rājamrgankā \( ^{5} \) (medicine), and a work on Astronomy (1042 A.D.)
C06-SAMAR-1-001   Vol. 1, p. 4 ⚠ old OCR   also Ch 4
Though K.M. Munshi and Dr. Ganguly doubt the handiwork of Bhoja on all. A general editor he may have been.
C06-SAMAR-1-002   Vol. 1, p. 5 ⚠ old OCR   also Ch 4
## The Temple Architecture of India — Adam Hardy (HARDY, 1)
The inscription at Sudi quoted in Chapter 2 relates how the general Nagadeva entrusted the building of his temple to a certain Shamka. He was a ‘mine of splendid learning’, a ‘model for the acquisition of wealth’, a ‘Vachaspati [poet] of punning phrase’. Virtuosity is prized in architecture as in literature, as the ‘finials’ of the temple (presumably referring to the ingenious aeducular components) were ‘completed in a manner that none could possibly imagine’.⁵ Another inscription from Karnataka, of a century or so later, states that, in the village of Kuppatur, was built ‘as if by Viśvakarma [the divine architect] himself, out of sublime devotion for the Lord of the Kailāsa mountain [Shiva], the elegant, equipoised and shapely temple of Kotisa-Bhava, freely ornamented with Drāvidja, Bhūmija and Nāgara, and, with bhadra-offsets manipulated in many ways’.⁶ The Dravida, Bhumija and Nagara (here meaning Shekhari) are the complex, detailed shrine models found over niches in the walls of later Karnata Dravida temples, including ‘exotic’ northern modes, and often exhibiting unusual conceptions never realised at full scale.
C06-HARDY-001   p. 68

## The Cultural Heritage of Madhya Bharat — Patil, D. R. (MADHYABHARAT, 8)
They generally consist of massive bettermented walls, strengthened by still more massive bastions at convenient intervals, and having gates, the number and situations of which differ with the individual forts. It has been observed that the gate at the highest point is often called as Hawa Paur or wind gate ob, iously from its more open situation which admitted of a good breeze from all possible directions. The other gates were often named after important towns or villages in the direction of which they actually faced.
C07-MADHYABHARAT-001   p. 75
The fort proper, it appears, was occupied mainly by the chief or military commander and his paraphernalia and military establishment and other followers the rest of the civil population residing in the villages or towns close to a convenient side of the fort. These outlaying areas were also often enclosed by fortification walls pierced with gates. In case of siege this acted as an outer defence work. If there was a protracted or hopeless siege the civil population could be allowed to seek refuge in the main fort.
C07-MADHYABHARAT-002   p. 76
Islamic architecture in Madhya Bharat begins in 14-15th century with the mosques at Dhar, Mandu, Bhonrasa and Bhilsa. This first phase consisted mainly of conversion of older Hindu temples into mosques built often on the same sites with the materials of the destroyed temples adjusted with certain skilful devices to conform to the accepted standards of the Muslim religious edifices.
C07-MADHYABHARAT-007   p. 49
At the provincial or important towns of the Malwa kingdom such as Chanderi, Sarangpur, Ujjain, Sadalpur, Nalcha, Bhilsa and Bhonrasa Islam was treated with favour as reflected in the surviving monuments at these places. In the north also near-about Gwalior Islam received a good deal of following as is obvious from the mosques and tombs at Gwalior and Narwar. It will be observed that there are nearly 113 old mosques, large or small, on record to suggest that Islam had been quite a religion of consequence in Madhya Bharat during the last few centuries.
C07-MADHYABHARAT-009   p. 75
It was a prosperous centre of Janism and Hinduism in Central India and a number of magnificent temples were built here which the Muslim chronicles refer to have been destroyed by invaders in subsequent times. The famous Bijamandal mosque in the town must have been once one of the largest temples in Central India which was later converted into a mosque.
C07-MADHYABHARAT-010   p. 111
Wells, Tanks and Ghats.—There are 211 old tanks, ghats and wells in the various parts of the State. They were constructed apparently as measures of public utility and social welfare which have throughout ages been regarded as a meritorious cause. A well is often a necessary corollary of a temple and ancient temples are often found to have wells sunk in the vicinity.
C07-MADHYABHARAT-013   p. 79   also Ch 8
are sometimes found to be very elaborately planned and constructed with built-in apartments in the walls of the interior of the well and with flights of steps leading down to water. The most interesting examples of such huge stepped wells are found at Mandu, Chandei, Baodipura (Morena District), Bhonrasa and Burro (Bhilsa District), Dhakoni and Deokani (Guna District), Gwalior, etc. Some of these wells contain inscriptions referring to the facts of their construction and also to the ruling sovereign.
C07-MADHYABHARAT-014   p. 80
Civil or Secular Architecture.—Unlike religious architecture, as exemplified in temples, rock-cut caves, stupas, etc., there have survived no edifices such as palaces and other residential buildings giving us some idea of the ways of living of the people in ancient Madhya Bharat. There are many references in literature to the building of palaces and mansions, but none of them, like some of the ancient temples, have survived. It would seem that they were built of perishable material like wood, which was readily available and could lend itself to carvings and designs required to adorn these buildings.
C07-MADHYABHARAT-017   p. 50
## Madhya Pradesh District Gazetteers: Vidisha — Government of Madhya Pradesh (GAZ, 3)

C07-GAZ-007   p. 340   also Ch 10
In villages, generally, houses are of mud and have only the ground floor. Pakka houses are infrequent. In the towns, poor people live in mud-made kachcha houses, whereas, economically better off people live in pakka houses made of bricks or stones or both.
C07-GAZ-010   p. 88
## History of the Paramāra Dynasty — Ganguly, D. C. (GANGULY, 4)
It was issued by Kesava, the younger brother of the merchant Dhala, a son of Bilhaṇa, who was a son of Dosi, an inhabitant of Umdapura, when the Parama-bhattâraka Mahârâjâdhirâja Devapâladeva was ruling at Dhârâ. The object of the inscription is to record that in Sam. 1275=1218 A. D., Kesava built a temple of Sambhu, together with a tank, on the north-eastern side of Harsapura, close to which he also set up idols of Hanumat, Kṣetrapâla, Ganesa, Krṣṇa, Nakultîsa, Ambikâ, and others.
C07-GANGULY-001   p. 220   also Ch 8
(v) On the outskirts of the town of Bhilsa there is an old mosque known as Bijamandira. On one of its pillars there is an inscription \( ^{2} \) of the reign of Naravarman, in which he is given the epithet of Nirvâna-Nârâyaṇa. It records that it was by the grace of the Goddess Cacikâ (or Carcikâ) that the lord of Dhârâ assumed the sovereignty of the earth. She was the favourite goddess of Naravarman on whom she bestowed all the qualities of a good ruler. This suggests that the present building was originally a temple of Carcikâ, which was subsequently converted into a mosque.
C07-GANGULY-002   p. 174   also Ch 4
On the outskirts of the modern town of Bhilsa, in the Gwalior State, there is a Muhammadan mosque known locally as 'Bijamandira'. A careful examination … of this building reveals the fact that it is a converted Hindu temple of mediaeval times. A long inscription of Naravarman's reign has been discovered on one of its pillars, which records the king's unvarying devotion to the Goddess Carcikâ. It may be that the fane originally belonged to this Goddess.
C07-GANGULY-004   p. 280–281   also Ch 6
A number of the inhabitants of the village of Bhundipada united in contributing various sums of money for the construction of a step-well, with the object of providing pure drinking water for the people of the locality. The donors are described as realising that one can remain alive even for a month without food, but that without water none can survive even for a day and a night.
C07-GANGULY-008   p. 340   also Ch 8

## The Temple Architecture of India — Adam Hardy (HARDY, 1)
Tanks – for irrigation, drinking water, ablutions, laundry, swimming, ritual, festivals and fish (not always all of these together) – are an ubiquitous feature of important temple groups, when these are not built by rivers. As sacred as they are useful, more elaborate tanks are lined with miniature temples.
C07-HARDY-002   p. 109
## The Hindu Temple, Volume I — Stella Kramrisch (KRAM-1, 1)
As the positions of the images are fixed in relation to the Västumandala underlying the Prāsāda, so are, theoretically at least, the positions determined of the various temples within any kind of settlement and also outside of it and in relation to it.
Hamlets, villages, market towns, citadels, cities, capitals and suburbs, all these walled habitations, \( ^{15} \) as the Prāsāda itself, are laid out on the Vāstupuruṣamandala, and the temples of the village or city have their place assigned on it. In practice, equal in Śaiva and Vaiṣṇava texts, the main position of a Śiva temple is in the North-East, in Iśāna, and that of a Viṣṇu temple in the West, in Varuna; the Centre is the place for the temple of Brahmā, or of Viṣṇu or of any other form of God as the main divinity of the temple.
C07-KRAM-1-002   p. 246

## King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture (Raja Bhoj aur Parmarkaleen Nagar Sthapatya) — English translation — Bhagwatilal Rajpurohit (RAJABHOJ-EN, 26)
The rampart or enclosing wall (*vapra* or *prakara*) is stated to be of five kinds: square, oblong, circular, circular-oblong, and fully round.
> **चतुरस्रमायताभं वृत्तं वृत्तायतं च पुनः ।**
> **स्याद् गोलवृत्तमेषां वप्राकारास्तु पंचैव ॥ स.सू.**
>
> *(Sanskrit verse, Sa.Su.: "Square, oblong, circular, and again circular-oblong, and fully round — of these, the forms of the rampart are just five.")*
*Gloss [my translation]:* Square, oblong, circular, again circular-oblong, and fully round: of these, the forms of the rampart are only five.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-001   p. 25
In the middle of the *rashtra*, below a river, the capital should have its own political [buildings], distinct from the town. In the various directions there should be four gate-passages (*hara*), a *gopura* (gateway-tower), and an enclosing wall. There should be three-and-two markets, settlements of all kinds of people, and temples of all kinds. Outside there should be an earthen rampart (*bhulakota*, *supamshuchaya sala*), around which there should be a moat, and which the [outlying] camps (*shibira*) guard from many sides. A capital is one that has, facing east and south, the royal force; high *gopuras*; a chain of caverns/passages (*guha-mala*); temples of all kinds; various courtesans; many gardens; [forces] endowed with elephants, cavalry, chariots and infantry; gate-passages (*hara*) filled with all kinds of people and endowed with gift-tributes (*upahara*); within, the residence of many people; and adorned with the royal residence.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-002   p. 26
Outside the bell-road (Ring-Road), all around, the rampart (*vapra*, enclosing wall or outer fortification) should be built, to the measure of the great highway. The moats should be three. The rampart should be built from the earth [dug for] the moat; if necessary, more earth should be added. It should be built flat like a roof, or sloping like an elephant's back.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-003   p. 29
Around the town three moats should be made, whose beds should be properly built of stone or brick. They should be filled with water brought through channels, or with naturally-arriving water. Various lotuses should be planted in them.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-004   p. 29
The upper and middle part of the rampart should be built without break, of large slabs or of baked bricks. Its width should be twelve, ten or eight cubits, and its height seventeen, fifteen or thirteen cubits. It should not be higher than seventeen cubits, nor lower than thirteen. For each cubit of height, the width should be reduced by two *angulas*. Thus, if at the base the width is twelve cubits, the top will remain ten cubits wide.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-005   p. 30
To the east of these temples, and to the south from the river, the remains of the rampart encircling this ancient *pattana*, and the remains of square bastions, are found. In that period, the Paramara era, bastions used to be square only. Later, for protection from cannonballs, after the coming of Babur, bastions began to be made round.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-006   p. 49
These bastions were, in fact, made four-cornered or square in the Paramara era, in keeping with Hindu and Paramara architecture. Examples of this are found in the forts of Dhar, Vidisha and Omkareshvara.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-007   p. 67
The entrance-gates of the Paramara-era rampart were not straight. They were swastika-shaped, so that enemies, on entering the gate, could not go straight in. From the swastika-shaped gate being crooked, it is easy to attack and strike even the enemies who have entered the gate. Hence, gates were built not straight but swastika-shaped. At sites such as Dhar, Burhi Mandav, Omkareshvara, Vidisha and others, such gates are found.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-008   p. 68
In keeping with the ancient [towns], the towns of this era too were protected by a deep moat and rampart. Not only that — around the town there was a protective arrangement of a river or tanks or both. In Dhara, along with the ditch, there were thirteen tanks on all sides, which not only enhanced the town's beauty but also protected it. At Mandav too, along with the moat, there were protective tanks. At Omkareshvara there is the river's moat. At Vidisha there is the river's, and a constructed, moat.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-009   p. 69
King Bhoj says that in truth the strength of a king is no strength; the fort alone is strength. Even a king of little power can stand firm, by the strength of the fort, before one more powerful. A single archer secure within a rampart can fight a hundred men outside, and a hundred men can fight ten thousand.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-010   p. 79
In ancient times square bastions were built. But once cannon began to be used, they began to be built round, so that when cannonballs were fired the wall would not break and they would slip off.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-011   p. 80
In the upper-middle part of the rampart, thick stones should be laid, or else baked bricks. The large [rampart] should be twelve cubits [wide], the middling ten cubits, and the small eight cubits. In height the large should be seventeen cubits, the middling fifteen, and the inferior thirteen cubits. It should be neither more than seventeen nor less than thirteen. Nor should the measure be of even (4, 8, etc.) cubits. With a two-*angula* [reduction of] width per cubit, at the base there is a width of twelve cubits; four cubits high and ten cubits wide at the top; a one-cubit-high coping (battlement); and a two-cubit *kandavarini* (arrow-parapet).
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-012   p. 81
*dhulakota*, let a rampart (*prakara*) be built. Its bricks should be twice as long as they are wide. Its height should be 12 to 24 cubits (18 to 36 feet). Its base should be firm like a palm-tree. At the top it should be so broad that chariots can easily run upon it.¹ Its crest should be made firm with a *mridanga* [ridge] or coping, or let ramparts of large slabs be built. Never have a rampart of wood built, because fire dwells in wood.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-013   p. 86
The pair of door-leaves should be of three or five parts. In it there should be two bolts. The pivot-pin (*indrakila*) should be about one cubit high. The small door should be five cubits. The chief door should be broad enough for four elephants to pass out at once.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-014   p. 87
In all forts there should be a rampart and moat. There should be exit-gates. The rampart should be built 12 cubits high, of bricks.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-015   p. 103
In the east of the town there should be temples of Vishnu, Surya, Indra and Dharma. Sanatkumara, Savitri, Marut and Maruta should be in the east–south quarter. The temples of Ganesha, the Mothers (*Matri*), Bhuta, the lord of ghosts (*Pretapati*), Bhadrakali and the ancestors (*Pitri*), and shrines (*chaitya*), should be in the southwest. The temple of the river, sea, Prajapati and Varuna should be in the west. The temples of Naga, Shani and Katyayani should be in the northwest. The shrines of Shiva, Shri and Agni should be in the northeast.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-016   p. 29
In the middle of the town there should be the cells or chambers of Aparajita (Durga), Apratihata (Vishnu), Jayanta (Indra's son) and Vaijayanta (Indra). Let temples of Shiva, Vaishravana (Kubera), the Ashvini-kumaras, Shri (Lakshmi), and Madira (Kali or Durga) be built. Let the building-deity (*vastu-devata*) be installed in its proper place. Let them be built, in order from the northeast, [in the quarters of] Brahma, Indra, Yama, and the general (*senapatya*).
Outside the moat, at a distance of a hundred *dhanus*, let shrines (*chaitya*), holy places, forests, causeways and so on be built, and let the direction-deities be established each according to its own direction.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-017   p. 88
A Buddhist temple should be built in the northwest, a Jain temple in the southwest, a Bhairava temple in all directions near the gate, and a temple of Durga or Ganesha in the four directions or the intermediate directions. Similarly, the arrangement of various temples too is stated.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-018   p. 100
The famous east-facing Bijamandal mosque situated in the town must once have been a vast temple, which was later converted into a mosque.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-019   p. 43
Building a stepwell (*vapi*) in the middle of the rampart, let a lotus-pond (*pushkarini*) gate be built.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-020   p. 87
Digging a moisture-free stepwell, building it up all around and to the bottom with thick slabs, [make it] fitted with a firm wooden cage — a three-storeyed [structure] level with the ground, with many chambers by [various] design: an upper storey, a middle storey and a bottom storey. It should have one door, and a mechanized stairway and an underground room or stairway. Above it, on both sides, inside and out, there should be a bolt-fitted veranda built of brick — let a treasury or a palace be built, full of and enclosing various valuables. The Champa stepwell of Mandav is a beautiful example of this.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-021   p. 89
*Sara*, *Mahasara*, *Bhadraka*, *Subhadra*, *Parigha*, *Yugmaparigha* — these are the six kinds of tank. Where there is one heron it is a *parigha*; with two herons, a *yugmaparigha*. A half-moon [tank] is a *sara*; round or circular, a *mahasara*; square, a *bhadraka*; with a Bhadra, a *subhadra*. Of a thousand *dandas* the superior, of five hundred *dandas* the middling, of two hundred and fifty *dandas* the inferior — thus they are counted by the length of the embankment (*pala*). In the width of the embankment, of fifty cubits the superior, half of that the middling, and twelve cubits wide the inferior embankment. Building many stepwells, wells and tanks, festivals should be held.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-022   p. 108
The stepwell is of four kinds — Nanda, Bhadra, Jaya, Vijaya. The one-mouthed, three-terraced (*trikuta*) Nanda is best. The two-mouthed, six-terraced Bhadra is beautiful. The three-mouthed, nine-terraced Jaya is hard even for the gods to obtain. The four-mouthed, sun-terraced (*suryakuta*) Vijaya is all-facing (*sarvatomukhi*).
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-023   p. 109
and *kundas* of various kinds should be built. There are ten wells, four stepwells, four *kundas*, and six kinds of tank.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-024   p. 109
King Bhoj had built, besides the Bhojpur lake, several other tanks too. In Dhar, Ujjain and Mandav, those tanks can still be seen today. Besides these, there were several other tanks in his kingdom too.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-025   p. 111
According to it, the houses of kings were of several kinds. Among them, 135×108 cubits is the finest, 125×100 the second kind, 115×92 the third, 105×84 the fourth, and 95×76 the fifth kind.
C07-RAJABHOJ-EN-026   p. 90
## भोजदेव (Bhojadeva) (BHOJDEV-HI, 12)
घण्टामार्ग के बराबर घण्टामार्ग से बाहर सब ओर विज्जन वत्र (परकोटे) की भूमि स्थापित करें।116
तव महारथ्या के प्रमाण से उस भूमि के बाहर चौड़ी खुदाई में अन्तर के साथ तीन परिखाएँ वनायें 117
खुदाई में आरम्भ से ही तिहाई या आधा छोड़ दें। चौड़ाई में मूल से पूरी तरह वैसी ही हों।118
परिखा की खोदी गयी मिही से अपने भू-भाग पर वत्र बनायें। चकूईदार, गजपृष्ठाकार और गायों के पैरों से घूँदकर दवाया हुआ हो।119
*Gloss [my translation]:* Set the ground for the rampart outside the ghanta-marga all round; beyond it, in proportion to the great road, dig three moats of wide excavation with intervals; leave a third or half unexcavated; the earth dug from the moat should make the rampart on its own ground, curved, elephant-backed and rammed down by the feet of cows.
C07-BHOJDEV-HI-001   p. 46   also Ch 5
हाथ भर ऊँचा कपिशीर्ष (कंगूरा) हो और दो हाथ की काण्डवारिणी हो जो शत्रुओं के वाण रोके 130
द्वार के कर्णान्ति में स्थित कर्णाश्रितों से जुड़े अड्हालक (अटारी-महल) चारों दिशाओं, दिशा-दिशा में प्राकार में वनायें 131
प्राकार की ऊँचाई से विस्तृत दुर्भजिले चरिकोर्ध (चरिक के ऊपर) या उसके आधे शाला अटारियों सहित निर्भम ढार करें 132
अटारियों के बीच परस्पर सी-सी हाथ की दूरी पर यह व्यवस्था हो । इस प्रकार पैदल, अथ्र, रथ, गज के लिए नगर अगम्य हो जाता है 133
*Gloss [my translation]:* A cubit-high kapishirsha (battlement) and a two-cubit kandavarini to stop enemy arrows; build attalakas (watchtowers) joined to the corner supports on the four sides; arranged a hundred cubits apart, the city becomes inaccessible to infantry, horses, chariots and elephants.
C07-BHOJDEV-HI-002   p. 47
इसकी चौड़ाई तीन प्रकार की हो - वड़ा वारह हाथ, मध्यम दस हाथ और कनिष्ठ आठ हाथ चौड़ा हो 126
बड़ा वश सत्रह हाथ डेंचा अच्छा वताते हैं । मध्यम पंद्रह हाथ और अन्तिम तेरह हाथ
डेंचा हो 127
चप्र न सत्रह हाथ से अधिक डेंचा हो और न तेरह हाथ से कम हो ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Its width is of three grades: largest twelve cubits, middle ten, smallest eight; the largest rampart is said to be seventeen cubits high, the middle fifteen, the last thirteen; it should be neither above seventeen nor below thirteen cubits.
C07-BHOJDEV-HI-003   p. 47   also Ch 5
मार्गद्वार वाली चरिका वेदिका व सोपान सहित सुखारोह (आराम से चक्ने योग्य) हो जाती है । उसे निर्दूह (मीनार) सहित तथा कपिशीर्फक (कंगूरे) सहित वनावें 134
*Gloss [my translation]:* The carika with the gate-way becomes easy to walk, with platform and steps; build it with turrets (nirvyuha) and battlements (kapishirshaka).

महारथ्या का द्वार छ:, पाँच, चार, हाथ ऊँचा और डेँह हाथ कम चौड़ा हो 137
सभी महाढारों पर दृष्ण प्रतोली (पोल) हो । उसमें दृष्ण अर्पला (आगल) और इन्क्रीण (इन्धधज या मुख्य खीला) कपाट के परिघ (आड़चा) सहित हो ।38

*Gloss [my translation]:* The gate of the main road should be six, five or four cubits high; every main gate should have a strong pratoli with strong bar and indrakila (main peg) with the door's cross-bar; the pratoli should be like the royal road, extending as a hall.

महादार के ऊपर तीसरा तल वनवाकर जो रोधनढार वाला हर्म्र और परिक्रमा वाला
हो, स्तम्भ-वेदी स्थापित कर उस पर वनवाये - जो व्याल, जाल, शतघ्नी आदि अस्फशन

नगर की वृष्टि (उस्ति), शोभा और संरक्षण के लिए सव और तिमॉजिली पोल सहित विशाल द्वार वनवाने चाहिए । ४७
*Gloss [my translation]:* Above the main gate make a third floor with a barred-door chamber and circumambulation, set on pillars and platform, equipped with vyala, jala, shataghni and the like; for the city's growth, beauty and protection build large gates with three-storeyed portals all round.
C07-BHOJDEV-HI-006   p. 48
जो पुर के प्राकार या परकोटे में द्वार हो वह गोपुर होता है 151-52
प्राकार के बीच-बीच में ऊँचे निकले अडालक और क्लीम (चौवारा या भवनभाग)
उपकार्य (राजभवन) कहलाते हैं 153
खाई की मिही का ढेर और प्राकार-शाला पुरीसंवरण या नगरी का आवरण (डिपाव)
कहलाता है। प्राकार से रक्षक प्राकार या उपनिष्कल कहलाते हैं 154
*Gloss [my translation]:* A gate in the rampart or wall of the city is a gopura; high projecting attalakas and kleema (chauvara or building part) along the rampart are called upakarya (royal building); the heap of moat earth and the rampart-hall are called purisamvarana or covering of the town; guards from the rampart are upanishkala.
C07-BHOJDEV-HI-007   p. 66   also Ch 5
नालियों के जल से पूर्ण अथवा आगामी (वर्ष) या बहने वाले जल से परिपूर्ण विविध कमलों से मनोहर तथा जलनिकासी और मगरों से मनोहर हो 122
इनके आसपास सब दूर फूलदार वृक्षों के उद्यान लगे ही जिनमें गन्ध से पंगलायी भाँरियाँ अपना आवास करने को उत्पुक हो या मधुमक्रिद्धयों के जाले हो 123
*Gloss [my translation]:* They should be filled with water from drains or flowing water, delightful with various lotuses, with outlets and crocodiles; around them gardens of flowering trees all round.

नगर मे शिला और काठ्ठ से ढकी नालियाँ वनवानी चाहिए। प्रदक्षिणा करती ये
जलनालियाँ दो या एक हाथ की हो 152
*Gloss [my translation]:* In the city, drains covered with stone and wood should be made; these water-drains going round should be of two or one cubit.
C07-BHOJDEV-HI-009   p. 49
खेट, उसका आधा विफक्ष्म, उसका आधा ग्राम । पूर (नगर) से एक योजन (प्रायः सोलह मील) दूर खेट (खेड़ा), खेट से ग्राम एक योजन दूर और ग्राम से ग्राम की दूरी एक ग्व्यूति (दो कोस - प्रायः छह किलोमीटर) के बराबर बतावी गयी है 179-80
विषय में दो कोस से सीमा, उसकी आधी पुर की, खेटक में पुर की सीमा से आधी, खेट से आधी ग्राम में 181
पुर में दिशा-मागों पर तीस धनुप पर विकम्प भेता है अर्थात् एक मार्ग से दूसरा मार्ग तीस धनुप (1 नत्व/400 हाथ/ 600 फीट) दूर हो। खेटक में वीस धनुप पर मार्ग और ग्राम में दस धनुप पर बताया गया है 182
*Gloss [my translation]:* Kheta, its half vikrama, its half grama; a kheta lies one yojana (about sixteen miles) from the city, a village one yojana from the kheta, and village to village a gavyuti (two kos, about six km); the boundary of a vishaya is two kos, half of it for a city; in a city roads on the directions are thirty dhanus apart, in a kheta twenty, in a village ten.
C07-BHOJDEV-HI-010   p. 51   also Ch 4
जिस नगर में राजा हो उसे राजधानी समझे । उससे भिन्न (नगर) शाखानगर कहलाने
नगर जैसा शाखानगर ही कर्थत कहलाता है। कुछ कम गुणी कर्थत कमाला
निगम से छोटा ग्राम होता है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* The city where the king resides is to be understood as the capital; other cities are called branch cities; a branch city like a city is called karbata; a village is smaller than a nigama.
C07-BHOJDEV-HI-011   p. 62   also Ch 4
चारों और से छाया चतुःशाल कहलाता है । तीन से जिशाल और दो से डिशाल कहलाता है । एक ओर से छायादार एकशाल और जो एक घर पूरा छाया हो तो वह शाला कहलाती है ।१८-19
शालाओं के मध्य की वावड़ी पुष्करिणी होती है। जिसकी पुष्करिणी ढेंकी हो वह गर्भगृह कहलाता है 120
*Gloss [my translation]:* Roofed on four sides it is called chatuhshala; on three, trishala; on two, dvishala; on one, ekashala; a house wholly roofed is a shala; the pool in the middle of shalas is a pushkarini; one whose pushkarini is covered is called a garbhagriha.

was built upon a good foundation with a terrace.® Often, there
was no arrangement inside the house for the supply of water
which had therefore to be fetched from a common well probably
dug by the king or some other rich person, desirous of acquiring
merit for the next world. There was a small reservoir for collecting
rain water inside the house.

## Samarāṅgaṇasūtradhāra: An Ancient Treatise on Architecture, vol. 2 — Bhojadeva (attrib.) (SAMAR-2, 1)

पुर(ककंट ? कवंट)दुर्गेषु ग्रामेषु नगरेषु चा।१२४।।
Established in individual spots they protect the entire universe. In a Pura \( ^{2} \) (a place containing large buildings surrounded by a ditch and extending not less than one Krośa in length), Karvaṭa \( ^{3} \) (a small market town), in forts, villages \( ^{4} \) and bigger towns.
