# Chapter 8. Religion and the Material Life of Udaypur: Ritual, Agriculture, Trade and Craft
## Jagta Hua Kasba / कथा उदयपुर — English translation (JAGTA-EN, 3)
In some period, some craftsman had carved deities out of stones for some king. Priests had sung their praises for centuries. Every section of society did something or other, in its own way, for their devotion. The gardeners must have tended those gardens from where fresh flowers were brought every morning for the adornment of Neelkanth.
C08-JAGTA-EN-003   ≈ p. 88 (Hindi original)
Santosh Kumar Verma tells us that the provisions of the *akshayanivi* (permanent-endowment) system for the management of religious institutions appear in the Satavahana period. Evidence of their extensive allotment of agricultural land, too, begins to be found — the first mention of which is in the inscriptions of Gautamiputra Satakarni and in the accounts of the Chutu rulers, who gave such donations to the *viharas* of Nagarjunakonda and Amaravati. In the Gupta period their number began to grow, where various Smriti-writers and text-authors mentioned *akshayanivi* in the form of wealth and, in later times, *nidhi*, *nyasa* and *nikshepa*. In the south, too, it was in vogue. After the Gupta period, land-donations were given to temples on a large scale — and that with special privileges such as '*sa-dandashapa-aparadha*' and '*bhutopata-pratyaya*', which was, in one way or another, indeed to some extent a transfer of the absolute exclusive rights of the royal power.
C08-JAGTA-EN-006   ≈ p. 145 (Hindi original)
Now the question: after building so vast a temple as Udaipur — whose construction went on for two decades — what could be the meaning of a king donating to the temple only such a small piece of land as 4 *biswa*? Verma tells us that this seemingly trivial land-donation is not the only example of its kind. In that era, in Rajasthan and Gujarat, in some writings of the Chauhans of Nadol and Shakambhari and of the Chalukyas of Gujarat, there are records of the donation of small parcels of land for religious works.
C08-JAGTA-EN-013   ≈ p. 149 (Hindi original)
## कथा उदयपुर (Jagta Hua Kasba) — Hindi original (JAGTA-HI, 5)
विशाल परिसरों में ये पूजास्थल दरअसल शिक्षा, संस्कृति और कला के विकसित और व्यस्त केंद्र होते थे, जहाँ देवताओं की साक्षी में संस्कारवान नई पीढ़ी गढ़ी जाती थी। राज्य इन्हें पोषित करता था। समाज सहजता था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* These worship places in vast complexes were in fact developed, busy centres of education, culture and art, where a cultured new generation was shaped in the presence of the gods. The state nurtured them. Society preserved them.
C08-JAGTA-HI-006   p. 71
विशाल मंदिरों का निर्माण एक काम था और एक संस्थान के रूप में उनके संचालन की आर्थिक और प्रशासनिक जिम्मेदारियां दूसरा महत्वपूर्ण काम था। नालंदा विश्वविद्यालय के छात्रों और आचार्यों की आर्थिक पूर्ति के लिए दो सौ गांवों का राजस्व, अनाज, फल, फूल और दूध आदि सुनिश्चित किए गए थे। ये एक मुक्त दान राज्य की ओर से इन विशाल परिसरों के संचालन और रखरखाव के काम आते थे, जिसकी निगरानी के लिए अधिकारियों और कर्मचारियों की एक तंत्र काम करता था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Building huge temples was one task, and the economic and administrative responsibilities of running them as institutions were another important task. For the economic needs of Nalanda University's students and teachers, the revenue of two hundred villages, grain, fruit, flowers and milk were assured. These free grants from the state were used to run and maintain these huge complexes, and a system of officers and staff worked to oversee them.
C08-JAGTA-HI-008   p. 145
प्राचीन काल से ही मंदिर और विहार भारतीय समाज की न केवल धार्मिक बल्कि शैक्षिक और सामाजिक गतिविधियों के सक्रिय और समृद्ध केंद्र रहे हैं। इन्हें हमारी सामाजिक व्यवस्था की सबसे पुरानी धुरी भी माना जा सकता है। मंदिरों का निर्माण और उनकी व्यवस्था सार्वजनिक उत्तरदायित्व का विषय रही है, जिसमें राज्य की भूमिका सबसे अहम थी।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Since ancient times temples and viharas have been active and prosperous centres not only of religious but of educational and social activities in Indian society. They can also be considered the oldest pivot of our social system. The construction and management of temples has been a matter of public responsibility, in which the state's role was most important.
C08-JAGTA-HI-009   p. 145
संतोष कुमार वर्मा बताते हैं कि धार्मिक संस्थाओं की व्यवस्था के लिए अक्षयनीवी तंत्र के प्रावधान सातवाहन काल में दिखाई देते हैं। इन्हें व्यापक रूप से कृषि भूमि के आवंटन के प्रमाण भी मिलने लगते हैं, जिनका पहला जिक्र गौतमीपुत्र शातकर्णी के अभिलेखों और चुटू शासकों के विवरण में है, जिन्होंने नागार्जुनकोंडा और अमरावती के विहारों को इस तरह के दान दिए। गुप्त काल में इनकी संख्या में इजाफा होने लगा, जहाँ धन के रूप में अक्षयनीवी और कालांतर में निधि, न्यास और निक्षेप का जिक्र विभिन्न स्मृतिकारों और ग्रंथकारों ने किया। दक्षिण में भी इसका प्रचलन था। गुप्त काल के बाद मंदिरों को बड़े पैमाने पर भूमिदान दिए गए, वह भी ‘सांदंडशपापराध’ और ‘भूतोपातप्रत्याय’ जैसे विशेषाधिकारों के साथ जो कहीं न कहीं राजसत्ता के नितांत एकअधिकारों का कुछ परिमाण में अन्तरण तो था ही।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Santosh Kumar Verma says that provisions of the akshayanivi system for managing religious institutions appear in the Satavahana period. Evidence of allotment of agricultural land also begins to appear widely, first mentioned in the inscriptions of Gautamiputra Satakarni and accounts of the Chutu rulers, who gave such donations to the viharas of Nagarjunakonda and Amaravati. In the Gupta period their number began to increase, where akshayanivi in the form of money and later nidhi, nyasa and nikshepa are mentioned by various smriti and other writers. The practice existed in the south too. After the Gupta period temples were given land grants on a large scale, with privileges like 'sandandashaparadha' and 'bhutopatapratyaya', which was to some extent a transfer of the exclusive rights of royal authority.
C08-JAGTA-HI-010   p. 145
विदिशा के पास प्रसिद्ध उदयगिरि के लेखों में हम चन्द्रगुप्त द्वितीय विक्रमादित्य के सेनापति आत्रककार्दव को काकनादबोट (सांची का पुराना नाम) महाविहार को इस तरह का दान देते हुए देखते हैं। कुमारगुप्त के काल में नालंदा जैसे महाविहार और विश्वविद्यालय की स्थापना में भी इस राजकीय प्रश्रय की भूमिका थी, जिसे हर्ष ने कई सौ गांवों का दान करके विस्तृत करने में योगदान दिया। नालंदा ही नहीं उस दौर के और भी कई विश्वप्रसिद्ध संस्थान इस भूमिदान पर निर्भर थे जैसे पश्चिम में वल्लभी से लेकर दक्षिण में चोल राजाओं के बिग टेम्पल तक। इन दानों को अग्रहार (विद्वान अथवा धार्मिक महत्व के व्यक्तियों को) देवदेय अथवा मठपुर कहा जाता था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* In the inscriptions of the famous Udayagiri near Vidisha we see Atrakakardava, commander of Chandragupta II Vikramaditya, making such a donation to the Kakanadabota (old name of Sanchi) mahavihara. In Kumaragupta's time royal patronage had a role in establishing a mahavihara and university like Nalanda, which Harsha extended by donating several hundred villages. Not only Nalanda, many other world-famous institutions of that era depended on land grants, from Vallabhi in the west to the Big Temple of the Chola kings in the south. These grants were called agrahara (to scholars or persons of religious importance), devadeya or mathapura.
C08-JAGTA-HI-011   p. 146   also Ch 3
## Madhya Pradesh District Gazetteers: Vidisha — Government of Madhya Pradesh (GAZ, 13)
Until the beginning of the present century, a certain rebate on the actual assessed revenue was made in the case of each village. The rebate known as gaonti kharch was utilised to cover the cost of nazars (presents) paid to State Officials, charitable grants to temples, expenses at festivals and the like.
C08-GAZ-001   p. 210
The paramount importance of agriculture in this District is manifest by the very fact that, 75 per cent of the total workers of the whole District were either cultivators or agricultural labourers at the Census of 1961. In actual numbers there were 1,22,626 cultivators (males 87,207, females 35,419) and 51,315 agricultural labourers (males 32,119 females 19,196) out of 2,31,250 total workers (males 1,60,426 females 70,824).
C08-GAZ-002   p. 91
Irrigation does not ever seem to have been conspicuously used in farming in the District. Perhaps the richness of the soil, and numerous streams and rivers intersecting the District acted as a damper on investment in irrigation. Restrictions on poppy cultivation gave a further set-back to the use of irrigation. Irrigation statistics go to show that irrigated area has all along been quite small and has not reached even one per cent of the cropped area.
C08-GAZ-003   p. 94
Vidisha is predominantly a rabi crop area, the proportion of rabi to kharif being 65:35 in the year 1971-72. In the same year 391.8 thousand hactares were devoted to the cultivation of rabi crops and 109.6 thousand hactares to kharif crops. Amongst the crops, wheat is the most important crop, commanding 50 per cent of the cropped area of the District in 1971-72. Next in importance came gram and jowar each of which was responsible for 18 and 8 per cent of the cropped area respectively, in the same year the latter of which is by far the largest kharif crop of the District.
C08-GAZ-004   p. 99
All the operations are carried out with reference to the rain-fall and in the manner in which they are generally performed in all other places of Malwa tract. In Malwa, fields are first broken up and cleared of weeds by means of the bakhar, a sort of harrow or weeding plough. Ploughing (halna) for the autumn crops then commences about the end of May. As soon as sufficient rain has fallen to moisten the soil the field is sown (boni).
C08-GAZ-005   p. 100
As might be expected, the most important means of livelihood in the District in the year 1951 as well as in 1961, was agriculture. Of the total workers in 1951 about 72.95 per cent were engaged in agriculture which increased to 75.22 per cent in 1961.
C08-GAZ-006   p. 183   also Ch 2, Ch 12
The District is predominantly rural. This is evident from the fact that the highest percentage of agriculture in the rural area had been maintained in the proportion of total workers. Cultivation was the main occupation followed by agricultural labour. In the 1951 census this District had the largest number of agricultural labourers in the former Madhya Bharat.
C08-GAZ-007   p. 184   also Ch 12
During the Third Five Year Plan period, there were seven Community Development Blocks in the District. During the period 31,060 quintals of improved seeds, 2,537 quintals of improved seeds other than cereals, 12,760 quintals of chemical fertilizers, together with 2,871 quintals of other fertilizers were distributed. The demonstrations of improved agricultural practice during this period numbered 3,571. Towards the provision of irrigation facilities 972 new kutcha and pucka wells were constructed and 340 old wells were repaired or renovated. Four tanks were repaired. The area of land reclaimed for agricultural purposes was 7,456 hectares.
C08-GAZ-008   p. 196   also Ch 12
In Vidisha where poppy was largely grown, the rate of land revenue ranged from Rs. 14 to Rs. 40 per acre. The current demand for Bhilsa zila was fixed at Rs. 3,41,658 as against the previous demand of Rs. 4,40,827.
C08-GAZ-009   p. 206   also Ch 10
According to the Census of 1961, 79.3 per cent of the total 'workers' in the District were employed either as cultivators (62.7 per cent) or agricultural labourers (16.6 per cent). According to the provisional
Rural Wages and agricultural Labour
figures of 1971 Census, the respective percentage were 52.1 and 26.3.
C08-GAZ-010   p. 215
There were 1,276 leather-cutters, lasters, sewers, etc., (180 females) engaged in shoe-making and other connected jobs. Of these, 492 with 74 females were in the urban sector. The total number of blacksmiths, hammer-smiths and forggmen was 337 with 49 females and 84 out of them (4 females) were in the urban sector.
C08-GAZ-015   p. 180
Out of a total of 1,826 such workers only 42 were females. As villagers largely need a carpenter's service, mainly in connection with the pursuance of their agricultural operations, only 279 of the carpenters (2 females) plied their occupation in the urban sector. All these jobs are pursued conventionally by a particular sections of the population since long past.
C08-GAZ-016   p. 180
Out of the 5,011 working proprietors in the District 4,706 went to retail trade and naturally, due to somewhat … commercial character of this vocation, female participation was quite low (562). About 50 per cent urban component of the retailers numbering 2,266 with 156 female participants, shows that they cater to the daily needs of about 13.14 percent of the District population, while the rest cater for 86 86 per cent of rural population, spread over 7,294 45 sq.

## The Udayeśvara Temple: Art, Architecture and Philosophy of the Śaiva Siddhānta — Anupa Pande (PANDE, 8)
In the epics, especially the Mahābhārata, the cult of Paśupati is one of the five major cults. Paśupati combines in himself the god who not only creates the world, but transcends it, and yet who is the teacher of all spiritual aspirants. Just as Bhāgvatism attracted foreign rulers, so also the Śaiva cult. Wima Kadphises is known to be a devotee of Māheśvara. In the Gupta period, Śaivism is one of the major religious cults. The Purāṇas which are generally held to have been compiled in the Gupta age contain Śaiva mythology and theology in many versions.
C08-PANDE-001   p. 100
Between the 8th and 12th centuries, several distinct schools of Śaiva worship and philosophy came to be formulated. The commentators on Śaṅkarācārya mention four distinct Śaiva schools- Pāśupata, Māheśvara, Śaiva Siddhāntis and Kālāmukhas. Of these, it is the Śaiva Siddhāntis who dominated Central India and the South, i.e. from Malwa to the south. In the 11th century CE, Bhoja Paramāra wrote his Tattvaprakāśa expounding the tenets of Śaiva Siddhānta.
C08-PANDE-002   p. 100   also Ch 4, Ch 5
The Pāśupatas, who were perhaps the most ancient Śaiva cult, are described in the Vedānta Sūtras as clearly dualistic. God is the lord or pati, man is the paśu who is bound by pāśa or fetters. When man is freed from these bonds by the grace of god, he attains a state which is god-like but he does not become god or Śiva himself.
C08-PANDE-003   p. 100
The sources of Pāśupata philosophy are *Pāśupata Sūtras*, *Pañcārthabhāṣya* of Kaundinya, the *Vāyavīyasaṃhitā* of the *Śiva Purāṇa*, *Gaṇakārikā* attributed to Haradatta and its commentary *Ratnaṭīkā* of Bhāsarvajña, and the *Sarvadarśanasaṃgraha* of Mādhavācārya.
The Pāśupatas desired liberation to give them a condition like that of god. This is possible by a knowledge of the five objects or *pañcārtha*. These are *kāraṇa* (cause), *kārya* (effect), *yoga* (spiritual praxis), *vidhi* (religious rituals) and *dukhānta* (cessation of sorrow).
C08-PANDE-004   p. 102
belonged to a Brahmin family of Gujarat. He seems to be a historical figure. The *Pañcārthabhāṣya*, commentary on the *Pāśupatasūtras*, says that he went on foot to Ujjain and delivered his first sermon there.
C08-PANDE-005   p. 102   also Ch 4
*Vidhi* is the behaviour appropriate to virtuous ends. However, these included what seemed to be like antinomian practices, not to harm society but to keep away from it. Thus, the Pāśupatas hold that Lakuliśa himself prescribed bathing (or rather smearing oneself) with ashes thrice a day and sleeping on ashes. This is referred to in the Pāśupata literature and other texts as also inscriptions.
C08-PANDE-006   p. 102
*Upahāra* meant regulations. These practices were of six kinds viz, laughing, singing, dancing, the sound of *huḍḍukāra*, *namaskāra* and *japa*. Laughter meant a loud, bellowing laughter. It is interesting that *gita* refers to the compositions of Māheśvara which are in accordance with the Gāndharva system of music.
C08-PANDE-007   p. 102
Just as for the Southern school of Śaiva Siddhāntins, the *Śivajñānabodham* of Meyakaṇdeva became the authoritative source, the *Tattvaprakāśa* of Bhoja was the most important text
THE UDAYEŚVARA TEMPLE
for the Northern school of Śaiva Siddhāntins. Furthermore, the two commentaries of *Tattvaprakāśa* give to this text a virtually canonical status. The *Sarvadarśanasaṅgraha* of *Mādhavācārya* gives a standard summary of the principles of these schools, relying basically on the *Mrgendrāgama* (which is basically a part of the *Kāmikāgama*) and *Tattvaprakāśa*.
C08-PANDE-008   p. 103–104
## History of the Paramāra Dynasty — Ganguly, D. C. (GANGULY, 22)
Viśvesvára Sambhu was a resident of the village of Púrvagráma, in Rádha. He migrated to Dâhala manḍala, and established a large number of Saiva monasteries in the central and southern India in the 13th c. A. D. Málava Kings are said to have been his disciples.
C08-GANGULY-001   p. 257
Amaresvára, Omkára, Mahákkála, Kálakálesvára, Siddhanátha, Nílakanthésvára, Manesvára, Udaľesvára, Vaidyanátha, and Gohađesvára. \( ^{1} \)  Carciká was the favourite goddess of Naravarman, \( ^{2} \)  and Devapála \( ^{3} \)  has great reverence for Limbáryá.
C08-GANGULY-002   p. 257
\( ^{2} \)  There were numerous Hindu monasteries which fostered the study of the religious scriptures. At the head of each monastery was a superintendent, highly distinguished for learning. In Ujjain there was a Saiva monastery of established repute, called Nātana; the Candikāsrama was a similar one in the same locality, where the following personages successively assumed the office of Superior.
C08-GANGULY-003   p. 258
saintly people belonging to various religions. There he attained the office of Superintendent of Śaiva temples, and wrote a treatise on the doctrine of the cult of Śiva. The Paramāra kings were attracted by his qualities, and became his disciples. Later on, he changed his residence and went to Gujarat, where he became the Superior of the Śaiva monasteries.
C08-GANGULY-004   p. 259
During the whole course of the Paramâra rule, though the Jainas spent much effort in an attempt to further the cause of their faith, they never succeeded in attaining predominance over the Brahmanical religion. Merutunga tells us that when the flag of the Mahâkâla temple was hoisted all those of the Jainas had to be lowered.
C08-GANGULY-005   p. 264
\( ^{3} \) The people worshipped Śiva under various names – viz Koteśvara, Atulanātha, Kanakhalanātha, Śulapāni, Kanakhala Śambhu \( ^{4} \) and Kāseśvara. \( ^{5} \) The Gods Nārāyaṇa, Varuna, Ganesa and the Goddess Sarasvati were also invoked and worshipped.
C08-GANGULY-006   p. 341
The images of all these deities were bathed and anointed with perfumes and incense. The usual ceremonial for worship consisted in sacrificing holy wood, Kuśa grass, sesamum-seed, and rice in the sacred fire. Food and flowers were also offered.
C08-GANGULY-007   p. 257
The people observed many religious ceremonies with great devotion. The most important of these seems to have been the 'Vasantotsava' or the Spring Festival. This was celebrated amidst various amusements, dramas occasionally being acted. \( ^{3} \)  The other ceremonial events of significance were:
Diwāli \( ^{4} \) , Sendhavadeva \( ^{5} \) , Candragrahaṇa (lunar eclipse), Pavitraka \( ^{6} \) , Mahāvaisākha, Sūryagrahaṇa (solar eclipse).
C08-GANGULY-008   p. 259   also Ch 9
(g) The seventh inscription \( ^{2} \) was found broken in a temple of Śiva. It reports that, during the victorious reign of Dhāravarṣa, son of Yaśodhavala and a descendant of Dhumarāja, certain persons, viz. Rāṇā Vaija, son of Vijaisi, and Lasamsīha, Kamaṇa, Śobhā etc., sons of Rāthauda Āna, of the family of the Hathundi Rājputts, took a vow of observing festivity for two days commencing with the day of Mahārātri (Śivarātri). The inscription was issued in Sam. 1274, between Mâgha and Phâlguna=January 1217 A. D., on the occasion of a solar eclipse.
C08-GANGULY-009   p. 317
It was first staged at the court of Dhāravarsa, on the occasion of the investiture of the god Acalesvara with the sacred thread.*
C08-GANGULY-010   p. 329
Rānaka Amma of the Gānga family, the chief officer of the viṣaya, while residing in the above-mentioned village, on the occasion of a solar eclipse, on the new-moon day of Caitra, granted certain pieces of land at Mahisabuddhikā, in the holy tārtha of Kālakālesvara, to the temple of Munisuvrata, which was situated in the Śvetapāda country. The inscription also mentions the donation of two oil-mills, fourteen shops, fourteen drammas, etc., for the benefit of the same temple.
C08-GANGULY-011   p. 100
It records that the Pattakila Janna, the son of the Pattakila Câhila, who was born in the family of Tailika, built a temple of Śiva and a tank in Cirihilla between Câdâghansa Kûpikâ and Vruvasaka. He promised to make an annual gift of four palas of oil and a lump of sweetmeat in the ceremony of Sendhavadeya.
C08-GANGULY-012   p. 150
In the year Sam. 1191=1134 A. D., on the occasion of the annual funeral ceremony of his deceased father Naravarman, Yasovarman, from the imperial city of Dhârâ, granted the two villages of Vadauda and Uthavanaka to the householder Dhanapâla, a Karnata Brahman, whose grandfather Thakkura Mahirasvâmin came to Ujjain from the place called Adrelav(i)-ddhâvarî.
C08-GANGULY-014   p. 178
Its object is to record that Hariscandra, having bathed in the holy water of the Narmada, near the temple of the four-faced Mārkanḍesvara, granted two shares of the registered rents of the village of Palasavāḍā to the learned Brahman Dāsaratha, son of the learned Sindhu. It is further stated that, on the full moon of Vaiśākha of the same year, he gave the remaining shares of the above village, with the addition of the shares of both customary dues from the bazaar below the fort of Gunapura, to the Brahman Malvinu, son of the learned Delu.
C08-GANGULY-017   p. 197
(ii) The second inscription \( ^{2} \) registers the fact that Arjunavarman, on the 15th Āsādhā, having bathed at the holy place of Somavatī, entrusted to the charge of his family-priest, Govinda, the temple of Dandādhi-pati, in the city of Mahākāla.
C08-GANGULY-018   p. 211
(a) A stone inscription, \( ^{x} \) found in a shed near a temple at Kayadra, Sirohi States, records that on Saturday, the 15th half of the bright half of Jyestha, Sam. 1220 = May, 1163 A. D., Mahârâjâdhirâja Mahâmanada-lésvara Dhârâvarsadeva granted a remission of taxes on the village of Phulahâl, belonging to Bhattâraka Devesvara, of the temple of Kâsesvara.
C08-GANGULY-019   p. 315
when Bhimadeva (II) was on the throne of Anahilapura and Mahâmandalesvara Râjakula Somasimha, son of Dhârâvarşa, was ruling in Mount Abu, Tejahpâla, the minister of Vitradhavala, built in the village of Deulavâdâ, on the top of Mount Abu, this temple of the holy Neminâtha, called Lûna-simhavasahikâ. C08-GANGULY-024   p. 356   also Ch 10
The country was very wealthy. Besides the dwelling quarters in every village, there were fields for growing crops, pasture-lands for cattle, and woods which produced fuel. There were markets in the villages and cities, which contained the houses of traders. ## Corpus Inscriptionum Indicarum, Vol. VII, Part 2: Inscriptions of the Paramāras — Trivedi, Harihar Vithal (ed.) (CII, 46)
This is followed by a prose portion introducing the Paramabhaṭṭaruka, Mahārājādhvāja and Paramēśvara Dēvapāla, who was “endowed with everything auspicious and resplendent with the epithet pañcha-mahā-sabda”, who was a devout worshipper of Mahēśvara and who was ruling at Dhārā, gloriously because of the grace of the goddess Limbārvā, who may be taken to have been a local deity.¹ Then the record provides the date with details: it is Saturday, the fifth of the bright half of Mārgaśirsha of the (Vikrama) year 1275, which, according to kielhorn, corresponds to Saturday, the 24th November, 1218 A.C.² Then is mentioned the object of the inscription, which is sectarian: it is to record the construction of a temple of Sambhu by one Kēśava, who belonged to the mercantile community and who was a brother of Dhala, a son of Bilhaṇa and a grandson of Dōsī, a resident of Undapura. Kēśava also excavated a tank near the temple which was on the northeastern side of the place and near it also put up images of Hanumat, Kshētrapāla, Ganēśa, Krishna and others, Nakulīśa and Ambikā (vv. 6-11).

Following this, it mentions Chēṭaka who was the servant of the office of religion of the God Śiva (Chandūśa), and then the supreme teacher of the Pāśupatas, who was Jāvala.
C08-CII-002   p. 478
The inscription is important in that it shows the prevalence of the Pásupata sect of Śaivism in the locality.

The practice of measuring land by a bamboo-rod is quite common even to-day in Mālwā, as in many other parts of the country.
C08-CII-036   p. 170

Arahata (or araghatta) is often, as taken here by Sastri to be a ‘Persian wheel,’ but I find nothing like Persian in it. It only means, literally, the pots attached to aras, i.e., spokes or radius of a wheel (to lift water), as we find used in Rajasthān (for irrigation) even to-day.

## The Cultural Heritage of Madhya Bharat — Patil, D. R. (MADHYABHARAT, 7)
It is, however, interesting to find that of all 750, or so, temples so far recorded in Madhya Bharat, leaving aside about 180 sanctuaries, of which we do not know the sect to which they were sacred, nearly 240 are Shaiva temples, which is a fact of considerable importance in the religious history of Madhya Bharat.
C08-MADHYABHARAT-001   p. 72
Shaivism had even gone a step further and evolved a monastic order credited to one of its celebrated sects called the Mattamayura, "the mad peacocks", whose habitats or monasteries are still seen at Kadwaha, Terahi and Ranod in Guna district. At Surwaya in Shivapuri district also there exists a similar monastery though it is not known, for certain, whether it belonged to this Shaiva sect.
C08-MADHYABHARAT-002   p. 73
But as compared with the overwhelmingly large number of Shaiva shrines, those in honour of the god Vishnu are quite small in number totalling hardly 87 in all. In fact this number does not compare favourably even with the 89 Jain shrines in the whole of the State.
C08-MADHYABHARAT-003   p. 74
The monastery is a little interesting for the history of Shaivism in these parts, for the inscription found there clearly suggests that it was built for the ascetics of the Shaiva sect known by the name of Mattamayura or "Mad Peacock" the significance of which is not, however, quite clear. The widespread influence of this sect is attested by similar records and monasteries being found in the territories of Madhya Pradesh also.
C08-MADHYABHARAT-004   p. 104
Khokhai Math.—Though now used as a temple, this building was originally a monastery of Shaiva ascetics as is recorded in the large Sanskrit inscription on it, and confirmed by the form of the structure. The inscription tells us that the monastery was built by Purandara, a religious preceptor of king Avantivarman, and was extended by Vyomashiva, a disciple in the fourth generation from the original builder. It is recorded that Vyomashiva also built the tank Chopda which is seen still in front of the monastery, and around it a number of temples (which no longer exist).
C08-MADHYABHARAT-005   p. 105
The line of Shaiva ascetics to which the builders of this monastery belonged, appears to have been very well-known and influential. They have left inscriptions at various places, e. g. at Siyadoni near Lalitpur, Bilhari near Jubbulpore, Chandrehe near Rewa and a fourth inscription now in the Archaeological Museum at Gwalior. Monasteries very similar to Khokhai now existing in various states of preservation at Surawaya, Kundalpur, Kadwaha and Terahi in this part of the country, are probably works of these same ascetics.
C08-MADHYABHARAT-006   p. 105
The most ancient of these industrial crafts which has struggled to live, though in a much altered form, is the Art of Stone Carving in northern Madhya Bharat. The numerous temples built in this region in the medieval period must have provided a useful occupation to the class of stone carvers or craftsmen, now called Patharphod. With the advent of Islam, by the 12th century, the art of carving, especially the making of the stone … images, must have received a fatal blow so that these craftsmen were now forced to orientate their craft to some other direction.
C08-MADHYABHARAT-009   p. 61–62
## Temple Economics (Arthavyavasthā of Mandirs), Volume I — Singh, Sandeep (TEMPLE-ECON, 43)
Closely connected with the temples, there were many Vaishnava and Saiva mathams which were both teaching institutions and their educative influence was very great, especially in the Vedas and Sastras and the Puranas. The 'mathams', were important religious institutions They were encouraged by the State and possessed a vast amount of wealth'. There was a religious head for each 'matham' who was highly respected. Generally, every matham stood for the propagation of a particular school or religion and encouraged teaching and the spreading of knowledge in religious lore. Many pious gifts were made to these institutions by the rulers as well as the people and these 'mathams' figured as important adjuncts of the temples for the preservation of traditional culture.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-001   p. 17
The Mathas of southern India were extraordinary multi-purpose institutions. On one hand, they were educational institutes attached to temples, in which monks and students lived together. On the other, they served as rest houses for travellers including pilgrims and musicians, and gave food to anyone who walked in. Some of them had hospitals to take care of the sick. There are inscriptions indicating that even sick animals were treated in some Mathas by physicians trained in veterinary medicine. The monks who taught here were well-versed in various branches of learning. Many Mathas were equipped with libraries and preserved manuscripts. Thus, these public institutions rendered a variety of services while promoting learning. They were funded by kings or communities or philanthropists.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-002   p. 126
The Veda Agama School is functioning from 1959, covers the subjects related to the temple pooja service, performance of Tamil Archana, Conduct of Kumbhabhishekama and other festivals. A five-year intensive training is given to the students with the provisions of food, dress, accommodation and medical aid, free of charge. At the end of the fifth year the student is awarded with the title called "Agamamani". The present strength is 25 and so far, 71 persons have been issued diplomas.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-003   p. 19
For instance, the special feature of Tirumalai is the offering of wealth which takes the form of dropping cash or jewels in the hundi. In Palani, people carry and offer kavadi. At Rameswaram, the bathing of the deity with water brought from the Ganges is considered sacred.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-004   p. 30
Food offerings require good harvests, which in turn, require high agricultural activity. The food, after it is offered to Bhagwan is distributed among assembled devotees irrespective of caste, creed, gender, age and social status. The sacred food or prasadam is, in fact, a happy event of daily feeling the community to take care of each other as human beings with divine guidance. The purpose of our ancestors in setting up this beautiful ritual is thus achieved when the devotee gets the blessings of God in the form of the prasadam.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-005   p. 53
The Spirit behind remunerating the pujari as recommended in the agama is one of honoring and making happy rather than payment for his service. In Vastudevabali, the acharyalaksana specifies that the acharya should be happy and content and honoured with dakshina. In fact, here the agama is quite visual in its description and brings forth a scenario of the acharya seated on an appropriate seat, wearing all five specified ornaments, with all five specified ornaments, with a headgear and topcloth, surrounded by similarly attired murtipas accompanied by the chanting of veda, storta and mantra. He has been properly honoured with dakshina, to gladden his heart. In the Vyadhinasanavidanavidhi, the adisaiva officiant who conducts the prescribed ritual for either healing or protection is to be honoured with appropriate dakshina, to gladden his heart. The agama declares that if the acarya is happy, everything moving and stationary becomes happy.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-006   p. 160
A mandir is the abode of the devata, who has been invoked through prana pratishtha during the mandir's consecration. After consecration, the Devata lives in the murti and the mandir is His/ Her abode.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-007   p. 191
“Though the construction of temples and the provision for worship therein by grants of lands seem to be common, there is a vast amount of epigraphical evidence to show that temple charities were not exclusively meant for ceremonials in the temple but also for scientific’ (sastraic) and religious (Vedic) education. The South Indian temples served the purposes of educational institutions.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-008   p. 16
It is the Deity around whom the entire fabric of Nathadwara's religion and economy rotates. The temple provides work to as many as 1000 families supporting at least 5000 souls. The rest of the population subsists on industries which are indirectly associated either with various functions, feasts and festivals of the temple or fulfil the needs of the pilgrims. Thus, the origin, evolution and entire prosperity of the town can be traced, in part, to the shifting of the idol to Nathdwara and in part to the pilgrims who visit the shrine.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-009   p. 16
In brief the great temples of South India were public ‘institutions’ representing the collective energy of the state rather than monuments for the king’s personal glorification. They had civic as well as religious uses; they fulfilled the purposes of a town-hall, college and technical school. The people met in the temple porches to elect representatives for local bodies, to listen to sacred music, recitations or plays.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-010   p. 17
One of the Tiruvorriyur inscriptions states that as many as 60 velis were given for the maintenance of a mandapa built in the temple, for the upkeep of the teachers and pupils who studied grammar in that hall, and, for the worship of the god Sivadharman and Siddhanta were also taught. In a stone inscription from the Venkatesa Perumal temple at Tirumukkudal near Conjeevaram it is stated that besides a hospital, the temple maintained a hostel and college. An epigraph' of the time of Rajendara Chola I at Ennayiram (South Arcot District) gives many interesting details to show that temples of South India served the purpose of educational institutions. This inscription is of great importance for it shows clearly that in ancient temples not only was the regular conducting of worship maintained but also the study of the Vedas, philosophy, grammar and other sciences, and in a few cases, provision was made for a few persons versed in the Vedas.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-011   p. 17   also Ch 6
Revenue of the temple was Rs. 114.49 crores in 2012, an Rs. 111.12 crores in 2011. Temple entry ticket collection alone rose to Rs 25 crore, 2 per cent up from previous year.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-012   p. 18
Students were provided end to end support, that is from grant to hostel facility. Professional artists were supported through grants. Community decisions were taken in the mandir. Mathams were integral part of mandir in all these initiatives. Apart from religious function, mandirs were responsible for social and civic duties. Undoubtedly the arthavyavastha of the city revolved around the mandirs.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-013   p. 18
It is definite that in the past, the temple stood by people in their hour of need. The temple met the need of the day by assuming the role of a money lender and financed people in their various endeavours
The mandir is multifaceted as it works not only as an employer, consumer or lender. In the past, the mandir worked as an assembly point where people gathered to resolve their disputes or to celebrate marriage.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-014   p. 20
Some letters written to the pradhan (president) of the temple Shri Jwalamukhi for lending money have been found. This request was not only by the priestly class but
even the local people of other castes requested to the Prabandhkarini committee, Shri Jwalamukhi, to lend them a certain amount.
A letter shows that a request was made to the Prabandhkarini committee, to lend him Rs 200 for the treatment of his daughter by a fellow named Madan Lal whose profession and caste are not known and his request was taken into consideration.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-015   p. 20
The daily routine, especially of the larger mandirs, gave constant employment to numbers of priests, choristers, musicians, dancing-girls, florists, cooks and many other classes of servants.
The periodical festivals were occasions marked by fairs, contests of learning, wrestling matches and every other form of popular entertainment.
Schools and hospitals were often located in the mandir precincts and it also served often as the town hall where people assembled to consider local affairs or to hear the exposition of sacred literature.
The large endowments in land and cash bestowed on each mandir by successive generations of pious donors tended to make it at once a generous landlord and a banker whose aid was always available to those that needed it.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-016   p. 26
Cash endowments, which amounted to several thousands of kasus, were loaned to numerous villages assemblies at rates of interest fixed in kind or money, and generally about 12 per cent per annum.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-017   p. 26
According to Dr S P Sabharatnam Sivacharyar, the activities to be undertaken in mandirs as mentioned in Suprabhedagama, Vijayagama, Analagama, Prodigtagama and so on are:
1. Arrange for the systematic study of the Veda-s and the Agama-s
2. Establish schools, from the lower to the higher levels
3.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-018   p. 32
Each of them had a mandir orchestra from the days of the Chola Emperors who had laid down that gita, vadya and nritta as part of the 16 upacharas were to be done daily to the main deities in the mandir on the chief occasions of worship. Gita-vadya was instrumental music and Tevaram songs were refreshingly sweet. The nadasvaram orchestra was played in the temple on all occasions of daily worship, which were six in major mandirs and at least three in small minor mandirs. The orchestra consisted of a minimum of four players and it might be much larger. The Cholas had made many grand grants for the maintenance of these instrument players, to enable them to lead a contented life with the grants, without waiting for outside patronage from any individual.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-019   p. 40
For example, an inscription from the time of Rajendra Chola I (11th century) lays out the endowments given for the boarding and tuition of 340 students studying at a Vedic college. The college received 45 velis (300 acres) of land. Each student of Veda was noted to cost 6 Nalis of paddy per day and \( \frac{1}{2} \) Kalanju of gold per year. A student studying the more advanced Vedanta, Mimamsa or Vyakarana got 66% more. Meanwhile, a teacher was noted to receive a meal allowance equivalent to that of 16 students per day.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-020   p. 42
The royal grants were of two kinds: Sarvamanya and Devadana. In the first type, the entire village was given to the temple and the second type specified that all taxes payable to the king were to be collected and enjoyed by the mandir.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-021   p. 43
Food Centre: Feeding the poor and deserving persons on the occasion of feasts and festivals was considered an act of piety. A portion of the prasadams offered to the deity was distributed to the devotees in the mandir without any class distinction, as it is being done every day even now in the Tirumalai mandir. Royal grants as well as private endowments sustained this service. In certain Vaishnava mandirs, separate feeding houses like the Ramanuja-Kutams were attached to the mandir with specific endowment for feeding the pilgrims and desantris.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-022   p. 44
From inscription it appears that a lucrative prasadam trade began in the mandir as the quantity of daily offerings returned to the donor and the mandir functionaries far exceeded what could be consumed by them. Pilgrims visiting the mandir also desired to receive the prasadam for themselves and their family members who could not attend the mandir. From inscriptional evidence, it appears mandir functionaries and donors would turn their prasadam shares over to a group of 11 vendors (prasadakkar) who would subsequently sell the prasadam to visiting pilgrims. On average, it appears that a typical donation would return a total annual return of \(10\%\) (split as described above: \(25\%\) to the donor and \(75\%\) to mandir functionaries). The prasadakkar would pay a lease fee to the mandir functionary or donor in exchange for the ability to sell the prasadam to pilgrims at a profit. Based on inscriptions designating the right of the donor to transfer their prasadam share to a prasadakkarar, the prasadam trade was not illegal nor did it have negative connotations.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-023   p. 53
The Matham is a socio-economic entity. There are many cottage industries within a Matham, using the produce of its farmlands. The local people are engaged in this micro-economy. They till the land of the Matham which is given to them on lease. They grow produce for themselves, the Matham and for selling in the market. The Matham combines agriculture, industry, and trade, based on indigenous methods.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-024   p. 72
People give charity to these Mathams in money in kind. The Mathams get charity from the rich as well as the poor. In the past, kingdom the administrative authorities also gave grants to the mandir or the Mathams.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-025   p. 73
The temple also owned cultivable land which was leased out and revenues were earned. By this method the temple was sustaining the maintenance of the temple and able to also support the needful during poverty and emergency. Temple provided means of livelihood for a large number of persons and greatly influenced the economic life of the community. The written evidences of Brihadeshwar temple, Tanjavur (1011 AD) shows that the temple had 600 employers.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-026   p. 107
Although many mandir in the past offered land, a house, provisions, and a share of prasad to musicians, only a handful of wealthy mandir provide these privileges to musicians today.
The monthly salary to the musicians is meagre, except at a handful of wealthy mandir.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-027   p. 113
They constructed new mandirs and maintained surviving mandirs.
"Innumerable inscriptions from Chola times tell us of the philanthropy of the devadasis. They created new shrines, rebuilt old and crumbling ones, plated some of the roofs with cooper, gold, and brass. They commissioned master sculptors to make gold and bronze idols of gods, goddesses, and saints to be installed in mandirs. To conduct the worship of these idols, they also gifted rice and paddy. Part of their devadana was agricultural land. Sometimes they donated entire villages to make sure that the mandir was well maintained. Devadasis who had specific roles to play in the festivals and processions of mandirs donated lands to ensure the continuation of these celebrations. The wealthier devadasis donated jewels, lamps, plates, and bells made of slid gold for mandir service. To ensure that lamps remained perpetually lit in mandirs, they donated lands which yielded crops. Whenever natural calamities like cyclones and storms damaged mandir goruprams devadasis contributed money for their reconstruction. Their generosity extended to feeding brahmins and pilgrims.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-028   p. 131
A nineteenth century mural painting based on the life story of Manikka Nachiyar was seen until recently on the walls of the Tiruvarur mandir." The esteem in which devdasis were held can only be imagined when one reads the following inscription: "This gift of lands shall be enjoyed by the devadasi Muthu and her successors as long as the Sun and the Moon exist, and such lands will not be liable to any form of taxation. Furthermore, they are entitled to receive 'Rama Prasadam' (cooked food from the temple) every day of the year.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-029   p. 132
When Jaziya was imposed on the pilgrims visiting Varanasi, a Hoysala King of Carnatak, Nrisimha
III, donated in 1279 A.D. a village with a revenue of 645 Nishkas, to provide money for the payment of this tax by the residents of Carnataka, 'Telen- gana, Talvi, Tithut and Gauda countries, and for the worship of Visvesvara.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-030   p. 142
The Bhartiya Princes provided not only employment to learned Pandits in their own courts but also gave agricultural land and residential houses to scholars in Varanasi. They also established Chhatras here where any and every Brahman student got free food.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-031   p. 145
Brahmins who ran the temple were also responsible for economic activities in that region. Records also show that priests guided the farmers with their weather prediction based on astronomy and observation of monsoon pattern etc. They also decided the time of sowing, selected seeds, ensured crop rotation, and managed water resources as per seasonal requirements.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-032   p. 158
For example, mandir properties: Tamil Nadu mandirs, under Hindu Religious & Charitable Endowments Department (HR & CE), has control over more than 4.7 lakh acres of agricultural land, 2.6 crore square feet of buildings and 29 crore square feet of urban sites of mandirs. By any reasonable measure, the income from these properties should be in thousand of crores of rupees. The government, however, collects a mere Rs. 36 crore in rent against a 'demand' of mere Rs. 304 crore around 12 per cent realisation.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-033   p. 178
Mandirs were not only place for dharmic and spiritual activity and art related to it but were also centre of education, science, wealth creation as banker, land owner etc. Different facets of mandirs not only contribute enormously to wealth creation but also to the knowledge creation across all possible fields and to the society.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-034   p. 210
It is important to recall that one of the functions of the brahmin at the mandir was to advise the farmers about which crop to grow.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-035   p. 36
The village of Vikramadityamangala was next mentioned in an inscription of 1495 when it was recorded that Kandadai Ramanuja Ayyangar, the agent for the endowments of the emperor Saluva Narasimha and an important teacher and official at the mandir, granted 6,500 panam for food offerings to the major deity Sni Venkatesvara and two minor deities. Of the 6,500 panam, it was provided that 1,300 panam was to be used for the excavation of an irrigation channel in the mandir village of Vikramadityamangala, 'and with the yield obtained by means of the said irrigation channel, all the necessary articles (for the food offerings) shall be supplied from the Shri Bhundaram (temple treasury)'.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-036   p. 43
The system of lease also prevailed side by side. In this, the mandir was entitled for the melvaram or owner's share of the produce. The owner-cultivator ratio that prevailed in CE. 1535 was 75:25. But the same record informs us that during a severe drought, the mandirs' share was reduced to two thirds.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-037   p. 43
One might be surprised that one not only makes money by supplying fresh flowers to the mandir but also make money from the same flowers after they have done their duty. There are entrepreneurs, NGOs who collect flowers from the mandirs for recycling. They make incense stick, design textiles using these flowers. Not only they recycle the flowers but also provide employment to hundreds of individuals while generating profit for themselves.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-038   p. 38
Our Mandirs need variety of commodities and services to fulfil day to day activities. This demand goes up drastically during special puja or utsav. These commodities range from cereals, vegetables, fruits, spices etc. to milk, honey, ghee, oil etc. Besides these, they need sandal wood and paste, kumkum etc.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-039   p. 44
Temples across India have always encouraged art. For centuries now, muralis (paintings on rock or stone walls and ceilings), art on palm leaves, papier maché, textiles, exquisite metal crafts and other handicrafts have been made by artisans who lived around the temples. Much of the religious art, until date, is inspired by the deities and the legends of the gods and goddesses of temple towns.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-040   p. 54
This was however a general nomenclature with a much wider connotation covering a variety of skills and occupations besides the conventional handicraft. In fact, in its first appearance, in the Brahmanas, it stands for works of art. In the Kausitaki Brahmana the term is extended even further to cover dance, drama and music. Silpa has also been defined in some texts as synonymous with the Vedic concept of karu a term which stands for a 'maker', covering a wide range of performers, an artisan, a singer, a poet. No difference or distinction was made between the various forms of expression and embraced a wide range of skilled activity from music to horsemanship, painting to cookery, weaving to magic show, all being regarded as equal. In course of time this acceptance of all creative force as an integrated power emerging from a single source, became defied as Viswakarma - the maker of the universe. The making of things was therefore a noble act, the object a manifestation of Viswakarma. The tradition, thus, grew of offering a light and flowers to Viswakarma or a deity with a prayer before starting on any operation by a craftsman, for it was an offering to the deity as a sign that he, the worker-maker, was going to enter into communion with the divine spirit.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-041   p. 111
Melas promote the circulation and integration of goods within the wider economic system by allowing the marketing of manufactured city goods in rural areas, and vice versa. Several melas, such as those at Pushkar and Sonepur, function as large regional livestock markets where bovines, camels and horses are traded. Such melas allow for the redistribution of livestock and determination of their market value.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-042   p. 117
"Benares in the North-Western Provinces is the first city in Bharat for the multitude and excellence of its cast and sculptured with images and emblemata, not only in Brass and Copper, but in Gold and Silver, and also in Wood and Stone and Clay. These images of the Gods are not made by a separate caste, but the carpenters and the masons respectively make the large wooden and stone idols set up in the mandir, the potters the clay murti consumed in daily worship, and the braziers, coppersmiths, and goldsmiths the little images in Brass and Copper, mixed metal, and Gold and Silver which are always kept in private houses.
C08-TEMPLE-ECON-043   p. 147

be made to the superb stone terraces around the sacred tanks
which drew the enthusiastic admiration of so gifted a critic
as Alberuni.'^

## King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture (Raja Bhoj aur Parmarkaleen Nagar Sthapatya) — English translation — Bhagwatilal Rajpurohit (RAJABHOJ-EN, 12)
The synthesis of astrology and religious law, the synthesis of architecture and machines, the synthesis of life and water, the synthesis of principle and practice, and finally the synthesis of the various contemporary religions. His saying is that one should listen to the doctrines of the Buddhists, act according to the Jains, conduct oneself according to the Vedics, and meditate upon Lord Shiva.
C08-RAJABHOJ-EN-001   p. 15   also Ch 4
> श्रोतव्यः सौगतो धर्मः कर्तव्यः पुनराहितः ।
> वैदिको व्यवहर्तव्यो ध्यातव्यः परमः शिवः ॥
>
> *(Sanskrit verse: "The doctrine of the Buddha should be heard; that of the [Jain] should be practised; the Vedic should be followed in conduct; and the Supreme Shiva should be meditated upon.")*
*Gloss [my translation]:* The Buddha's doctrine should be heard; that of the Jain should be practised; the Vedic should be followed in conduct; the Supreme Śiva should be meditated upon.
C08-RAJABHOJ-EN-002   p. 16
On seeing an image at the doorway, Dr. Bhandarkar wrote that it was a Jain temple, but in truth that image was of the upward-seeded (*urdhva-retas*) Lakulisha.
C08-RAJABHOJ-EN-003   p. 47
> *(Sanskrit verses — in sense: "The doctrine of the Buddha should be heard; that of the Jain [restored/practised]; the Vedic followed in conduct; and the Supreme Shiva meditated upon. The seeker of compassion should resort to Jainism; the seeker of delight, to the Kaula view; [the seeker of right] conduct, to the Vedas; and the seeker of liberation, to the Formless One. One should avoid the reviling of and hatred toward the gods of others too; by the grace of all the gods one may obtain the supreme fortune.")*
C08-RAJABHOJ-EN-004   p. 188
King Bhoj, though a Shaiva, respected the various current contemporary faiths. According to him, one should listen to the doctrines of the Buddhists, follow the conduct of the Jains, let one's behaviour be according to the Vedics, and let one's meditation be on Shiva. According to him, one should avoid the reviling of, or hatred toward, the gods of others; by the grace of all the gods one should attain the supreme fortune. Jainism gives compassion; the Kaula [gives] delight (*rasa*); the Veda, [right] conduct; and the Formless One (*niranjana*), liberation.¹ This is the folk-religion (*loka-dharma*) of the royal ethic.
C08-RAJABHOJ-EN-005   p. 188
In front of the platform (*jagati*) there should be a small mound (*bhitta*); at the spring festival, hold swings here. Facing the deity there should be covered dance-halls; they are auspicious in the east, southeast or northwest. In the southeast direction there should be a sacrifice-pavilion (sacrificial hall); in it there should be a building-plot and an altar. In the south there should be a monastery; behind it, an ascetics' meditation-hall.
C08-RAJABHOJ-EN-006   p. 106
In the inscription of 878 CE found at Mahalghat, Bhelsa, in a hymn to the Sun, it is mentioned that two bazaars of Bhailasvami had been given out on rent, and from them a rent of 130 *panchiyaka dramma* came in; this was donated to the Sun temple.
C08-RAJABHOJ-EN-007   p. 60
Let there be given to the officiating priests (*ritvik*), teachers, chaplains and Vedic scholars (*shrotriya*) *brahmadeya* grants (of donation or land) fit for hereditary use, free of fine and tax. To the cowherds, the *sthanika* (officer of a quarter of the fort), the elephant-trainer skilled in elephant-lore, and the horse-courier, let regions be given without the right of sale or mortgage.
To the tax-paying cultivators, land should be given for one generation; then let it revert to the state.
C08-RAJABHOJ-EN-008   p. 84
In the east, south and north, market-streets (*hatha-marga*, bazaars) should be made. In the northeast, craftsmen, washermen and cloth-printers (*chhipak*, *chhipa*) should dwell. Leather-workers and *varuta* [should be settled] in the southeast, distillers (*kalala*) in the southwest, and weavers (*tantuvaya*, *julaha*) in the northwest. Beyond this, markets of goods for buying and selling should be made, where the crowds of all people can come and go with ease. Goldsmiths, [dealers in] perfume, perfumers (*gandhika*), ivory-work, and grain-houses should be in the east.
C08-RAJABHOJ-EN-009   p. 28   also Ch 7
Commercial towns should be founded for the import and export, and the water- and land-trade, of goods — at the centres of minerals, [precious] substances, elephant-forests, cattle-stations, and on the merchant-routes.
C08-RAJABHOJ-EN-010   p. 85
In the west direction let the wool-workers, weavers, and the craftsmen who make bamboo, leather, armour (*varma*, shield), weapon-sheaths (scabbards) and so on, and Shudras, dwell. In the northwest direction, shops, hospitals, dispensaries; in the northeast part, the treasury, cattle and horses. Beyond that, in the north, let the town-deity, the royal deity, the iron-and-gem craftsmen, and Brahmins dwell. In the unbuilt places without buildings, or in enclosures, let guilds (*shreni*) and groups of foreign traders dwell.
C08-RAJABHOJ-EN-011   p. 88   also Ch 7
In the northeast there should be craftsmen; beyond them, washermen and cloth-printers. In the southeast direction there should be leather-workers and *varuta* (basket-makers); in the southwest, liquor-sellers; and in the northwest, weavers. On the rampart and everywhere in the town there should be thread-machines (spinning-wheels).

## The Temple Architecture of India — Adam Hardy (HARDY, 7)
The earlier, that of the Kapalikas ('skull men', because they drank from skulls), stemmed from cremation ground asceticism, and involved appeasing the ferocious form of Shiva (Bhairava) or of the Goddess (Kali) with blood sacrifice, and offerings of forbidden substances and acts known as the 'five Ms' (matsya, fish; mamsa, meat; mudra, grain; mada; wine; maithuna, sex). Magical powers were obtained in return. From around the 9th century there emerged movements referred to as 'Kaula' ('clan') cults, whose adherents conceived of the processes of emission
C08-HARDY-001   p. 54
There are many different Shaiva sects, the oldest known being that of the Pashupatas, its teachings attributed to Shiva via an incarnation, the sage Lakulisha (2nd century AD). The esoteric, proto-Tantric Bhairava cult of the Kapalikas is another early form.
C08-HARDY-002   p. 56
The foundation and endowment of temples played a central role in the development of state and society. Temples became social and educational centres, and important economic institutions – landowners, employers, moneylenders and dispensers of charity. They were a canvas for the visual arts, a stage for the performing arts. By the end of this period the great temple complexes in south India could have hundreds of employees, from priests and administrators to masons, dancers, cooks and potters.
C08-HARDY-003   p. 17
Inscriptions (usually engraved on copper plates) record vastly more donations of villages with attendant land, tax free, to groups of Brahmins than to feudatories or state officials. Around a capital, Brahmin settlements provided the state with administrative and ideological specialists \( ^{14} \) and may have created a kind of ‘demilitarised zone’, since any potential usurper hoping to maintain legitimacy would have to respect them.
C08-HARDY-004   p. 32
Apart from the royal family and officials such as ministers and generals, temples were increasingly founded and supported by mercantile and artisanal guilds. Inscriptions show that all kinds of people gave to temples; they might donate á field or a water tank, or fund a perpetual lamp, or give two sheep to supply milk to make ghee to keep a lamp burning.
C08-HARDY-005   p. 32
Nagadeva, the inscription continues, assigned the village of Sivunur to Someshvara, a Brahmin votary of Nageshvara. He piously assembled an estate, defined by the Great River, and certain trees and stones, such as the Great Hog's stone, carefully apportioning the income accruing from it to the various needs of the temple. Oblations were provided for, and food for the ascetics, and repair of broken masonry – an income was assigned to a stone-cutter named Candoja. There must have been an attached matha or monastery, as the youths studying there are mentioned, and there was income for their teachers, as well as the ‘professors lecturing to the ascetics’ and the Brahmin officiants. There was generous provision for the public women – including the one ‘acting for the god’s enjoyment’ (who earned more than anyone), the four fan-bearing ones, the four who graced the four pillars of the temple, to the right and the left, and the four dancers – as opposed to the 400 famously assigned to the Brihadeshvara by Rajaraja Chola.
C08-HARDY-006   p. 37
Of the four roles referred to in the previous quotation, \( ^{4} \) the first was that of the sthapati, with overall responsibility. Second, the ‘drawer of the thread’ (sutra-grahini), was son or disciple of the sthapati, in charge of layout and measurement. Third, the takshaka is a cutter (taksh) (and carver?) of the stone or other material, and lastly the ‘fitter’ (vardhak) fits, plasters and paints. These were hereditary vocations in which whole families participated, travelling from site to site, and there is evidence that women could be involved in craft as well as their usual chores. The castes of craftsmen were organised in a system of guilds (seni), which, like their European counterparts, carried out functions of welfare as well as regulation, and could become rich and influential bodies.
C08-HARDY-007   p. 67
## The Hindu Temple, Volume I — Stella Kramrisch (KRAM-1, 7)
To the pilgrim and devotee who goes to the temple, it is a Tirtha made by art, as others are by nature, and often it is both in one. A Hindu temple unlike the Vedic altar does not fulfil its purpose by being built; it has of necessity to be seen. Darsana, the looking at the temple, the seat, abode and body of divinity and its worship (pūjā), are the purpose of visiting the temple. To fulfil this purpose in addition to being an offering and work of pious liberality the temple has not only its proportionate measurement but also the carvings on its walls, and the total fact of its form.
C08-KRAM-1-001   p. 154
Stone, when quarried and cut is an enduring and noble material, fit for gods, priests and the ruling classes. It is “100 times more meritorious to give a brick temple than a thatched temple; 10,000 times more meritorious to give a stone than a brick temple” (‘Mahānirvāna Tantra’, XIII. 24, 25).
C08-KRAM-1-002   p. 124
"Let him who wishes to enter the worlds that are reached by sacrificial offerings and the performance of religious obligations (istāpūrta) build a temple to the gods, by doing which he attains both the results of sacrifice and the performance of religious obligations" (Br. S. LV. 2)\(^{31}\). The commentary explains that sacrificial fire offerings are called 'ista' and all other offerings are 'pūrta'. The latter include the construction of tanks, wells, lakes and houses of the gods (devatāyatana).
C08-KRAM-1-003   p. 150
The resolve to build and generally also the funds for the building were the contribution of the Yajamāna or Kāraka and he entrusted the work to priest and craftsman. The merit of their knowledge came back to him who had employed, remunerated and then dismissed them \( ^{42} \) .
C08-KRAM-1-004   p. 153
The guilds of the architects (sthapati) and potters, for instance, levied a small cess on every one of their members, working at a particular temple, when a donor intended to make a permanent provision for the maintenance of the temple or the supply of materials for the worship of the deity (Kaman inscr. of about the eighth century; 'Ep. Ind.', XXIV, p. 329).
C08-KRAM-1-005   p. 154
The Taksaka is so called because he cuts off and carves (taks) the large pieces and the subtle detail. He is also expert in working in clay. He should be qualified, able, and capable to perform all sorts of work on his own initiative, in the right way, devoted to the Guru, ever cheerful, and obedient to the Sthapati.
The Vardhakin is so called because he increases (vrdh) [by placing together what the Taksaka has carved and by adding to the finished work, the painting] and he always follows the Sūtragrāhin.
Without these four nothing can be undertaken.
C08-KRAM-1-006   p. 21
Stone as part of the earth shares in the rites due to it; building stones however are quarried, taken from the earth by force, they have been rent from their living context like the trees which are felled for their wood. So the Sthapati and astrologer go to the quarry or to the forest and propitiate in the same manner the spirits who live in stones, all the gods, Yakṣas, Vidyātharas, Rākṣasas, Piśācas, Nāgas, Gandharvas and the eighteen Ganas" ('Vaikhānasāgama', X.), and those who live in the trees ('Visṇudharmottara', Part III.
C08-KRAM-1-007   p. 125
## The Hindu Temple, Volume II — Stella Kramrisch (KRAM-2, 4)
They are Śāntidvāra: the Door "Peace", in the East; Vidyādvāra, the Door "Knowledge" in the South, Nivṛttidvāra, the Door "Turning away from the world" in the West and Pratijśthādvāra, the Door "Firm basis" in the North. These four names are those of the four Kalās, the ontological part-aspects of manifestation, according to Śaiva terminology.
Their hierarchical sequence is, beginning from the lowest Kalā: Nivṛtti, Pratijṣṭhā, Vidyā and Sānti. All these doors however lead to the fifth and highest Kalā, and to the Supreme Essence of the Pure Principles.
C08-KRAM-2-001   p. 30
Then at dawn, of another day, the Sthapati, the master architect, and the Sthāpaka, the priest architect, ascend the Vimāna and with a golden needle, perform the rite of opening the eyes (netra mokṣa) of the Prāsāda in front of the cupola (Śikhara) of its High Temple (Harmya).
The Sthāpaka then instals the Prāsāda, in its concrete shape (prāsādamūrti) on its altar or pedestal (dhīṣnyamūrti) and places in it the Seed (bija) of the Temple.
C08-KRAM-2-002   p. 72
Then the Yajamāna, the sacrificer-patron, presents the Sthāpaka and Sthapati with gold, clothes, ornaments, etc., according to his ability. The Sthapati makes over a gift of gold to the Takṣan, and receives from him the entire merit (dharma sarvasva) of the work. The entire merit in building the temple, the Sthapati gives to the Yajamāna.
C08-KRAM-2-003   p. 73
The word Vasala (Vāsara?) inscribed in different places on the Duladeo Temple might be the name of a leading sculptor.
C08-KRAM-2-004   p. 90

Kuṭumbēsvara means the god who bestows prosperities on families. When the poison Halālaha was deposited in this Linga, Lakulīša (the founder of Pāśupatism and an Avatāra of Śiva) was asked to cover it. Thenceforth, the Linga became favourable to families and progeny.

## Temples of India (TEMPLES-IND, 1)
Founded in 972, the Lakulisha Temple at Ekalingaji (District Udaipur) is a large but simple structure, comprising a pancha-ratha sanctum, vestibule, closed hall with latticed windows in the lateral transepts and porch enclosed by a balustrade. The socle mouldings are simple and the wall is plain, but for two niches in the facade, one inset with an inscribed slab and the other with an image of Sarasvati. The brickwork super-structures of the shrine and the hall have practically crumbled. The sanctum enshrines an image of seated Lakulisha and the sanctum door-frame, showing 2 bands, displays an image of Lakulisha as the tutelary deity, surmounted by 5 niches containing figures of Ganesha, Brahma, Shiva, Vishnu and Karttikeya.
C08-TEMPLES-IND-001   p. 56

पांचवा सिरा: गढ़ी में खजाने की खोज में दूर-दूर के लालची आए। खूब खुदाई की। ज्यादातर पागल हो गए। आसपास के इलाके में ऐसे कई विश्वासों की कहानियां हैं, जिन्होंने गढ़ी का रख किया था। तकदीर किसी पर मेहरबान भी हुई थी। उनके किस्से भी हैं, जिन्हें यहां माल मिला।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Fifth thread: greedy people from far and wide came to the gadhi in search of treasure. They dug a lot. Most went mad. In the surrounding area there are stories of many beliefs that concerned the gadhi. Fortune also favoured some. There are tales of those who found wealth here.
C09-JAGTA-HI-012   p. 157
लालची लोग अब भी गढ़ी का रख करते हैं। वे झुंड में बंदूक और तलवारों से किसी से लड़ने नहीं आते। वे गेंती और कुदाली लेकर चुपके से आते हैं। अपने भाग्य की आजमाइश के लिए कहीं भी मलबा खोदकर चले जाते हैं। मुमकिन है किसी को कुछ मिलता भी हो।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Greedy people still come to the gadhi. They do not come in a crowd to fight anyone with guns and swords. They come quietly with pickaxes and spades. To test their luck they dig up rubble anywhere and go away. It is possible someone does find something.
C09-JAGTA-HI-013   p. 158
## Madhya Pradesh District Gazetteers: Vidisha — Government of Madhya Pradesh (GAZ, 11)
A vast majority of the population in the District professes Hinduism and 436,814 (89.29 per cent) returned under its fold in 1961. Hindus were followed by Muslims 44,601 (9.12 per cent), Jains 7,333 (1.50 per cent) and Sikhs 367 (0.07 per cent) in 1961. There were only 98 persons who professed other religions (86 Christianity and 12 Buddhism) in that year.
C09-GAZ-001   p. 75
The main castes according to Census 1941 were Teli (55,947), Chamar (43,904), Rajput (37,149), Kachhi (22,248), Brahman (17,808), Lodhi (10,413), Ahir (8,808), Koli (8,496), Kurmi (2,702), Mina (8,038), Rawat (6,833), Nai (4,739), Kirar (4,467), Saharia (4,179), Kumbhar (4,023), Kayastha (2,461), Dhobi (4,003) and Luhar (3,358). Their relative position could not be ascertained later on because of suspension of caste-wise enumeration of data in subsequent Censuses.
According to Census 1961, the Scheduled Castes \( ^{1} \) and the Scheduled Tribes \( ^{2} \) formed 20.92 and 4.34 per cent of the total District population.
C09-GAZ-002   p. 76
Hindi is the most widely spoken language in the District constituting the mother tongue of 444,947 persons or 90.95 per cent of total population in 1961. Other prominent speeches enumerated in the District were Urdu (38,338 or 7.84 per cent) and Marathi (1,763 or 0.36 per cent). The remaining 4,165 or 0.85 per cent speakers used 33 different speeches.
C09-GAZ-003   p. 73
In 1908, the prevailing dialect was Bundeli, spoken by 101,466 persons. But in census 1921, Malwi was reported as the most widely used colloquial dialect spoken by 7,366 persons per 10,000 of population against 1,178 persons per 10,000 of population speaking Bundelkhandi. Malwi retained its importance as the second most widely spoken dialect in 1931 and 1941 census, spoken by 73,450 and 59,370 persons respectively. But after the first census of free India in 1951, and in census 1961 only five and one person returned under it, respectively.
C09-GAZ-004   p. 74
Instructions in Hindi, English, Sanskrit, Urdu, Persian and Marathi subjects were given here.
C09-GAZ-005   p. 255
In the observance of socio-religious rites, all castes exhibit the same enthusiasm. If there is a bathing festival all take part in it. What is true of bathing festival is also true of all other festivals. No caste or sectarian differences are found there. Every one goes and bathes in the same water, drinks it for the sake of purification and pays homage to the sacred river, tank or pool.
C09-GAZ-006   p. 81
The engagement ceremony is performed in advance, known as sagai or teeka. After that lagan is sent from the bride's side to the groom's side. It contains details of programmes connected with the various marriage ceremonies.
In all castes, the bridegroom goes to the bride's village with relatives and friends. This party is called barat.
C09-GAZ-007   p. 84
It is believed that Maharshi Balmiki had also established his ashrama near Madagan (Lateri Tahsil), where instruction on Vedic lines in a number of subjects and arts \( ^{1} \) was given. Similarly, it is also claimed that Lateri village and its surrounding forest area were once the site of the famous Rishi Jamdagni's ashram where a number of subjects were taught, namely, Purana, Brahma Vidya, Jyotish, Nritya, Ayurveda, Agneye-shashtra, etc.
C09-GAZ-010   p. 253
The prevalent dress-pattern of agri. cultural class is a short dhoti, bandi and pagri for males and lehenga, odhni and choli for females. For festive occasions red, yellow, or green coloured muslims are purchased and garments made in the village itself by some local tailor.
C09-GAZ-013   p. 88
Wheat is the staple food of the District populace with milk and milk-products for proper nourishment especially among the rich and middle class people.
Puri, kachori, halwa, jalebi, milk, ghli, khir, phulki, rice (plain food and sweet), curries, bara, phulori, dal, dahi and sweets are the usual items of food, frequently eaten by them depending upon
the occasion. Among the poor classes, bajra and jowar or gram bread form the staple food with dal, vegetables, etc. Porridge, known as maheri or rabri is also eaten.
C09-GAZ-014   p. 89
Tracing the District's rich historical traditions during the time of Raja Bhoj, it is said that "Lavanya Sundari", a beautiful classical and rai-dancer hailed from this region. Performed by the professionals, known as bednis, ral is a characteristic folk-dance of this region, usually performed on the occasion of any festival or marriage. Decked in colourful dress and veil on the face, ral is usually danced in the torch-light. In the accompaniment of five to eight musical instruments it is performed on the rhythm of dadariya and keharya.
C09-GAZ-015   p. 90   also Ch 4
## History of the Paramāra Dynasty — Ganguly, D. C. (GANGULY, 1)
The population of the country mainly consisted of the Brahmans, Ksatriyas, Kâyasthas, Vaiśyas, Śâdras, and Tailakas. \( ^{6} \)  The Brahmana, as usual, occupied the highest position in the community.
C09-GANGULY-001   p. 249
## The Cultural Heritage of Madhya Bharat — Patil, D. R. (MADHYABHARAT, 1)
The whole of northern Madhya Bharat is included in the sphere of Western Hindi with its respectable dialects of Brij-Bhasha prevalent in northern extremities and Bundeli spoken in the central parts of northern Madhya Bharat. Of these the latter has a good literature to its credit. The last census returns have shown nearly 59 lakhs of the population speaking the Western Hindi indicating that even parts of Malwa proper have now alligned themselves with this group. It will, however, be found that almost the whole of the plateau once used the dialect of Malwi, the southern variety of Rajasthani, though last census returns show a little over five lakhs of people only coming under its influence.
C09-MADHYABHARAT-001   p. 25
## King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture (Raja Bhoj aur Parmarkaleen Nagar Sthapatya) — English translation — Bhagwatilal Rajpurohit (RAJABHOJ-EN, 8)
The Brahmin should be in the north, the Kshatriya in the east, the Vaishya in the south, and the Shudra in the west. The merchant and the physician, and the army, should be stationed in all four directions.
C09-RAJABHOJ-EN-001   p. 83
King Bhoj, in his *Shringara-prakasha* and *Sarasvati-kanthabharana*, has discussed various festivals which, along with being folk-festivals, were also royal festivals. Of these, some were vow-festivals, some of the season, and some crop-festivals. Noteworthy among such festivals are: Ashtami-chandraka, Kunda-chaturthi, Suvasantaka, Andolana-chaturthi, Dola-vilasa, Shalmali, Madanotsava, Udaka-kshvedika, Ashokotsika, Chuta-bhanjika, Pushpavachayanika, Amralatika, Bhutamatrika, Kadamba-yuddha, Navapatrika (Phulpati), Bisakhadika, Shakrarcha, Kaumudi-mahotsava, Yaksharatri (the festival of lamps), Abhyushakhadika, Navekshu-bhakshika, Toya-krida, Preksha, Dyuta, Madhupana, Navalatika, Navapatrika, Indrotsava and so on.
C09-RAJABHOJ-EN-002   p. 148
Even in his own lifetime he became the hero of tales and stories. Thereafter a world of fascinating stories relating to King Bhoj kept being woven continually. In various languages of India and abroad, strange tales, poetic narratives and stories about his generosity are especially [numerous]. He is present even in the stories of the thirty-two puppets in the *Simhasana-dvatrimshika* (सिंहासनद्वात्रिंशिका). Those Bhoj-tales are present also in the ancient Mongolian-language book *Arajibuji* (अराजिबुजि).
C09-RAJABHOJ-EN-003   p. 15
Myths relating to Bhoj are found in abundance. King Bhoj is the finest example of a history-
154 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture
person becoming a myth.
C09-RAJABHOJ-EN-004   p. 154–155
A proverb about his valour is famous: "Where is King Bhoj, and where is Gangu the oilman!" (*Kान राजा भोज ने कान गंगू तेली*). In princely Malwi it is said thus: *"Kathe Raja Bhoj ne kathe Gangu Teli."* The root of this proverb is in fact in accord with history. In the above proverb, the *teli* (oilman) is also called *telan* (oil-woman): *"Kathe Raja Bhoj ne kathe Gangi Telan."* This reading of the proverb is closer to its origin, because King Bhoj's contemporary Gangeyadeva Kalachuri is known. The folk turned Gangeyadeva into "Gangi"; and turned "Telang" into "Telan." Thus Gangeyadeva Telang became Gangi Telan.
C09-RAJABHOJ-EN-005   p. 156
In Dhar, on a platform in front of the Lat Masjid, huge stones lie. About them there is a folk-belief that these are the *telan*'s *aindra-weights* (*bat*), with which the *telan* weighed and sold oil. To weigh oil with such huge weights is no ordinary task. There too, leaning against the platform, three large sections of an iron pillar lie, whose total length is nearly 44 feet. About those pillars it is said that this is the beam of the *telan*'s balance-scale.
C09-RAJABHOJ-EN-006   p. 157
From this whole account it should become clear how much breadth there is in the proverb *"Kathe Raja Bhoj ne kathe Gangi Telan,"* and how much history is packed into it. It is not merely a folk fabrication; its background is solid and strong. It is also said that in "Gangi Telan" there is an indication of Bhoj's victory over two [foes] in succession — Gangeyadeva the Kalachuri and Tailapa of Telangana.
C09-RAJABHOJ-EN-007   p. 158
About it a verse is current —
"Where the Paramara, there Dhar."
"Where Dhar, there the Paramara.
Without Dhar, no Paramara.
Without the Paramara, no Dhar."
C09-RAJABHOJ-EN-008   p. 161
