## भोजदेव (Bhojadeva) (BHOJDEV-HI, 4)
यह विल्लु विपुला का तद्भव शब्द है । विपुला पृथ्वी है । तो यह प्रार्थना धरतीमाता से की जा रही है । भोज धरती का वेटा है । पूरी पृथ्वी ही माता रही भोज की । सबके प्रति भोज की समान आस्था होने से वह सवका अपना था ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Villu is a corruption of Vipula; Vipula is the Earth; the prayer is made to Mother Earth; Bhoja is earth's son; the whole earth was Bhoja's mother; with equal faith in all he was everyone's own.
C09-BHOJDEV-HI-001   p. 2
उसके व्यक्तित्व की विभिन्न छटाओं के कारण भी लोक में यह प्रचलित हो गया कि ऐसा राजा न हुआ, न होगा। भोज के अद्धपुत व्यक्तित्व की परिकल्पना लोक में प्रचलित हो गयी और उन्हें पुट करती कई कथाएँ न केवल सुनित साहित्य में अवतरित हो गयीं अभितु लोकगीतों के माध्यम से कंठ-कंठ में वैठती प्रकट होती गयीं। ऐसा ही एक गीत मालवी के देवीगीत में गाया जाता है। तद्रनुसार विल्लु वाई वाँझ को पुत्र देती है। उसने भोज का भी जनम सुधार दिया था, भोज के समान हमारा भी जनम सुधार दे माता!
*Gloss [my translation]:* Owing to his many-sided personality it became a saying that there was and will be no such king; stories and folk songs kept it alive; one Malwi devi-song tells how Villu Bai gives a son to a barren woman and had set right Bhoja's birth, praying to be treated likewise.
C09-BHOJDEV-HI-002   p. 2
वारंपरिक दोहों में प्राप्त होती है -
कसी चाले डोकरी कीका कोई खोज।
कई धारो खोवई गयो पूछे राजा भोज ।।
महारा से धारे गई जीका कोई खोज ।
धारा से भी जायगी मत गरवावे भोज ।
*Gloss [my translation]:* A conversation of an old woman with Raja Bhoja is found in traditional Malwi couplets; roughly: Raja Bhoja asks what the old woman has lost on Dhara's road; she answers she has gone from Dhara and none knows; Dhara too will go, do not be proud, Bhoja.
C09-BHOJDEV-HI-003   p. 11
यहाँ तक कि परकाय प्रवेश, इन्द्रजाल इत्यादि में भी भोज की दक्षता इतनी लोकव्यापी हो गयी थी कि परम्परा में भोजविधा शब्द ही इन्द्रजाल का अर्थ देने लगा, यह भी कारण है कि अद्भुत कथाओं के नायक के रूप में प्रसिद्ध होकर विक्रणादित्य के समान राजा भोज भी लोकव्यापी कथाओं, गीता और किंवर्दिन्तयों का नायक होता गया। विक्रमादित्य और भोज एसे लोकनायक हो गये कि उनके आसपास नवी-नवी चमत्कारी रहानिथियों का पुतला में बुना जाता रहा। पन्द्रहवीं शती के राजवल्लभ के भोजवरिस्त्र सहित कितनी ही पुतला में भोज की ऐसी अनोखी कहानियाँ पायी जा सकती हैं। वहाँ वह आदमी से तोता-रूप में परिवर्तित होता भी पाया जा सकता है। ऐसी अगणित कथाओं का एकत्र संकलन और अध्ययन अभी अपेक्षित है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* His skill in thought-transference and jugglery became so widespread that 'Bhoja-vidya' came to mean indrajala; like Vikramaditya he became hero of popular tales, songs and legends, with miraculous stories woven round him, e.g. in Rajavallabha's Bhojacharitra (15th century) where he turns from man into parrot; collecting and studying these tales is still needed.
C09-BHOJDEV-HI-004   p. 12

# Chapter 10. Political Change and Economic Decline: Udaypur under Successive Regimes
## कथा उदयपुर (Jagta Hua Kasba) — Hindi original (JAGTA-HI, 19)
विदिशा में विजय मंदिर का वैभव तो पूरी तरह ध्वस्त कर दिया गया। इतिहास ने धार, उज्जैन और मंडप दुर्ग के सरस्वती कंठाभरणों को इबादतगाहों और मकबरों में बदलते हुए देखा। स्थापत्य के महान स्मारक उजाड़ टीलों या बदशकल इमारतों में बदल गए।
*Gloss [my translation]:* At Vidisha the splendour of Vijay Mandir was totally destroyed. History saw the Sarasvati Kanthabharanas of Dhara, Ujjain and Mandapadurga turned into places of worship and tombs. Great monuments of architecture became desolate mounds or ugly buildings.
C10-JAGTA-HI-008   p. 30
कालांतर में परमारों का वैभव एक और इस्लामी आतंकी अलाउद्दीन खिलजी के द्वारा भेजे गए एक हमलावर जत्थे के हाथों मांडू में खत्म हो गया। वह महाराज महलकदेव थे, जो खिलजियों के खूनी और लालची अंधड़ों से टकराए थे। हम नहीं जानते कि उनके खानदान के कितने लोग मारे गए, कितने बचे और वे फिर कहाँ गए?
*Gloss [my translation]:* In time Paramara splendour ended at Mandu at the hands of a raiding band sent by Alauddin Khilji. That was Maharaj Mahlakdeva, who clashed with the Khiljis' storms. We do not know how many of his family were killed, how many survived and where they went.
C10-JAGTA-HI-009   p. 50
देवपाल देव के समय ही शमसुद्दीन इल्लुतमिश ने विदिशा और उज्जैन को लूटकर बुरी तरह बरबाद किया था। उसकी लुटेरी फौजों ने पूरे मालवा को एक ही लंबी मुहिम में दूर-दूर तक जाकर तहसनहस किया था। वह इस क्षेत्र का पहला इस्लामी संपर्क था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* In Devapaladeva's time Shamsuddin Iltutmish plundered Vidisha and Ujjain and badly ruined them. His raiding armies devastated all of Malwa in one long campaign. It was the first Islamic contact of this region.
C10-JAGTA-HI-010   p. 50
वह मंदिर पहली बार शमसुद्दीन इल्लुतमिश के हमले में जमींदोज किया गया था। समकालीन इतिहासकार मिनहाज सिराज ने भिलसा के इस मंदिर की ऊँचाई 105 गज लिखी है। विजय मंदिर की विशाल बुनियाद से उसके ऊपर खड़े मंदिर की ऊँचाई का अंदाजा करेंगे तो ऊँचाई बिल्कुल इतनी ही रही होगी।
*Gloss [my translation]:* That temple was first razed to the ground in Shamsuddin Iltutmish's attack. The contemporary historian Minhaj Siraj wrote the height of this Bhilsa temple as 105 yards. Judging from its huge foundation, the height would have been just this.
C10-JAGTA-HI-011   p. 58
यह 105 गज ऊंचा मंदिर था। बहुत दूर से नजर आता था। दिल्ली के सुलतान शमसुद्दीन इल्तुतमिश ने इसे नेस्तनाबूत किया है। वे शहर को लूटकर उज्जैन की तरफ निकल गए हैं। वहां महाकाल मंदिर का यही हाल करेंगे। पहली बार इस्लामी फौजों
ने यहां दस्तक दी है।' मिनहाज यह कहकर गायब हो गया।
*Gloss [my translation]:* It was a temple 105 yards high, visible from very far. Delhi's Sultan Shamsuddin Iltutmish has destroyed it. They looted the city and went toward Ujjain, where they will do the same to the Mahakal temple. For the first time Islamic armies have knocked here.
C10-JAGTA-HI-012   p. 61
उज्जैन, धार और मांडू तीनों जगह के ये भोजकालीन भव्य स्थान 1401 में दिलावर खां गौरी ने एक साथ एक जैसे स्तूपकवर में बदले। सरस्वती कंठाभरण के मलबे से ही चारदीवारी में घिरे एक जैसे परिसर बने, जिन्हें अब आज भोजशाला कहते हैं। उज्जैन में वह अनंतपेठ मोहल्ले में आज बिना नींव की मस्जिद है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* In 1401 Dilawar Khan Ghuri turned these splendid Bhoja-era places at Ujjain, Dhar and Mandu all at once into similar stupa-covered structures. Enclosed complexes were built from the rubble of the Sarasvati Kanthabharana, now called Bhojshala. In Ujjain it is today a mosque without foundation in Anantpeth locality.
C10-JAGTA-HI-013   p. 65
परमारों के आखिरी राजा महलकदेव हैं, जिनके समय दिल्ली के हमलावर लुटेरे शमसुद्दीन इल्लतुतमिश ने मालवा पर धावा बोला था। तब इस इलाके के सारे मंदिर मिट्टी में मिला दिए गए। उज्जैन में महाकाल का मंदिर भी पहली बार तभी लूटा और तोड़ा गया। इल्लतुतमिश लूट के माल के साथ उज्जैन से भगवान महाकाल और विक्रमादित्य की मूर्ति को दिल्ली ले गया था और उन्हें हर रोज बेइज्जत करने के लिए एक मस्जिद के बाहर पटकवा दिया था। मिनहाज सिराज ने इसकी गवाही दी है। इब्नबतूता ने अपनी आंखों से दिल्ली में उज्जैन की उन मूर्तियों को मस्जिद के बाहर पड़े हुए देखा है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* The last Paramara king is Mahlakdeva, in whose time Delhi's raider Shamsuddin Iltutmish attacked Malwa. All the temples of the area were razed. The Mahakal temple in Ujjain was looted and broken for the first time then. Iltutmish carried the images of Mahakal and Vikramaditya to Delhi and had them thrown outside a mosque. Minhaj Siraj bears witness; Ibn Battuta saw those images lying outside the mosque in Delhi.
C10-JAGTA-HI-014   p. 65   also Ch 4
दिलावर
खां गौरी मालवा का पहला स्वतंत्र सुलतान बना। यह बाबर के भारत आने के सवा
सौ साल पहले का वाकया है। होशंग शाह गौरी उसका उत्तराधिकारी था, जिसके नाम
से होशंगाबाद है और जिसने भोपाल में भोजपुर के पास बने भोज के बांध को तुड़वा
डाला था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Dilawar Khan Ghuri became the first independent sultan of Malwa, a century and a quarter before Babur came to India. Hoshang Shah Ghuri was his successor, after whom Hoshangabad is named, and who had Bhoja's dam near Bhojpur in Bhopal broken.
C10-JAGTA-HI-015   p. 66
वह राजा नरवर्मन के सौ साल बाद ही दिल्ली पर कब्जा करने वाले बेरहम तुर्क लुटेरों के हाथों लूटकर बरबाद कर दिया गया। वह कुख्यात हमलावर इलतुतमिश था, मालवा पर जिसके भीषण हमलों में उसे तोड़ दिया गया था। उस विशाल देवालय के मलबे से एक मस्जिद बना दी गई।
*Gloss [my translation]:* A hundred years after King Naravarman it was looted and ruined by the merciless Turk raiders who had seized Delhi. That notorious invader was Iltutmish, in whose fierce attacks on Malwa it was broken. A mosque was made from the rubble of that huge temple.
C10-JAGTA-HI-016   p. 79
देवपाल देव के शिलालेख बताते हैं कि विदिशा को लूटकर बरबाद करने के बाद तैनात इल्तुतमिश के म्लेच्छ सरदार को मार गिराया गया था। परमारों के अधिपत्य में आने के बाद विदिशा अब वही नहीं था, जो हमले के पहले छोड़ा गया था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* The inscriptions of Devapaladeva tell that the mleccha chief posted by Iltutmish after plundering and ruining Vidisha was struck down. After coming under Paramara control Vidisha was no longer the same as when it was left after the attack.
C10-JAGTA-HI-017   p. 105
आप विदिशा में बीजा मंडल के उन खंडित पत्थरों को देखकर कल्पना कर सकते हैं कि यह अपने समय में देश के सबसे बड़े मंदिरों में से एक रहा होगा। इसकी ऊंचाई 105 गज मिनाज सिराज ने "तबकाते-नासिरी" में लिखी है, जो इल्तुतमिश के हमले की तबाही को दर्ज करने वाला दिल्ली का एकमात्र खबरनवीस है। विदिशा के दुर्ग पर तैनात परमार राजा देवपाल देव के सैनिक जब हारे होंगे तब वे वंशो इस्लामी लुटेरे शहर में घुसे होंगे और कोसों दूर से नजर आने वाले विजय मंदिर पर जत्थों में जाकर सबसे पहले कब्जा किया गया होगा।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Seeing those broken stones of the Bija Mandal in Vidisha you can imagine that it must have been one of the biggest temples in the country in its time. Its height of 105 gaz was written by Minhaj Siraj in the 'Tabaqat-i-Nasiri', the only Delhi chronicler recording the devastation of Iltutmish's attack. When the soldiers of the Paramara king Devapala Deva posted at Vidisha's fort were defeated, those Islamic plunderers must have entered the city, and the Vijay temple visible from miles away must have been seized first in groups.
C10-JAGTA-HI-018   p. 105
आप बिहार में नालंदा और
विक्रमशिला से लेकर दूर कर्नाटक में विजय नगर तक जाइए। मातम मनाने के लिए देश के कोने-कोने में ऐसा बहुत कुछ बिखरा हुआ है। हम नहीं जानते कि इन विकसित बसाहटों में बरबाद किए जाने से पहले कितने कीमती शिलालेख रहे होंगे, जिनको बुरी तरह मिटाकर पत्थर का चूरा कर दिया गया। विदिशा में ही नरवर्मन का बनवाया विजय मंदिर इसकी सबसे दर्दनाक मिसाल है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Go to Nalanda and Vikramashila in Bihar, to Vijayanagara far off in Karnataka. Much lies scattered in every corner of the country to mourn. We do not know how many valuable inscriptions there were in these developed settlements before destruction, which were badly erased and turned to stone dust. In Vidisha itself the Vijay temple built by Naravarman is the most painful example of this.
C10-JAGTA-HI-023   p. 135–136
यह अब किसी से छुपा हुआ नहीं है कि 12 वीं सदी के आखिर में दिल्ली पर तुर्क मुसलमानों के कब्जे के बाद की सदियों में देश भर की प्राचीन धरोहरों पर खतरा मंडराने लगा था। वे जहां भी गए, मंदिरों और मूर्तियों के लिए विनाशकारी साबित हुए। नदी के तटों पर घाट, मंदिर और गुरुकुलों के निर्माण की परंपरा नई नहीं थी। मंदिरों को तोड़ा गया और खंडित मूर्तियों को नदियों में फेंका गया। सोनारी गाँव की टूटी-फूटी मूर्तियां सदियों पहले के उसी विश्वास के सबूत हैं, जिनके बारे में हमारे स्कूल-कॉलेजों में कभी कुछ नहीं पढ़ाया गया। वह इस्लाम की विनाशकारी शक्ति के प्रमाण हैं, जो पैदाइशी तौर पर मूर्ति पूजा को गुनाह मानता आया है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* It is no longer hidden from anyone that after the Turk Muslim occupation of Delhi at the end of the 12th century, in the following centuries the ancient heritage all over the country came under threat. Wherever they went they proved destructive for temples and images. The tradition of building ghats, temples and gurukuls on river banks was not new. Temples were broken and the broken images thrown into rivers. The broken images of Sonari village are proof of that same belief of centuries ago, about which nothing was ever taught in our schools and colleges. They are evidence of the destructive power of Islam, which has by birth considered idol worship a sin.
C10-JAGTA-HI-025   p. 155   also Ch 9
मंदिर देहाती शैली में चालीसैक साल पुराना ही है, लेकिन चंद
मूर्तियां सदियों पुरानी गवाही देती हैं। वे कुछ इस तरह खंडित हैं, जो बताती हैं कि उन्हें
खंडित किया गया है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* The temple in rustic style is only about forty years old, but a few images give centuries-old testimony. They are broken in such a way as to show they were deliberately broken.
C10-JAGTA-HI-026   p. 156
तीसरा सिरा: मुसलमानों के राज में हमलों और लूट का जोर था। हिंदू अपनी जान और इज्जत बचाने के लिए ऐसे सुरक्षित पहाड़ों में डेरा जमा लेते थे। यह ऐसी ही सुरक्षित जगह थी।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Third thread: in the rule of Muslims, attacks and plunder were intense. Hindus used to camp in such safe hills to save their lives and honour. This was such a safe place.
C10-JAGTA-HI-027   p. 157
- खिलजी... तुगलक... लोदी और फिर मुगल....। मालवा-बुंदेलखंड के वैभवशाली जनपदों ने इन सभी की बदनीयत आक्रमण के कष्ट सहें.....
*Gloss [my translation]:* Khilji... Tughlaq... Lodi and then Mughal... The glorious janapadas of Malwa-Bundelkhand bore the pain of the ill-intentioned attacks of all these.
C10-JAGTA-HI-030   p. 163
सन् 1707 में सबसे बदनाम मुगल औरंगजेब की मौत दूर दक्षिण में हुई और इस वजह से दिल्ली में मची अफरा-तफरी के बाद के सालों में दोस्त मोहम्मद खान नाम का एक अफगान दिल्ली से निकला और इस इलाके में पहली बार नजर आया था। उसे भोपाल से 40 किलोमीटर दूर बैरसिया के पास किसी छोटी राजपूत जागीर में नौकरी मिल गई थी और जैसा कि उस दौर के पूरे भारत में यह आम प्रचलन था, छल-कपट, फसाद और बेवजह हमलों से वह जल्दी ही एक पुरानी गौड़ रियासत का नया मालिक बन बैठा।
*Gloss [my translation]:* In 1707 Aurangzeb died far in the south; in the confusion in Delhi in the following years an Afghan named Dost Mohammad Khan left Delhi and was first seen in this region. He got service in a small Rajput jagir near Berasia, 40 km from Bhopal, and through deceit and attacks soon became the new master of an old Gond principality.
C10-JAGTA-HI-031   p. 3
भोपाल की तरह कुरवाई भी दो सौ साल से एक और अफगान परिवार के कब्जे में छोटी सी रियासत रही थी, जिनकी रिश्तेदारियां भोपाल पर कब्जा जमाने वाले खानदान में थीं। भोपाल पर कब्जा अफगान सरदार की बेटी आबिदा बेगम कुरवाई के कब्जेदार से ब्याही थीं, जिन्होंने पाकिस्तान बनने पर कुरवाई और भोपाल दोनों को रहने और ठहरने लायक नहीं समझा था।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Like Bhopal, Kurwai too had been for two hundred years a small principality in the possession of another Afghan family, whose relatives belonged to the family that took Bhopal. Abida Begum, daughter of the Afghan chief who held Bhopal, was married to the holder of Kurwai.
C10-JAGTA-HI-043   p. 8
दूसरा सिरा: दौलतपुर नाम का एक गांव नीचे था, जो अब वीरान है। ऐसे और भी
गांव थे, जिनके नामोनिशान नहीं हैं। नाम हैं बस। गांव नहीं हैं। कभी यहां बाजार भरते
*Gloss [my translation]:* Another thread: a village named Daulatpur was below, which is now deserted. There were more villages like it of which no trace remains. Only names. No villages. Markets once met here. But how did the villages become deserted?
C10-JAGTA-HI-045   p. 156
## Madhya Pradesh District Gazetteers: Vidisha — Government of Madhya Pradesh (GAZ, 45)
Prior to this defeat the Chandella king Madan Varman (A.D. 1129-1163) forcibly occupied the Vidisha region from Yasovarman's possession. The Chandella reoccupation of the Vidisha region is proved by the fact that Madan Varman, when in residence near Vidisha, issued a grant bestowing ten ploughs of land in the Sudali Visaya (located in Vidisha) to Brahmana Rahul Sharma \( ^{8} \) .
But the region was snatched away by the Chaulukya ruler Yasovarman's son, Jayavarman.
C10-GAZ-001   p. 43
Vidisha (Bhilsa) first appears in the Muslim writings in Al Beruni's description of India, where it is said to be in Malwa, ten farsakh distant from Ujjain. \( ^{2} \)  In 1234 Iltutmish attacked and sacked Bhilsa during his Malwa campaign. He is also said to have destroyed the great idol temple, Viiay Mandir, which had taken three hundred years to build. He took possession of Bhilsa and proceeded towards Ujjain.
C10-GAZ-002   p. 44
During the reign of Mahlak Deva, the last Paramara ruler of Malwa, Alaud-din Khalji invaded Malwa in 1293 A. D., captured the town of Bhilsa, along with some bronze idols, and sent them in carts with a variety of rich booty as presents to the Sultan. For having sent the spoils of Bhilsa to the Sultan, Ala-ud-din was made Ariz-i-Mamalik of the territory of Oudh, in addition to that of Karra.\(^{4}\)
C10-GAZ-003   p. 44
Malwa continued to be a Tughluq province till about 1401 A. D., when Dilawar Khan Ghuri, the governor of Malwa, declared himself independent and established the Kingdom of Malwa.
C10-GAZ-004   p. 44
The Rana bestowed, in 1518 A. D., a large territory including, it seems, Bhilsa on Medini Rai. The latter seems to have passed on the Bhilsa territory to Salhadi, one of his generals.
C10-GAZ-005   p. 45
Pursuing Bhupat, Bahadur Shah reached Bhilsa via Sarangpur. He stayed for a few days at Bhilsa and consolidated his authority over the territory. During his stay here Bahadur Shah sacked the city, destroyed a number of temples and built some mosques. He left Bhilsa on 16 January, 1532 and reached Raisen the following day.\(^{5}\)
C10-GAZ-006   p. 46
\( ^{2} \)  Bahadur Shah, however, made over sarkars of Ujjain and Sarangpur, and the fort of Raisen, as also perhaps Bhilsa and Ashta, to Salhadi in jagir.
C10-GAZ-007   p. 46
Thus, for the second time Malwa lost its freedom and except for Raisen, Bhilsa and Chanderi the whole of Malwa was occupied by the Afghan officers. Raisen Bhilsa and Chanderi, however, continued to be under Puranmal for some time more.
C10-GAZ-008   p. 47
Qadir Shah appears to have given a large tract of eastern Malwa including, perhaps, Bhilsa to Puranmal and thus won him over as a loyal vassal and ally.
C10-GAZ-009   p. 47
The Rajpu's fought for every inch of ground against the Afghans, and Jalal Khan's army had to spend three months to occupy 120 miles of territory between Chanderi and Bhilsa. Sher Shah himself took over the command of the army at Bhilsa in the beginning of April, 1543.\(^{3}\) He then proceeded towards the mighty fort of Raisen, about 14 miles south of Bhilsa and 29 miles to the east of the modern city of Bhopal.
C10-GAZ-010   p. 48
But soon Shujaat Khan was pardoned by Islam Shah and was given in Jagir the territory comprising Sarangpur, Raisen and Vidisha (Bhilsa). \( ^{6} \)  But Mubariz Khan, successor of Islam Shah, again appointed Shujaat Khan the governor of the province. Shujaat Khan then made fresh arrangements for the administration of the province and placed Raisen and Bhilsa in charge of his youngest son, Mustafa Khan \( ^{7} \)
C10-GAZ-011   p. 48
He fled from Malwa, leaving Baz Bahadur to take possession of the forts of Raisen and Bhilsa. Thus the two forts along with the territory of the present districts of Raisen and Vidisha were brought under the direct control of Baz Bahadur, the Sultan of Malwa, who entrusted the government of these territories to his own men.
C10-GAZ-012   p. 49
As recorded in Minhaj-us-Siraj's Tabaqat-i-Nasiri, the Muhammadans destroyed a great temple of this region, 300 years after its erection, which was 5 Gaz in height. Probably he referred to none other than the Bhillasvami temple at Bhilsa.
C10-GAZ-015   p. 349
\( ^{5} \)  He was a local chief of considerable importance holding a large tract of the Malwa province. This is corroborated by Babur who writes in his Memoirs that, when he entered India (1527 A. D.) Bhilsa was held by Salhadi, (Salahuddin as he calls him), who had acquired the possession of Vidisha (Bhilsa), Sarangpur and other places during the decline of the Khalji dynasty of Malwa.
C10-GAZ-016   p. 45
When Akbar divided his empire into administrative divisions, the territory of the present Vidisha District was included in the Sarkars of Raisen and Chanderi of the Subah of Malwa. Bhilsa (Vidisha) was the headquarters of a mahal of Raisen Sarkar, and paid a revenue of 6,094,970 Dams. It also contributed 480 horsemen and 1000 Infantry to the Imperial army. Sironj was then the headquarters of a mahal of Chanderi Sarkar and paid a revenue of 11,065,765 Dams, while it contributed 100 horsemen and 2,500 infantry to the Imperial army.
C10-GAZ-017   p. 50
The religious intolerance of Aurangzeb, however, led to the destruction of a number of temples of Bhilsa in 1682, including the fine Vijay Mandir of the town. At the same time Aurangzeb erected a Dost Muhammad Khan mosque on the site of the former temple. He renamed the town as Alamgirpur. The new name, never coming into general use, was used in official documents only.
C10-GAZ-018   p. 50
According to the Gwalior State Gazetteer of 1907, “when the Gwalior State was founded, the prevalent forms of coinage were those of the Mughals and few local issues made by Rajput Chiefs. Mughal mints were situated at several places still within the limits of the State, coins issuing from Ujjain, Bhilsa, Gwalior and Narwar, while others such as Agra and Ajmer were at one time in the hands of Sindhia”.
"With the fall of Mughal power the Maratha chiefs acquired or assumed the right to coin, the coins being issued in the name of the Mughal Emperor".
C10-GAZ-019   p. 153
Some evidence of the system of land revenue assessment prevalent in the medieval period in the areas now constituting Vidisha District is available in Ain-i-Akbari. It has been reported that it had an area of 40,816 bighas with 60,94,970 dams as land revenue. This mahal was a part of the sarkar of Raisen in the Subah of Malwa. Todarmal's system of land revenue settlement was extended to these areas during Akbar's rule (1542-1605).
C10-GAZ-020   p. 204   also Ch 8
The old town was destroyed by the Muhammadans, possibly by Alamgir or Aurangzeb.
C10-GAZ-022   p. 339
By far the most interesting piece of the town is the Bijamandal mosque, which preserves its Hindu association in its name. The mosque is perched on the
foundation of an old Hindu temple of the 11th century A.D. Bijamandal Evidently the material of the demolished shrine was used mosque to construct the new building by Aurangzeb in 1682.
C10-GAZ-024   p. 350   also Ch 7
Under the Mughals its importance was reduced by making it the headquarters of a mahal. Aurangzeb tried to rename the city as Alamgirnagar after himself, but without success.
C10-GAZ-025   p. 350
Taking advantage of chaotic conditions in the Mughal empire, following the death of Aurangzeb, Dost Muhammad Khan (1708 to 1728 A.D.), an Afghan adventurer, set up an independent principality at Bhopal. After consolidating his position, he sent a strong force under his brother Sher Muhammad Khan against Vidisha (Bhilas). The battle between the two armies took place between the villages of Jamaldi and Bagri, near Vidisha.
C10-GAZ-026   p. 50
was killed by the „Mewati Chief of Doraha and the Bhopal force was thrown into confusion.
Dost Muhammad Khan who was hiding behind the hill of Bagri, rushed forward, surrounded Muhammad Faruk, the Governor of Vidisha, and attacked and killed him. Mounting on Muhammad Faruk's elephant, Dost Muhammad then marched to Vidisha, forcing the drummers to sound the drums. There he was taken for Muhammad Faruk returning victorious and was admitted to the fort. Having taken possession of the fort by fraud rather than by force he made himself master of Gyaraspur and the surrounding territories.
C10-GAZ-027   p. 51
Thus, Vidisha passed under the administrative control of the Marathas.
In January, 1737 the Peshwa made a move to realize the dues from Vidisha. Accordingly, the two brothers Tukoji and Jeevaji Pawar, founders, respectively, of the senior and junior branches of the House of Dewas, besieged Vidisha,\(^{8}\) and took it after a siege of a fortnight on 11 January, 1737 and realized the dues.\(^{4}\) The Peshwa then moved on to Bundelkhand, reaching Vidisha on 14 January, 1737.\(^{5}\)
C10-GAZ-028   p. 51
Sawai Jai Singh of Jaipur, then Governor of Malwa, granted Bhilsa to the Nawab of Bhopal, but it soon after passed into the possession of the Marathas.
C10-GAZ-029   p. 51
\( ^{3} \)  The Maratha forces attacked and took the fort of Vidisha (Bhilsa) in March, 1745. \( ^{4} \)  Vidisha remained with the Marathas until 1753, when the Peshwa renewed the settlement of lands with Bhopal State, which obtained possession of the fort of Bhilsa. \( ^{5} \)  But it seems that the Marathas succeeded in reoccupying it soon after.
C10-GAZ-030   p. 52
The possession of Vidisha tract then several times passed in quick succession between the Marathas and the Bhopal State. The Marathas reoccupied it sometime before 1761, for when Peshwa Balaji Bajj Rao marched into Malwa, in January, 1761, he stayed at Vidisha.
C10-GAZ-031   p. 53
\( ^{1} \)  By the year 1765 the Maratha's hold on the tract was well established. The affairs of Vidisha came in Sindhia's hands by the year 1775 and since then it formed part of the former princely Gwalior State.
C10-GAZ-032   p. 54
As stated before, by the year 1775 Vidisha was under the authority of Mahadaji Sindhia. Kurwai State including Basoda and Muhammadgarh came into being in the year 1713, when Muhammad Diler Khan, an Afghan adventurer from Tirah, belonging to the Feroze Khel Clan, seized Kurwai and the surrounding villages. Later, in return for certain services he was granted 31 parganas by the Mughal Emperor. The states of Basoda and Muhammadgarh were being held in 1753 by Muhammad Ahsanullah Khan, the brother of Izzat Khan the Nawab of Kurwai. Izzat Khan had made over these tracts to his brother. Later Ahsanullah Khan divided the two parganas between his two sons, Bakaullah Khan and Muhammad Khan the latter founding the town and State of Muhammadgarh.
C10-GAZ-033   p. 55
Under the Mughals, the land was apportioned into Khalsa and Jagirland. The khalsa land was divided into charges and was managed by Amils and other State officials. The Jagir land was divided into blocks, or estates which were made over for life (the grants became hereditary at a later stage) to certain military commanders, ministers and courtiers who utilised the revenue for their own needs and for the upkeep of military force which they were required to maintain \( ^{3} \) . But this system collapsed at the time of Maratha invasion when the Subas and other officials became independent during the early part of the eighteenth century.
C10-GAZ-034   p. 204
This jaredari system gradually went into disuse after the
death of Maharaja Jankoji Rao (1827-1843), when the Council of Regency was set up and the zamindari system took its place. Henceforth, the land management of big villages was given on contract to land-holders. They were not only required to credit annually the Government demand, but were also required to furnish a rough account of the recoveries made from the cultivators. Lands cultivated by the contractors (Khud-kasht) were not assessed. On the expiry of the period of a particular lease, it was given to the same holder as far as possible.
In 1853, the jiarcdari system was abolished and leases for a term of 12 years were granted.
C10-GAZ-035   p. 205   also Ch 8
Land tenures under the Sindhia regime may be classified under three heads, viz., (1) Guaranteed estates/Jagirs (2) State Jagirs, which were sub-divided into istimarari, tankedari, ubari, mufidari, etc., and (3) zamin-
Land Tenures
dari. Their holders were classed as Sardars of the first and second grade. \( ^{1} \)  The jagir areas were administered
by the jagirdars, some of them were even granted revenue, judicial and police powers.
C10-GAZ-036   p. 207
Vidisha region continued to remain a scene of rebellious activities. Adil Muhammad Khan, with his 500 followers, after passing through the villages of Newayanpura and entering through Tehri Jungle occupied, early in September, 1858, the fort of Bussul (Sironj), situated on a hill.
C10-GAZ-041   p. 60
On 15 may, 1859 Adil Muhammad Khan, Amanat Khan and Sarfaraz Khan captured the fort of Pipria under Vidisha. \( ^{4} \)  On the 18th May, 1859, a British detachment accompanied the Subah of Isagarh from Sironj to Sanchi and attacked Adil Muhammad Khan.
C10-GAZ-042   p. 60
The British Government, therefore, came out with a proclamation guaranteeing the rights and privileges of the princes and assuring them against the annexation of any further princely territory. \( ^{2} \)  Thus, Vidisha District, \( ^{1} \)  which was divided among the Sindhia and the Nawab of Kurwai, Tonk, Pathari, Muhammadgarh and Basoda, did not face any change in its ruling houses.
C10-GAZ-043   p. 62
Asad Ali, the ruler of Basoda, died in the year 1864 A. D. He was succeeded by his eldest son, Amar Ali Khan.
C10-GAZ-044   p. 62
In April, 1929 the Sarvajanik Sabha, a social and cultural organisation, was established in Gwalior State, of which Takhtmal Jain of Vidisha was the Secretary for six years from 1930-31 to 1936-37.
In the same year an attempt was made to form a Praja Mandal at Vidisha and get it registered, but the Gwalior State Government turned it down.
C10-GAZ-045   p. 63
The Maharaja of Gwalior, accordingly, made a declaration on 23 January, 1948 granting responsible government to the State, and a cabinet of ministers was sworn in on the next day. A major part of the modern Vidisha District, being included in Gwalior States also shared the responsible government.
The rulers of Gwalior, Indore and other states of Malwa signed the covenant on 22 April, 1948, and thus the Madhya Bharat Union came into being on 28 May, 1948. The present Vidisha District, except Sironj sub-division, thus became part of Madhya Bharat.
C10-GAZ-046   p. 64
Consequent upon the formation of the New Madhya Pradesh as a result of the Reorganisation of States, on the 1st November, 1956 Bhopal became the capital. At this juncture Sironj and Lateri which were transferred from Rajasthan to Madhya a Pradesh became tahsils of Vidisha District.
C10-GAZ-047   p. 65
The development of irrigation in the former Gwalior State started with the accession of the princely ruler Madho Rao Scindia. There was a devastating famine in 1898 and again in 1900, and the Ruler decided to constitute a special Irrigation Department in the year 1905. He was keen to get the minor irrigation works constructed in all districts of the State. This policy was followed by the Irrigation Department of the State till 1914. The genesis of irrigation thus lay in the distress caused by famines.
C10-GAZ-048   p. 95
In the former Gwalior State, of which Bhilsa (renamed Vidisha) was a district, the judicial arrangements were revolutionised in 1853, when the power of the Ijardars were abolished and appointments of Kamasdars and Subahs in charge of parganas and zilas, respectively were made. These officials exercised judicial powers within their areas. The Chief Court called 'Sardar Nizamat' was established at Lashkar.
C10-GAZ-051   p. 220
Two Parganas, viz, Bhilsa and Basoda with a population of 54,293 and 65,896, respectively in 1901, were recorded. During the 70 years (1901–71) the population of the District almost has trebled itself, registering a total increase of about 189 per cent.
C10-GAZ-052   p. 67   also Ch 12
In 1931 Vidisha recorded a population of 345,818. A fall of 0.64 per cent or 2,229 persons over 1921 is a peculiar phenomenon to the District. This uncanny behaviour of population in Vidisha has been Decade of Peculiar described in 1931 Census as fictitious. Seasonal influx of field Phenomenon labourers at the harvest time in the District was largely responsible for an unusual increase during the Census of 1921.
C10-GAZ-053   p. 68
1899-1900 and the numerous empty houses to be seen in every village and referred to laconically as the results of Chappan ka sal (V. S. 1956) as well as a seriously diminished supply of agricultural labour, show clearly how serious were its effects."1
C10-GAZ-054   p. 119
However, the prosperity of Sironj area declined with the fall of the Mughal empire, which effected trade and commerce.
With the construction of a section of the Great Indian Peninsula Railways at the end of 19th century, transport facilities were made available at (Bhilsa) Vidisha, Sumer, Gulabganj, Pabai, Ganj Basoda and Kulhar stations in the District. With the absence of metalled roads in the District, the trade centres of the District shifted to railway stations and Vidisha and Ganj Basoda developed as main trade centres of the District.
C10-GAZ-055   p. 155
## Jagta Hua Kasba / कथा उदयपुर — English translation (JAGTA-EN, 11)
When the Paramaras' glory ended, Udaipur too became an orphan. In the centuries after the 11th-century occupation of Delhi by the Turks, followers of Islam, Udaipur too came into the grip of the far-reaching changes. New marks began to appear in the old constructions. The high-handedness of the centuries is clearly visible everywhere.
C10-JAGTA-EN-001   ≈ p. 12 (Hindi original)
Perhaps Udaipur must have been that last city where the people of the Paramara dynasty itself must have kept up the fight to the end, because at Dhar and Mandu they had been surrounded and killed.
After the death of the last Paramara king Mahlakdeva at the hands of a chieftain named Ainul Mulk Multani — who came at the behest of Alauddin Khilji — at Mandu, the bands of Turk-Muslim plunderers who had seized Delhi must have begun to hover up to the other prosperous towns and cities of his kingdom.
C10-JAGTA-EN-004   ≈ p. 25 (Hindi original)
In Devpaldev's very time, Shamsuddin Iltutmish had looted and badly devastated Vidisha and Ujjain. His plundering armies had devastated the whole of Malwa in a single long campaign, going far and wide. That was the first Islamic contact of this region.
C10-JAGTA-EN-005   ≈ p. 51 (Hindi original)
In the course of time, the splendour of the Paramaras came to an end at Mandu at the hands of an invader-band sent by another Islamic terrorist, Alauddin Khilji. That was Maharaj Mahlakdeva, who had clashed with the bloody and greedy storms of the Khiljis. We do not know how many of his clan were killed, how many survived, and where they then went.
C10-JAGTA-EN-006   ≈ p. 52 (Hindi original)   also Ch 4
That temple was first razed to the ground in the attack of Shamsuddin Iltutmish. The contemporary historian Minhaj Siraj wrote the height of this temple of Bhilsa as 105 *gaz* (yards). If you estimate the height of the temple that stood upon the vast foundation of the Vijay Mandir, the height must have been exactly this much.
C10-JAGTA-EN-007   ≈ p. 59 (Hindi original)
This was a 105-*gaz*-high temple. It was visible from very far. Delhi's sultan Shamsuddin Iltutmish has annihilated it. They have looted the city and set off toward Ujjain. There they will do the same to the Mahakal temple. For the first time, the Islamic armies
have knocked here for the first time." Saying this, Minhaj vanished.
C10-JAGTA-EN-008   ≈ p. 61 (Hindi original)
Dilawar Khan Ghori became the first independent sultan of Malwa. This is an event of a hundred-and-a-quarter years before Babur came to India.
C10-JAGTA-EN-009   ≈ p. 66 (Hindi original)
In this region are, in sequence, the attacks, loot and occupations of Shamsuddin Iltutmish, Alauddin Khilji, Mohammad Tughlaq, Sher Shah Suri. After the Mughals, there are the ambushes of new occupiers by the name of nawabs. Babur's attack on Chanderi took place in 1528, and by then Udaipur — silently watching India's devastation from afar — became a part of the Chanderi suba.
C10-JAGTA-EN-010   ≈ p. 71 (Hindi original)
It was looted and ruined at the hands of the merciless Turk plunderers who seized Delhi, just a hundred years after king Narvarman. That was the notorious invader Iltutmish, in whose fierce attacks on Malwa it was broken. From the rubble of that vast shrine a mosque was made.
C10-JAGTA-EN-011   ≈ p. 78 (Hindi original)
You can imagine, seeing those broken stones of Bija Mandal in Vidisha, that this must have been one of the largest temples of the country in its time. Its height — 105 *gaz* — Minhaj Siraj wrote in *Tabaqat-e-Nasiri*, he being the only news-writer of Delhi to record the devastation of Iltutmish's attack.
C10-JAGTA-EN-012   ≈ p. 105 (Hindi original)

C10-JAGTA-EN-013   ≈ p. 105 (Hindi original)
## History of the Paramāra Dynasty — Ganguly, D. C. (GANGULY, 6)
The Moslems then began to persecute the peaceful Hindus. Devastation and plunder followed in their train. Temples were razed to the ground and idols overthrown and broken. Malwa and the countries beyond it became the resort of those who wished to save themselves from the tyranny of the plunderers.
C10-GANGULY-001   p. 207
In 1233-4, Iyal-Timish reduced Gwalior to subjection, and turned his arms against Malwa. Devapâla was not prepared for this onslaught and the Sultân easily captured the fort of Bhilsa and made a triumphant march into the city of Ujjain. Firishta tells us that, on this occasion, the Moslem chief “destroyed a magnificent temple, dedicated to Mahâkâl, formed upon the same plan as that of Somnat.
C10-GANGULY-002   p. 228
The Paramāra government thus received another rude shock, which no doubt, accelerated its decline and downfall.
The storm of the Moslem invasion passed away as quickly as it came, leaving desolation in its wake. The upheaval was tremendous, but Devapâla succeeded in restoring peace and order.
C10-GANGULY-003   p. 229
The Paramâra territory probably again fell a prey to the inroads of the Moslems. The Tabaqât-i-Nâsirî \( ^{1} \) tells us that “the sublime standard (of Balban) moved towards Gwâliyûr, Chandîrî, Nurwul (Nurwur) and Mâlwah, and on this expedition, they reached near unto Mâlwah.” Firishta records \( ^{2} \) that Nâsir-ud-dîn marched to Chundery and Malwa and subdued them. He, after having appointed an officer to govern them, returned to Delhi. It is extremely doubtful whether the Moslems were, on that occasion, able to establish a permanent authority over any part of Malwa, but it is quite certain that this raid played a large part in lessening still further the declining strength of the Paramâras.
C10-GANGULY-004   p. 231
D., Sultân ‘Alâ-ud-dîn Khiljī appeared, at the head of his picked troops, before the gates of Malwa. Mahlak Deo, together with his general Kokâ (Gogâ), collected all the forces he could muster, amounting in all to thirty or forty thousand cavalry and innumerable infantry, and confronted the Moslems in a last but by no means less formidable effort to stem the progress of the invaders. But Providence had decreed that the Hindus should no more hold the sovereignty of India. Hundreds of the Malwa army were slain on the battlefield, and Kokâ lost his life in the encounter. Mahlak Deo fled to the fort of Mandu, and Malwa was conquered and finally annexed to the Moslem empire, ‘Ain-ul-Mulk being appointed to carry on its administration as governor.
C10-GANGULY-005   p. 242
In 1145 A. D., Malwa was conquered by the Caulukyas of Gujarat, and was ruled by them till 1174 A. D. With the decline of the power of the house at Dhârâ, about this time, the Vâgada branch seems to have lost its political supremacy. Taking advantage of its helplessness, the Guhilas of Mewar invaded the country and annexed it to their own kingdom.
C10-GANGULY-008   p. 352
## The Cultural Heritage of Madhya Bharat — Patil, D. R. (MADHYABHARAT, 8)
Emboldened by this success Itutmish marched against Malwa and first captured the fort of Bhlsa before he proceeded on to Ujjain which easily fell into his hands.
C10-MADHYABHARAT-001   p. 37
The ravages of Shamsu-ud-din Ilutumish in Malwa and his destruction of the old cities of Bhilsa and Ujjain had only a temporary effect on the ruling power which was, however, finally smashed in the fort of Mandu itself by the general Ain-ul-Mulk specially commissioned by the Sultan Alauddin Khilji of Delhi, as it is often said, "to cleanse that old Gabristan from the stench of infidelity". This event finally and formally brought an end to the Paramara rule of Malwa under Milhakdeva in 1305 A. D. whence it became a province of the Delhi empire.
C10-MADHYABHARAT-002   p. 38   also Ch 4
But, as soon as Humayun left Malwa, Mallu Khan, an officer of the former Khilji dynasty, retook all the territory between the Narbada and Bhilsa and crowned himself at Mandu with the title of Qadir Shah in 1536 A. D.
C10-MADHYABHARAT-003   p. 41
After the fall of Baz Bahadur, Malwa ceased to exist as an independent kingdom. It formed an important province of the Mughal Empire in charge of a governor.
C10-MADHYABHARAT-004   p. 41
Under the Sultans of Malwa, Bhilsa was one of the provincial capitals of the kingdom in charge of a governor.
Under the Mughals, however, Bhilsa did not enjoy much political importance it being converted into a head-quarter of a small administrative unit. The emperor Aurangzeb renamed the town as Alamgirpur after his own name though it never came into general use. In the 18th century Bhilsa passed into the hands of the Marathas and remained with them since then.
C10-MADHYABHARAT-005   p. 111
The Maratha incursions into Malwa continued unabated and were more intensified during the years 1728 to 1740 A. D. In 1728 the Mughal Governor, Girdhar Bahadur, was routed and killed in battle near the fort of Mandu by Chimnaji, the younger brother of the Peshwa.
C10-MADHYABHARAT-007   p. 42
In all these campaigns the Maratha chiefs, Ranoji Scindia and Malharrao Holkar, had specially distinguished themselves as leaders of great military and political talents which eventually enabled them to carve out principalities for themselves covering almost the whole of the territory of Madhya Bharat.
In 1740 Bajirao died succeeded by his son Balaji as Peshwa who through the agency of the Nizam secured from the Mughal emperor a sort of formal or legal sanction for the otherwise de facto occupation of the Malwa territory by the Marathas through rather an interesting device of getting himself ceremoniously appointed as the emperor's humble Deputy Governor of Malwa. The Maratha chiefs acted as the agents of the Peshwa in the north where they wielded considerable influence in political and military matters. They were besides assigned certain territories within this realm for the maintenance of their troops and for the smooth performance of their duties. The Maratha States of Gwalior, Indore, Dhar and Dewas which covered the whole of Madhya Bharat owe their origin and history to this system.
C10-MADHYABHARAT-008   p. 43
In 1818 the power of the Peshwas was finally brought to an end by the British who had by now already started interfering with the political affairs of the Scindias and the Holkars.
C10-MADHYABHARAT-009   p. 44
## Some Paramāra Temples From Madhya Pradesh: A Case Study of Village Un — Swati Mondal Adhikari (PARTEMPLES, 3)
Meanwhile the army of Ilutmish, the Delhi Sultan, was advancing towards Málava. Leading the victorious march of the Sultani army, Ilutmish penetrated in Málava, proceeded to Ujjayinī, destroyed the celebrated temple of Mahākāla and also appointed a governor at Vidisha in 1233 A.D. Both of Vidisha inscription and Mandhata inscription respectively of 1263 A.D. and 1274 A.D. inform that Devapala recaptured Vidisha and killed the adhipa of the Mlechhas' there.
C10-PARTEMPLES-002   p. 12
Besides the internal dissension, Muslim invasion took place in Mālava. Ein-ool-Moolk, the general of Allauddin Khalji proved victorious and overran the cities of Ujjayinī, Mandu, Dhār and Chanderi in 1305 A.D.
C10-PARTEMPLES-003   p. 12
The growing weakness and the waves of Muslim invasion gradually led to the Paramāra dynasty to its impending downfall. With the decay of the Paramāra dynasty, their patronage of arts, religion and literature decreased. Temple building activity suffered in consequence which is evident from the construction of some small insignificant temples evincing a degenerated style in Mālava-Nimar area. As days gone by this feeble tradition of temple building also came to an end.

C10-CII-003   p. 285
We know that in 1233-34 A.C. Ilutumish, the Turkish Sultān of Delhi, had turned his arms against Mālwā, where he captured the fort of Bhilsā (modern Vidishā) and from there he marched to Ujjain where he destroyed the temple of Mahākāla. \( ^{2} \)  These aggressive operations, though of a temporary nature, \( ^{3} \)  had opened a way for the Muslims to make expeditions further in the south; and during the years that followed, Nāsiruddīn's general Balban successfully over-ran the land up to Chanderī and Narwar which were then on the northern fringe of the Paramāra dominions. It was about in 1250 A.C.; \( ^{4} \)  and almost at the same time, that this region was attacked from the south by the Yādava Krishna, whose enmity with the house of the Paramāras was hereditary and who claims success over the contemporary king of Mālava in his epigraphical records. \( ^{5} \)  Almost about the same time the vāghelā Visaladēva of Gujarāt sacked Dhārā. \( ^{6} \)  And it is possible to hold that in view of all these troubles Jayavarman or his predecessor Jaitugi may have thought it best to resort to the fort of Māndū, which is a place of better strategic position than Dhārā, situated on the plains.
C10-CII-004   p. 308   also Ch 4
The stone bearing the inscription is now preserved in the Archaeological Museum at Bhilsā, now known as Vidishā, the principal town of a district of the same name in Madhya Pradesh. It is a detached slab, and its find-spot and the circumstances under which it was obtained are not known, though from the mention of Bhillasvāmidēvapura in it, which is Bhilsā itself, it could not have been brought from far and may have been originally set in or in the vicinity of the temple which was broken by the Sultān Ilutmish in his conquest of that place.
C10-CII-005   p. 315   also Ch 3

C10-CII-006   p. 316
He is said to have vanquished his enemies and firmly established dharmu; but the information furnished by the last of these verses is historically important, telling us that he “by his sword, cut off into two (the body of) the arrogant lord of the Mlechchhas, in a battle fought near Bhillasvāmipura,” which is Bhilsā, now known as Vidishā. This statement appears to have an allusion to the invasion of the city of Bhilsā by Iltutmish, the Turkish Sultān of Delhi. We know from the Muslim historians that this ruler subjugated the fort of Gwālior, in 632 A.H., or 1233-34 A.C., and soon after, advancing further in the south, he demolished the temple of Bhillasvāmin (at Bhilsā). \( ^{4} \)  But in view of the statement of the inscription under study, it would appear that Dēvapāla succeeded in recapturing the fort at the city, which remained in the possession of the Paramāras about half a century, till it was again snatched from them by the Khalji Sultāns of Delhi.
C10-CII-007   p. 324   also Ch 4
\( ^{3} \)  From the Ujjain stone inscription of the Chaulukya Jayasimha of V.S. 1195 or 1138 A.C., we know that this king (Jayasimha) claims to be Avanti-nātha, which shows that he had conquered and annexed the whole of the Paramāra kingdom to the Chaulukya empire. \( ^{3} \)  This incident took place about fifteen years earlier than the time when the image bearing the present inscription was set up; and during the reign of Jayasimha's successor Kumārapāla (c. 1143-1172 A.C.) there was practically no change in the situation. From Jima-mandana's Kumārapālaprabandha we know that Yasōdhavala obtained the throne of Chandrā-vatī through the favour of Kumārapāla, \( ^{4} \)  to whom he remained faithful, and as such, he killed Ballāla of unknown lineage who had, during the period of confusion that followed Jayasimha's capture of Mālwā, usurped some parts thereof, proclaiming himself as the king of that region.
C10-CII-008   p. 365   also Ch 4

## The Temple Architecture of India — Adam Hardy (HARDY, 3)
Broadly, it covers the whole of our core period, ending, in the north, with the establishment of the Delhi Sultanate in 1206, and through most of the remainder of India with Muslim conquests over the ensuing two centuries. This does not leave much late medieval before the beginnings of early modern; and it should be noted that Islamic rulers initially adopted feudal structures similar to those of their predecessors. For our purposes, they put an end to the core period by building mosques instead of temples. The great age of medieval temples can thus be defined as roughly from AD 600 to 1300.
C10-HARDY-001   p. 29
Given the inextricable connection between a monarch and his royal temple, the inverted funnel through which power flowed down to him, it is not surprising that temple desecration became an act with which to set the seal on a conquest. Often this meant carrying home the principal idol, or it could be a matter of destroying the main shrine or even the entire temple. Later temple destruction by Muslims was largely a continuation of this tradition, rather than iconoclasm.
C10-HARDY-002   p. 34
The light shed, from the 1950s onwards, by Indian Marxist-inspired historians applying the model of feudalism to medieval India, was more penetrating. According to their analysis, the contraction of long-distance trade led to the decline of cities, of artisanal production, of a money economy and centralised taxation. Kings had to resort to land grants to support religious foundations and to reward secular officers. State power was fragmented as the class of landed intermediaries grew, and through sub-infeudation the process of fragmentation continued lower down the scale.
C10-HARDY-003   p. 30

## Jagta Hua Kasba / कथा उदयपुर — English translation (JAGTA-EN, 4)
Alexander Cunningham, while in the British Army, had, single-handedly, done so much work on the unknown history and archaeology of India lost in the ruins, as the Archaeological Survey of India (ASI) and the state governments' archaeology departments have not done together in 73 years.
C11-JAGTA-EN-001   ≈ p. 36 (Hindi original)
Staying for months everywhere, he probed Sarnath, Mathura, Sanchi, Vidisha, Bharhut. Writing letters to his fellow officers, he called for the details connected with the heritage being ruined in different places. At Sanchi, in two boxes, he discovered the relics of two close disciples of Gautam Buddha, and found more than two hundred short inscriptions. In that resource-less time, on the basis of this priceless discovery, he wrote a book — *The Bhilsa Topes*. Going to Vidisha, he saw Heliodorus's pillar.
C11-JAGTA-EN-002   ≈ p. 83 (Hindi original)
Only then, in 1861, was the Archaeological Survey of India (ASI) formed. In the very name of this new department, "Survey" came joined, which has remained joined ever since — from after independence to now. Cunningham himself was told — since you have shown the way, now go on ahead. He was made the first Director General of the ASI.
C11-JAGTA-EN-007   ≈ p. 84 (Hindi original)
Mishra had told me that every such piece of news of the theft of images, along with photos of the stolen image, immediately goes from the spot police station up to Interpol; and then there is every possibility that at some time or other that image will be caught. Recently, about two hundred and fifty priceless ancient images had been called back to India. Despite the crackdown, thefts are going on throughout the country.

In 1914, the Archaeology Department of the erstwhile State of a Gwalior conducted excavations in the ancient city of Vidisha. These statues were also placed in the compound of the Dak Bungalow. Again during the years 1963-65, excavations were carried on and the articles and statues thus unearthed were either transferred to Gujar Mahal Museum, Gwalior, or to the Dak Bungalow of the town.

## The Temple Architecture of India — Adam Hardy (HARDY, 1)
MA Dhaky and Krishna Deva have led the analysis of canonical texts and their correlation with actual temples from their respective regional traditions, bringing to light much indigenous architectural terminology (used here, but sparingly and without diacritical marks, for the general reader's sake). Dhaky has been the mainstay of the monumental project undertaken by the American Institute of Indian Studies, brought together in its Encyclopaedia of Indian Temple Architecture. This, together with the institute's photographic archive (much of it at last online \( ^{16} \) ), illustrates hundreds of sites, many previously undocumented. The Encyclopaedia includes authoritative dating of monuments, which I largely follow here.

What was there before and what happened later should be told to them in full detail with documentary evidence.
C13-JAGTA-HI-013   p. 104
वर्तमान मध्यप्रदेश में 30 से ज्यादा राजे-रजवाड़े रहे होंगे, जो अंग्रेजी हुकूमत के समकालीन हैं। इनकी ज्यादातर विरासत खंडहर में बदल रही है। उसका रखरखाव सही नहीं है। राजाओं के उत्तराधिकारी दूसरे स्थानों पर चले गए हैं। कई जगह वे बचे ही नहीं हैं। ऐसे में इतिहास के अनछुए अध्यायों को विस्तार से जानने के लिए एक सामाजिक अभियान की जरूरत है और इसका सुफल स्थानीय समाज की सक्रिय सहभागिता के बिना संभव नहीं है।
*Gloss [my translation]:* In present-day Madhya Pradesh there must have been more than 30 kings and princely states contemporary with British rule. Most of their heritage is turning into ruins. Its upkeep is not proper. The successors of the kings have moved to other places. In many places they do not survive. In such a case, to know the untouched chapters of history in detail a social campaign is needed, and its fruit is not possible without the active participation of the local society.
C13-JAGTA-HI-016   p. 153   also Ch 11
इसके लिए स्थानीय इतिहास और पुरातत्व पर उत्तम श्रेणी के लघु और विस्तृत प्रकाशन किए जाएं, जिन्हें स्कूल-कॉलेज में ही रखा जाए। शॉर्ट फिल्में बनाई जाएं और डिजिटल वर्ल्ड में इन्हें प्रसारित किया जाए। बच्चों के बीच लेखन और भाषण की प्रतियोगिताओं में हमारी विरासत एक केंद्रीय विषय की तरह अनिवार्य किया जाए। इससे वे अपने इतिहास को पढ़ने, जाकर देखने के लिए उत्सुक होंगे।
*Gloss [my translation]:* For this, high-quality short and detailed publications on local history and archaeology should be made and kept in schools and colleges themselves. Short films should be made and broadcast in the digital world. Our heritage should be made a compulsory central theme in writing and speech competitions among children. Through this they will be eager to read about their history and go and see it.
C13-JAGTA-HI-017   p. 159   also Ch 12
यह एक हफ्ते में होने वाला काम है। बस इस काम में स्थानीय स्वयंसेवी संस्थाओं को प्रेरित करने की जरूरत है। थोड़े से प्रशिक्षण से ही वे सब कुछ सूचीबद्ध कर पाएंगे।
*Gloss [my translation]:* It is a job that can be done in a week. Only local voluntary organisations need to be motivated for this work. With a little training they will be able to list everything.
C13-JAGTA-HI-026   p. 153
उपाध्याय ध्यान दिलाते हैं कि मध्यप्रदेश में शिलालेखों को पढ़ने वाले विशेषज्ञों का अकाल है। सरकारी विभागों और शिक्षण संस्थानों में भी लोग नहीं हैं, जो नए शिलालेख पढ़ सकें। उन्हें काला अक्षर बैस बराबर है। किस पत्थर पर क्या लिखा है, वे नहीं बता पाएंगे।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Upadhyay points out that there is a famine of experts who can read inscriptions in Madhya Pradesh. Even in government departments and educational institutions there are no people who can read new inscriptions. For them it is as good as black letters. They will not be able to tell what is written on which stone.
C13-JAGTA-HI-027   p. 153   also Ch 6, Ch 11
पंचायतें भी कुछ नहीं करतीं। जबकि इन्हें सहजकर नई पीढ़ी के लिए अपने पूर्वों से परिचय कराने का कितना उम्दा और कमाऊ जरिया यह हो सकता है। इनमें हमारे अतीत की कहानियां हैं। भारत का भोगा हुआ इतना कुछ है कि हर खंडहर और हर टीले की शिनाख्त हमें पीछे की कुछ सच्चाइयों से ही रूबरू कराएगी।
*Gloss [my translation]:* The panchayats also do nothing. Whereas, preserving these, what a fine and income-yielding means this could be to introduce the new generation to their ancestors. Our past's stories are in them. India has experienced so much that the identification of every ruin and every mound will confront us with some truths of the past.
C13-JAGTA-HI-028   p. 14
हर रविवार की एक घोषित यादगार हेरिटेज वॉक में सैलानियों को लुभाने के लिए कुछ छपी हुई सामग्री भी तैयार हो सकती थी। इस बेहद सस्ते और कम बजट के काम में केंद्र, राज्य सरकार, जिला पुरातत्व संघ या स्थानीय पंचायत ही कुछ हाथ बढ़ा देते।
*Gloss [my translation]:* Printed material could also have been prepared to attract tourists on a declared memorable heritage walk every Sunday. In this very cheap and low-budget work the Centre, the state government, the District Archaeology Association or the local panchayat could have lent a hand.
C13-JAGTA-HI-029   p. 104
